\part{Relate}

\chapter{Regression I}\label{regression-i}

Measures of association are essential tools in business statistics for
analyzing relationships between variables. They help determine whether
changes in one variable are linked to changes in another, providing
valuable insights into patterns and dependencies. Understanding these
relationships is critical for making informed decisions. By quantifying
the strength and direction of associations, businesses can better
interpret data, optimize strategies, and drive meaningful outcomes.
Below we study important measures of association. Association still
belongs to the \emph{describe} stage of learning from data --- here we
characterize how two variables move together --- but it also points
ahead to inference, where we will ask whether a relationship seen in a
sample is real or merely a product of chance.

\section{The Covariance}\label{the-covariance}

The \textbf{covariance} is a measure that determines the direction of
the relationship between two variables. It is calculated by:

\begin{bsformula}[THE COVARIANCE]

\[s_{xy}=\frac{\sum(x_i-\bar{x})(y_i-\bar{y})}{n-1}\] where \(x_i\) and
\(y_i\) are observations, \(\bar{x}\) and \(\bar{y}\) are the sample
means, and \(n\) is the sample size.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
\(s_{xy} > 0\) & \(s_{xy} = 0\) & \(s_{xy} < 0\) \\
\midrule
Direct relationship & No relationship & Inverse relationship \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's consider the following data that captures the price of stocks
(SPY) and bonds (BND):

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
Period & SPY & BND \\
\midrule
1 & 87 & 316 \\
2 & 86 & 380 \\
3 & 84 & 416 \\
4 & 85 & 430 \\
5 & 83 & 440 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The idea behind calculating a covariance is to determine whether there
is a relationship between the two variables. We start by finding the
deviations from the mean for both SPY and BND:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccccc}
\toprule
Period & SPY & BND & \(SPY - \bar{SPY}\) & \(BND - \bar{BND}\) & Product \\
\midrule
1 & 87 & 316 & \(2\) & \(-80\) & \(-160\) \\
2 & 86 & 380 & \(1\) & \(-16\) & \(-16\) \\
3 & 84 & 416 & \(-1\) & \(20\) & \(-20\) \\
4 & 85 & 430 & \(0\) & \(34\) & \(0\) \\
5 & 83 & 440 & \(-2\) & \(44\) & \(-88\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The covariance is the average of the products column using \(n-1\):

\[s_{xy} = \frac{-160 + (-16) + (-20) + 0 + (-88)}{5-1} =
\frac{-284}{4} = -71\]

Since \(s_{xy} < 0\) there is an inverse relationship between SPY and
BND. Intuitively, the covariance checks whether on average the products
of the deviations from the mean is positive or negative. Whenever \(x\)
lies below its mean and \(y\) lies above its mean, the product is
negative --- and vice versa --- reflecting the inverse relationship
between the two variables.

\end{bsexample}

\section{The Correlation}\label{the-correlation}

The \textbf{correlation} measures the strength of the linear
relationship between two variables. It is calculated by:

\begin{bsformula}[THE CORRELATION COEFFICIENT]

\[r = \frac{s_{xy}}{s_x \cdot s_y}\] where \(s_{xy}\) is the covariance,
\(s_x\) is the standard deviation of \(x\), and \(s_y\) is the standard
deviation of \(y\).

The correlation coefficient lies between \([-1, 1]\):

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
\toprule
\(r = -1\) & \(-1 < r < 0\) & \(r = 0\) & \(0 < r < 1\) & \(r = 1\) \\
\midrule
Perfect inverse & Inverse & No relationship & Direct & Perfect direct \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\end{bsformula}

The strength of the relationship is determined by the absolute value of
\(r\). Values close to \(1\) indicate a strong relationship, values
close to \(0\) indicate a weak relationship.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider the SPY and BND data. The standard deviation of SPY is
\(s_{SPY} = 1.58\) and that of BND is \(s_{BND} = 50.37\). Substituting
into the correlation formula:

\[r = \frac{-71}{1.58 \times 50.37} = -0.89\]

This indicates that the relationship between SPY and BND is inverse and
strong.

\end{bsexample}

\section{\texorpdfstring{The Coefficient of Determination
(\(R^2\))}{The Coefficient of Determination (R\^{}2)}}\label{the-coefficient-of-determination-r2}

The \textbf{coefficient of determination} or \(R^2\) measures the
percent of variation in \(y\) explained by variations in \(x\). It is
calculated by:

\begin{bsformula}[THE COEFFICIENT OF DETERMINATION]

\[R^2 = r^2\] where \(r\) is the correlation coefficient. \(R^2\) ranges
from \(0\) (no relationship) to \(1\) (perfect relationship).

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider once more the SPY and BND example. The correlation coefficient
is \(r = -0.89\). Hence:

\[R^2 = (-0.89)^2 = 0.79\]

This indicates that about \(79\%\) of the variation in BND can be
explained by changes in SPY. The two variables are closely related.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Measures of Association in
Excel}\label{measures-of-association-in-excel}

Excel provides straightforward functions to calculate all three measures
of association. Let's use the SPY and BND data from the example above.

\bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/spybnd.xlsx}{Download Data}

\textbf{Covariance:} To calculate the sample covariance use
\texttt{=COVARIANCE.S()}. Assuming SPY is in column A and BND is in
column B:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=COVARIANCE.S(A2:A6, B2:B6)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Correlation:} To calculate the correlation coefficient use
\texttt{=CORREL()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=CORREL(A2:A6, B2:B6)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Coefficient of Determination:} There is no direct Excel function
for \(R^2\) between two variables. Simply square the correlation
coefficient:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=CORREL(A2:A6, B2:B6)\^{}2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The results for the SPY and BND data are:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
Covariance & Correlation & \(R^2\) \\
\midrule
\(-71.00\) & \(-0.8916\) & \(0.79\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{Scatter Plot:} To create a scatter plot in Excel, select both
columns and go to \texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Scatter}. This will
display the relationship between SPY and BND visually. To add a trend
line, right-click any point in the scatter plot and select
\texttt{Add\ Trendline\ →\ Linear}.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-3}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S(array1,\ array2)} calculates the sample
  covariance between two variables. Use \texttt{=COVARIANCE.P()} for the
  population covariance.
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(array1,\ array2)} calculates the correlation
  coefficient between two variables. The result is always between \(-1\)
  and \(1\).
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(array1,\ array2)\^{}2} calculates the coefficient of
  determination \(R^2\) by squaring the correlation coefficient. Excel
  does not have a standalone \(R^2\) function for two variables.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Association.png}
\end{center}

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-5}

The following exercises will help you understand statistical measures
that establish the relationship between two variables. In particular,
the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculating covariance and correlation.
\item
  Creating scatter diagrams in Excel.
\item
  Calculating the coefficient of determination.
\item
  Interpreting measures of association in a real-world context.
\end{itemize}

Answers are in the \hyperref[answers]{Answers to Exercises} at the end
of the book. Try not to peek until you have formulated your own answer
and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\chapter{Regression II}\label{regression-ii}

Quantifying relationships between variables is an important skill in
business analytics. The regression line, representing the best linear
fit between two variables, enables businesses to make predictions,
identify trends, and optimize strategies using data-driven insights.
Understanding how to calculate and interpret a regression line provides
the foundation for analyzing business relationships, such as the effect
of advertising on sales or customer satisfaction on revenue. Below, we
delve into generating and analyzing a regression line. Fitting a line is
a descriptive act --- a compact summary of how the data behave --- but
it is also the model whose reliability we will later test in the
inference chapters, once we ask how much a different sample might have
shifted it.

\section{The Regression Line}\label{the-regression-line}

The regression line is calculated to minimize the average distance (or
errors) between the line and the observed data points. It is defined by
two key components: a \textbf{slope} (\(\hat{\beta}\)) and an
\textbf{intercept} (\(\hat{\alpha}\)). Mathematically, the regression
line is expressed as:

\begin{bsformula}[THE REGRESSION LINE]

\[\hat{y_i}=\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta}x_i\] where \(\hat{y_i}\) are the
predicted values of \(y\) given \(x_i\), \(\hat{\alpha}\) is the
intercept, and \(\hat{\beta}\) is the slope.

\end{bsformula}

The \textbf{slope} determines the steepness of the line and quantifies
how much a unit increase in \(x\) changes \(y\) on average. It is
calculated using the covariance between \(y\) and \(x\) and the variance
of \(x\):

\begin{bsformula}[THE SLOPE]

\[\hat{\beta}= \frac{s_{xy}}{s_{x}^2}\] where \(s_{xy}\) is the sample
covariance between \(x\) and \(y\), and \(s_x^2\) is the sample variance
of \(x\).

\end{bsformula}

The \textbf{intercept} determines where the line crosses the \(y\) axis
--- the predicted value of \(y\) when \(x\) is zero. Once the slope is
estimated, the intercept is calculated by:

\begin{bsformula}[THE INTERCEPT]

\[\hat{\alpha}=\bar{y}-\hat{\beta}\bar{x}\] where \(\bar{y}\) is the
mean of \(y\) and \(\bar{x}\) is the mean of \(x\).

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's examine a data set on Price and Advertisement. In general, one
expects that when a company advertises, it can convince consumers to pay
more for their product. Below is the data:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cc}
\toprule
Advertisement (x) & Price (y) \\
\midrule
2 & 7 \\
1 & 3 \\
3 & 8 \\
4 & 10 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The data shows a clear direct relationship between advertisement and
price. Regression allows us to answer two key questions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Effectiveness:} How much can we increase the price for every
  additional dollar spent on advertisement?
\item
  \textbf{Prediction:} What is the predicted price if we have a budget
  of \(6\) for advertisement?
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Step 1 --- Calculate the slope:} Given that the covariance is
\(s_{xy}=3.67\) and the variance of \(x\) is \(s_x^2=1.67\):

\[\hat{\beta}= \frac{3.67}{1.67}=2.2\]

For every additional dollar spent on advertisement, price increases by
\(2.2\) on average.

\textbf{Step 2 --- Calculate the intercept:} Since \(\bar{x}=2.5\) and
\(\bar{y}=7\):

\[\hat{\alpha}=7-2.2(2.5)=1.5\]

If we do not advertise, the predicted price is \(1.5\).

\textbf{Step 3 --- Write the regression line:}

\[\hat{y_i}=1.5+2.2x_i\]

\textbf{Step 4 --- Make a prediction:} With an advertisement budget of
\(6\):

\[\hat{y_i}=1.5+2.2(6)=14.7\]

In conclusion, for every dollar spent on advertisement the price
increases by \(2.2\), and with a budget of \(6\) the predicted price is
\(14.7\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Measures of Goodness of Fit}\label{measures-of-goodness-of-fit}

When analyzing the effectiveness of a regression model, it is crucial to
assess how well the model fits the data. Below we revisit the
coefficient of determination \(R^2\) and introduce additional measures.

\subsection*{Coefficient of
Determination}\label{coefficient-of-determination}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Coefficient of Determination}

The \textbf{coefficient of determination} or \(R^2\) is the percent of
the variation in \(y\) that is explained by changes in \(x\). The higher
the \(R^2\) the better the explanatory power of the model.

\begin{bsformula}[THE COEFFICIENT OF DETERMINATION]

\[R^2=\frac{SSR}{SST}\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(SST = \sum(y_i-\bar{y})^2\) --- total variation in \(y\)
\item
  \(SSR = \sum(\hat{y_i}-\bar{y})^2\) --- variation explained by the
  model
\item
  \(SSE = \sum(y_i-\hat{y_i})^2\) --- variation unexplained by the model
\item
  \(SST = SSR + SSE\)
\end{itemize}

\(R^2\) is always between \([0,1]\).

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider data on the Weight (\(y\)) and Exercise (\(x\)) of a particular
person:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cc}
\toprule
Weight (y) & Exercise (x) \\
\midrule
165 & 45 \\
170 & 10 \\
168 & 25 \\
164 & 30 \\
165 & 40 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

If we used only the mean \(\bar{y}=166.4\) as our prediction, the total
squared error would be:

\[SST=(-1.4)^2+(3.6)^2+(1.6)^2+(-2.4)^2+(-1.4)^2=25.2\]

Using the regression line reduces these errors. The mistakes made by the
regression line are quantified by:

\[SSE=0.81+0.29+0.69+5.76+0.02=7.57\]

The improvement gained by using regression over the mean is:

\[SSR=5.29+9.4+0.59+0+2.35=17.63\]

Hence the coefficient of determination is:

\[R^2=\frac{SSR}{SST}=\frac{17.63}{25.2}\approx0.70\]

The regression line explains about \(70\%\) of the variation in Weight
using Exercise.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Multiple Regression}\label{multiple-regression}

If we were to predict weight we could use several other variables to get
a better prediction. \textbf{Multiple regression} is a technique used to
predict a variable using more than one independent variable.

\begin{bsformula}[THE MULTIPLE REGRESSION LINE]

\[\hat{y_i}=\hat{\beta_0}+\hat{\beta_1}x_1+\hat{\beta_2}x_2+...+\hat{\beta_k}x_k\]
where \(k\) is the total number of independent variables included in the
model.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's consider an additional variable in our Weight and Exercise
example. The table below includes information on Calories (\(z\)):

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
Weight (y) & Exercise (x) & Calories (z) \\
\midrule
165 & 45 & 1200 \\
170 & 10 & 1260 \\
168 & 25 & 1220 \\
164 & 30 & 1180 \\
165 & 40 & 1190 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The multiple regression line estimated by computer software is:

\[\hat{y_i}=83.96-0.025x+0.069z\]

Two conclusions follow from this result. First, as exercise increases
weight tends to go down, while more calories increases weight. Second,
reducing calorie consumption by \(1\) unit decreases weight by \(0.069\)
pounds, whereas one additional minute of exercise only reduces weight by
\(0.025\) pounds --- making calorie reduction more effective per unit.

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Anova}\label{anova}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Anova}

The Anova table helps us understand how well each variable in our
regression model explains the dependent variable. It decomposes the
\(SSR\) by variable and tracks the remaining errors.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

For the Weight, Exercise, and Calories regression, the Anova table is:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cc}
\toprule
Source & Sum Squares \\
\midrule
x & 17.63 \\
z & 6.55 \\
Residuals & 1.01 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Exercise explains \(17.63\) of the total variation. Adding Calories
reduces the remaining unexplained variation by another \(6.55\),
bringing the total \(SSR\) to \(24.18\) and the \(SSE\) down to
\(1.01\). The \(R^2\) increases from \(0.70\) to:

\[R^2=\frac{17.63+6.55}{25.2}=0.96\]

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{Adjusted
\(R^2\)}{Adjusted R\^{}2}}\label{adjusted-r2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Adjusted \(R^2\)}

The \textbf{adjusted} \(R^2\) penalizes a model for including additional
explanatory variables, preventing artificial inflation of \(R^2\).

\begin{bsformula}[THE ADJUSTED]

\(R^2\) \[\bar{R}^2=1-(1-R^2)\frac{n-1}{n-k-1}\] where \(k\) is the
number of explanatory variables and \(n\) is the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

For the Weight example with both Exercise and Calories (\(k=2\),
\(n=5\), \(R^2=0.96\)):

\[\bar{R}^2=1-(1-0.96)\frac{5-1}{5-2-1}=0.92\]

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Residual Standard Error}\label{residual-standard-error}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Residual Standard Error}

The \textbf{Residual Standard Error} estimates the average dispersion of
the data points around the regression line.

\begin{bsformula}[THE RESIDUAL STANDARD ERROR]

\[s_e=\sqrt{\frac{SSE}{n-k-1}}\] where \(k\) is the number of
independent variables and \(n\) is the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

For the Weight and Exercise model (\(k=1\)):

\[s_e=\sqrt{\frac{7.57}{5-1-1}}=1.59\]

Adding Calories (\(k=2\)) reduces this to:

\[s_e=\sqrt{\frac{1.01}{5-2-1}}=0.71\]

The smaller residual standard error confirms that adding Calories
improves the model's fit.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Regression with Dummy
Variables}\label{regression-with-dummy-variables}

So far every predictor has been numerical. But many of the variables we
care about in business are \textbf{categorical} --- the brand of a
product, whether a customer churned, the gender of a respondent, or the
instructor teaching a class. Regression can use these variables too,
once we encode them as numbers. The tool for doing so is the
\textbf{dummy variable}.

\begin{bsformula}[THE DUMMY VARIABLE]

\[d_i = \begin{cases} 1 & \text{if observation } i \text{ is in the category} \\ 0 & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}\]
A dummy (or \emph{indicator}) variable equals \(1\) when an observation
belongs to a particular category and \(0\) when it does not.

\end{bsformula}

\textbf{A single dummy recovers a difference of means.} Suppose we
regress a numerical variable \(y\) on a single dummy \(d\), giving
\(\hat{y}=\hat{\beta_0}+\hat{\beta_1}d\). Because \(d\) takes only two
values, the line makes only two predictions. When \(d=0\) the prediction
is \(\hat{\beta_0}\); when \(d=1\) it is
\(\hat{\beta_0}+\hat{\beta_1}\). Those two predictions are exactly the
two group averages:

\begin{bsformula}[A DUMMY AND THE GROUP MEANS]

\[\hat{\beta_0}=\bar{y}_{0} \qquad \hat{\beta_1}=\bar{y}_{1}-\bar{y}_{0}\]
The intercept is the mean of the \textbf{baseline} group (the one coded
\(0\)), and the slope is the \emph{difference} between the two group
means.

\end{bsformula}

In other words, regressing a variable on a single dummy is just another
way of computing a difference of means --- the slope \emph{is} that
difference.

\textbf{More than two categories.} When a categorical variable has \(k\)
categories, we create \(k-1\) dummies and leave one category out to act
as the \textbf{baseline} (or reference). Each remaining dummy's
coefficient measures the gap between its category's mean and the
baseline mean. We never include a dummy for \emph{every} category
alongside an intercept --- doing so makes the columns perfectly
collinear (the \textbf{dummy variable trap}), and the regression cannot
be estimated.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

The \textbf{Spin} data set records \(90\) spin classes taught by three
instructors --- Olivia, Sam, and Ben --- along with \texttt{Total\_kj},
the total energy (in kilojoules) burned during each class. We want each
instructor's average energy output.

Because there are three instructors, we build two dummies and let
\textbf{Olivia} be the baseline:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(Sam = 1\) if the class was taught by Sam, \(0\) otherwise.
\item
  \(Ben = 1\) if the class was taught by Ben, \(0\) otherwise.
\end{itemize}

An Olivia class therefore has \(Sam=0\) and \(Ben=0\). Regressing
\texttt{Total\_kj} on the two dummies gives:

\[\widehat{Total\_kj}=197.03-75.67\,Sam+27.90\,Ben\]

Reading off each instructor's predicted (average) energy:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
\toprule
Instructor & \(Sam\) & \(Ben\) & Prediction & Mean \texttt{Total\_kj} \\
\midrule
Olivia (baseline) & \(0\) & \(0\) & \(197.03\) & \(197.03\) \\
Sam & \(1\) & \(0\) & \(197.03-75.67=121.37\) & \(121.37\) \\
Ben & \(0\) & \(1\) & \(197.03+27.90=224.93\) & \(224.93\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The predictions land exactly on the three group averages. The intercept
is Olivia's mean; the coefficient on \(Sam\) (\(-75.67\)) says Sam's
classes burn about \(75.67\) kJ \emph{less} than Olivia's on average,
and the coefficient on \(Ben\) (\(+27.90\)) says Ben's burn about
\(27.90\) kJ \emph{more}. Regression with dummy variables has reproduced
the group means --- and, as a bonus, reported how far each group sits
from the baseline.

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Dummy Variables in Excel}\label{dummy-variables-in-excel}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Dummy Variables in Excel}

\bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/Spin.xlsx}{Download Data}

With \texttt{Instructor} in column A and \texttt{Total\_kj} in column C,
we build the two dummies with \texttt{IF}. Rather than creating helper
columns, \texttt{HSTACK} lets us assemble both dummies \emph{inside} the
\texttt{LINEST} call:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(C2:C91, HSTACK(IF(A2:A91="Sam",1,0), IF(A2:A91="Ben",1,0)), TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Remember that \texttt{LINEST} returns the coefficients \textbf{in
reverse order}. Because we stacked \(Sam\) first and \(Ben\) second, the
first row of the output reads:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
Position & Row 1 value & Meaning \\
\midrule
Col 1 & \(27.90\) & coefficient on \(Ben\) \\
Col 2 & \(-75.67\) & coefficient on \(Sam\) \\
Col 3 & \(197.03\) & intercept (Olivia) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

If you prefer helper columns, put the two \texttt{IF} formulas in
columns D and E and pass \texttt{D2:E91} as the \(x\) range --- the
result is identical.

To predict each instructor's average directly, use \texttt{TREND} with a
new \(x\) row for each combination:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=TREND(C2:C91, HSTACK(IF(A2:A91="Sam",1,0), IF(A2:A91="Ben",1,0)), \{0,0\})  → 197.03}
\NormalTok{=TREND(C2:C91, HSTACK(IF(A2:A91="Sam",1,0), IF(A2:A91="Ben",1,0)), \{1,0\})  → 121.37}
\NormalTok{=TREND(C2:C91, HSTACK(IF(A2:A91="Sam",1,0), IF(A2:A91="Ben",1,0)), \{0,1\})  → 224.93}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can confirm each group mean directly with \texttt{AVERAGEIF}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=AVERAGEIF(A2:A91, "Olivia", C2:C91)   → 197.03}
\NormalTok{=AVERAGEIF(A2:A91, "Sam", C2:C91)      → 121.37}
\NormalTok{=AVERAGEIF(A2:A91, "Ben", C2:C91)      → 224.93}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Regression in Excel}\label{regression-in-excel}

Excel estimates regressions through three worksheet functions.
\texttt{LINEST} fits the model and reports every diagnostic we need;
\texttt{TREND} produces predictions; and \texttt{HSTACK} assembles the
independent variables into the single block that \texttt{LINEST}
requires. All three work for simple \emph{and} multiple regression, so
one set of tools covers the whole chapter.

\bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/weight_exercise.xlsx}{Download Data}

\subsection*{Reading the LINEST Output}\label{reading-the-linest-output}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Reading the LINEST Output}

\texttt{LINEST} is an \textbf{array function}. Written with both
optional arguments set to \texttt{TRUE}, it returns a block of \(5\)
rows by \(k+1\) columns:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(y\_range, x\_range, TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The third argument asks Excel to estimate an intercept; the fourth asks
for the full diagnostics. In current versions of Excel the result
\emph{spills} automatically --- type the formula in one cell and press
\texttt{Enter}. The block is laid out as follows, for a model with \(k\)
independent variables:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
Row & Column 1 & Column 2 & Remaining columns \\
\midrule
\textbf{1} & \(\hat{\beta_k}\) \ldots{} \(\hat{\beta_1}\), then \(\hat{\beta_0}\) &  & coefficients \\
\textbf{2} & standard error of each coefficient &  &  \\
\textbf{3} & \(R^2\) & \(s_e\) & \texttt{\#N/A} \\
\textbf{4} & \(F\) statistic & \(df=n-k-1\) & \texttt{\#N/A} \\
\textbf{5} & \(SSR\) & \(SSE\) & \texttt{\#N/A} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Two details matter enormously in practice.

\textbf{Row 1 runs backwards.} The coefficients appear in reverse order
of your \(x\) columns --- the \emph{last} variable first and the
intercept \emph{last}. With one predictor the row is simply
\(\{\hat{\beta}, \hat{\alpha}\}\); with two it is
\(\{\hat{\beta_2}, \hat{\beta_1}, \hat{\beta_0}\}\). Misreading this is
the most common \texttt{LINEST} error.

\textbf{The \(x\) range must be one contiguous block.} \texttt{LINEST}
will not accept scattered columns. When your independent variables are
not side by side, \texttt{HSTACK} joins them into a single array:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(B2:B6, HSTACK(C2:C6, F2:F6), TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

To pull a single number out of the block instead of spilling all of it,
wrap the call in \texttt{INDEX(array,\ row,\ column)}.

\subsection*{Simple Regression (Weight and
Exercise)}\label{simple-regression-weight-and-exercise}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Simple Regression (Weight and
Exercise)}

With \texttt{Weight} in column A and \texttt{Exercise} in column B:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(A2:A6, B2:B6, TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Reading the block, and recalling that row 1 is reversed:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,B2:B6,TRUE,TRUE),1,1)} →
  \(\hat{\beta}=-0.153\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,B2:B6,TRUE,TRUE),1,2)} →
  \(\hat{\alpha}=171.00\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,B2:B6,TRUE,TRUE),3,1)} → \(R^2=0.70\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,B2:B6,TRUE,TRUE),3,2)} → \(s_e=1.59\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,B2:B6,TRUE,TRUE),5,1)} → \(SSR=17.63\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,B2:B6,TRUE,TRUE),5,2)} → \(SSE=7.57\)
\end{itemize}

Since \(SST=SSR+SSE\), the total variation is \(17.63+7.57=25.2\),
matching the value we computed by hand. (\texttt{=DEVSQ(A2:A6)} returns
the same \(25.2\) as a check.)

\subsection*{Multiple Regression (Weight, Exercise, and
Calories)}\label{multiple-regression-weight-exercise-and-calories}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Multiple Regression (Weight, Exercise,
and Calories)}

With \texttt{Calories} in column C, the two independent variables are
adjacent, so \texttt{B2:C6} works directly. Using \texttt{HSTACK} makes
the intent explicit and keeps the formula correct even if the columns
are later moved apart:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(A2:A6, HSTACK(B2:B6, C2:C6), TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The first row of the output is \(\{0.069,\ -0.025,\ 83.96\}\) ---
reading \textbf{right to left}, that is an intercept of \(83.96\), a
coefficient of \(-0.025\) on Exercise, and \(0.069\) on Calories, giving

\[\hat{y_i}=83.96-0.025x+0.069z\]

The remaining diagnostics come from the same block:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6),TRUE,TRUE),3,1)} →
  \(R^2=0.96\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6),TRUE,TRUE),3,2)} →
  \(s_e=0.71\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6),TRUE,TRUE),4,2)} →
  \(df=n-k-1=2\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6),TRUE,TRUE),5,1)} →
  \(SSR=24.19\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6),TRUE,TRUE),5,2)} →
  \(SSE=1.01\)
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Building the Anova Table}\label{building-the-anova-table}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Building the Anova Table}

\texttt{LINEST} reports the \emph{total} \(SSR\) for a model, not the
contribution of each variable. To split \(SSR\) by variable, run the
models in sequence and take the difference:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6, B2:B6, TRUE, TRUE), 5, 1)                      → 17.63   (x alone)}
\NormalTok{=INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6, HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6), TRUE, TRUE), 5, 1)        → 24.19   (x and z)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The difference, \(24.19-17.63=6.55\), is what Calories adds once
Exercise is already in the model. The residual \(SSE=1.01\) comes from
row 5, column 2 of the larger model. Those three numbers are exactly the
Anova table above.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{Adjusted
\(R^2\)}{Adjusted R\^{}2}}\label{adjusted-r2-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Adjusted \(R^2\)}

\texttt{LINEST} does not report \(\bar{R}^2\), but every ingredient is
in the block. With \(R^2\) in row 3 column 1 and \(df=n-k-1\) in row 4
column 2:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=1{-}(1{-}INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6),TRUE,TRUE),3,1))}
\NormalTok{     *(COUNT(A2:A6){-}1)/INDEX(LINEST(A2:A6,HSTACK(B2:B6,C2:C6),TRUE,TRUE),4,2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

which returns \(0.92\), matching the hand calculation.

\subsection*{Making Predictions}\label{making-predictions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Making Predictions}

\texttt{TREND} applies the fitted model to new values of the independent
variables. It accepts the same \(x\) block as \texttt{LINEST}, so it
works for one predictor or many:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=TREND(y\_range, x\_range, new\_x\_range, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For the Advertisement and Price example, with a budget of \(6\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=TREND(B2:B5, A2:A5, 6)   → 14.7}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For the Weight model, predicting a person who exercises \(35\) minutes
and eats \(1210\) calories:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=TREND(A2:A6, HSTACK(B2:B6, C2:C6), HSTACK(35, 1210))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{new\_x} block must have the same number of columns, in the
same order, as the \(x\) block used to fit the model. Omitting
\texttt{new\_x} returns the fitted value for every observation in the
original data.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-4}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=LINEST(y\_range,\ x\_range,\ TRUE,\ TRUE)} estimates the
  regression and returns a \(5 \times (k+1)\) block containing the
  coefficients, their standard errors, \(R^2\), \(s_e\), the \(F\)
  statistic, the degrees of freedom, \(SSR\) and \(SSE\). Works for any
  number of independent variables. \textbf{Row 1 is in reverse order},
  ending with the intercept.
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(...),\ row,\ column)} extracts a single value
  from the \texttt{LINEST} block. Common picks: \texttt{(1,1)} the slope
  of the last \(x\), \texttt{(3,1)} \(R^2\), \texttt{(3,2)} \(s_e\),
  \texttt{(4,2)} \(df\), \texttt{(5,1)} \(SSR\), \texttt{(5,2)} \(SSE\).
\item
  \texttt{=HSTACK(range1,\ range2,\ ...)} joins ranges side by side into
  a single array, so that non-adjacent independent variables can be
  passed to \texttt{LINEST} or \texttt{TREND}. It also lets dummy
  variables be built inline without helper columns.
\item
  \texttt{=TREND(y\_range,\ x\_range,\ new\_x\_range,\ TRUE)} returns
  predicted values of \(y\). Handles simple and multiple regression;
  \texttt{new\_x\_range} must match the column layout of
  \texttt{x\_range}.
\item
  \texttt{=IF(cell="Category",\ 1,\ 0)} builds a dummy variable, coding
  each row \(1\) when it belongs to a category and \(0\) otherwise.
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGEIF(range,\ criteria,\ average\_range)} returns the
  mean of a numerical column for the rows matching a category --- a
  quick way to verify the group means produced by a dummy-variable
  regression.
\item
  \texttt{=DEVSQ(y\_range)} returns \(SST\) directly, as a check on
  \(SSR+SSE\).
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, breakable, left=2mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, coltitle=black, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, opacityback=0, titlerule=0mm, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, bottomrule=.15mm]

\textbf{A note on versions.} \texttt{HSTACK} requires Microsoft 365 or
Excel 2024. In earlier versions, place the independent variables in
adjacent columns and pass the block directly to \texttt{LINEST} and
\texttt{TREND} --- every other formula in this chapter is unchanged.
\texttt{LINEST} and \texttt{TREND} are array formulas: in versions
before dynamic arrays, select the full output range first and confirm
with \texttt{Ctrl+Shift+Enter}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\Needspace{7.4in}

Sheet}\label{chapter-summary-cheat-sheet-6}

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-6}

The following exercises will help you get practice on Regression Line
estimation and interpretation. In particular, the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Estimating the slope and intercept.
\item
  Calculating measures of goodness of fit.
\item
  Prediction using the regression line.
\end{itemize}

Answers are in the \hyperref[answers]{Answers to Exercises} at the end
of the book. Try not to peek until you have formulated your own answer
and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\part{Uncertainty}

\chapter{Probability I}\label{probability-i}

Decision-making under uncertainty is at the core of business strategy,
risk management, and forecasting. This section will study tools and
concepts that will make us smarter when making data-driven decisions.
Mastering probability in finance, marketing, supply chain, or operations
helps professionals analyze risks, optimize strategies, and stay
competitive in uncertain environments. We start with the basic concepts
and ideas of probability below. Probability marks a turning point in the
book. Until now we have described data we already collected; from here
we build the mathematics of uncertainty --- the bridge that will later
let us reason from an imperfect sample back to the world that produced
it.

\section{Frequentist Vs. Bayesian
Statistics}\label{frequentist-vs.-bayesian-statistics}

There are two interpretations of probability. The \textbf{frequentist}
interpretation assumes that probabilities represent proportions of
specific events occurring over infinitely identical trials. In contrast,
the \textbf{Bayesian} interpretation assumes that probabilities are
subjective beliefs about the relative likelihood of events. Despite
their philosophical differences, both approaches adhere to the core
rules of probability ---including the Addition Rule, Multiplication
Rule, and Bayes' Theorem--- which provide a consistent mathematical
foundation for making probabilistic inferences.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

A company might use frequentist statistics to determine the average
return rate of a product, but apply Bayesian methods to adjust sales
forecasts based on changing consumer behavior.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Experiments and Sets}\label{experiments-and-sets}

The following concepts form the foundation of probability theory. They
provide a structure that is useful when analyzing uncertainty and
randomness.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  An \textbf{experiment} is a process that leads to one of several
  outcomes. \emph{Ex: Tossing a Die, Tossing a Coin, Drawing a Card,
  etc.}
\item
  An \textbf{outcome} is the result of an experiment. \emph{Ex: A coin
  landing on heads, drawing the ace of spades.}
\item
  The \textbf{sample space} \((S)\) of an experiment contains all
  possible outcomes of the experiment. \emph{Ex:} \(S=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}\)
  is the sample space for tossing a die.
\item
  An \textbf{event} is a subset of the sample space. \emph{Ex:}
  \(A=\{2,4,6\}\) is the event of tossing an even number when rolling a
  die. \(B=\{1A,1D,1S,1H\}\) is the event of drawing an ace from a deck
  of cards.
\end{itemize}

\section{Basic Probability Concepts}\label{basic-probability-concepts}

A \textbf{probability} is a numerical value that measures the likelihood
that an event occurs. To calculate \textbf{probabilities}, we find the
ratio between favorable outcomes and total outcomes. Hence, the
probability of an event is given by:

\begin{bsformula}[PROBABILITY OF AN EVENT]

\[P(A)=\frac{Number\ of\ outcomes\ in\ A}{Number\ of\ outcomes\ in\ Sample \ Space}\]

\end{bsformula}

The probability of A satisfies \(0\leq P(A) \leq1\). Since A is a subset
of S, the number of elements in A is at most that of the sample space S.
Hence, if A=S, then P(A)=1. If A is a null event (i.e., has no elements)
P(A)=0 and is impossible. We can conclude that P(A) ranges from 0 to 1,
inclusive.

In many cases, we analyze how multiple events interact within the sample
space, particularly when they either exclude each other or collectively
cover all possible outcomes.\textbf{Mutually exclusive} events do not
share any common outcomes. The occurrence of one event precludes the
occurrence of others. \textbf{Exhaustive} events include all outcomes in
the sample space.

Given these concepts if follows that the sum of the probabilities of a
list of \textbf{mutually exclusive} and \textbf{exhaustive} events
equals \(1\). Formally, we state: \(\sum P(x_i)=1\), where \(x_i\) is
outcome \(i\).

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's consider a standard deck of 52 playing cards to illustrate the key
probability concepts.

If we randomly draw one card from a standard deck, the sample space
\(S\) consists of all 52 cards. Suppose we define event \(A\) as
\emph{drawing a red card}. Since there are 26 red cards, the probability
is:

\[P(A) = \frac{\text{Number of red cards}}{\text{Total number of cards}} = \frac{26}{52} = 0.5\]

Since \(( 0 \leq P(A) \leq 1)\), this follows the basic probability
rule. Next let's consider mutually exclusive events. Let event \(B\) be
drawing a heart and event \(C\) be drawing a spade. Since a single drawn
card cannot be both a heart and a spade, these events are mutually
exclusive.

The four suits (\emph{hearts, diamonds, clubs, spades}) form an
exhaustive set, because every card belongs to exactly one suit. The sum
of their probabilities equals 1:

\[P(\text{hearts}) + P(\text{diamonds}) + P(\text{clubs}) + P(\text{spades}) = \frac{13}{52} + \frac{13}{52} + \frac{13}{52} + \frac{13}{52} = 1\]

Note that this also satisfies the rule \(\sum P(x_i) = 1\), where
\(x_i\) represents each possible outcome (suit).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Venn Diagram}\label{venn-diagram}

A Venn diagram is a visual representation of sets and their
relationships. It consists of overlapping circles, each representing a
set. Below is an example:

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/venn.png}
\end{center}

The rectangle enclosing the circles represents the sample set (i.e., the
52 playing cards). Each circle represents a set or event The heart
circle contains all 13 hearts; the same holds for the clubs circle. The
area outside the circle that has hearts represents its complement,
meaning elements that are clubs, spades, or diamonds. Since the two sets
do not overlap, they are mutually exclusive (i.e., if a card is a heart,
it can't be a spade). The total area covered by both sets represents the
\textbf{union} denoted by \(\cup\). This is the set that contains all
elements that belong to at least one of the sets. Formally, we would
represent this set as \((Hearts \cup Spades)\).

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/venn2.png}
\end{center}

The overlapping region of two sets contains elements that belong to both
sets. This region is called the \textbf{intersection}. The intersection
contains elements that are Hearts and Jacks (i.e., the jack of hearts).
Formally, we denote the intersection by \((Hearts \cap Jacks)\).

\section{Probability Rules}\label{probability-rules}

Probability rules help us quantify uncertainty, avoid cognitive biases,
and make rational predictions based on available data. These rules allow
us to make reliable decisions. Below we introduce some useful rules.

\subsection*{Complement Rule}\label{complement-rule}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Complement Rule}

The \textbf{Complement Rule} states that:

\begin{bsformula}[THE COMPLEMENT RULE]

\[P(A^c)=1-P(A)\] where \(A^c\) is the complement of \(A\).

\end{bsformula}

This rule is useful when calculating probabilities for complex
scenarios, where finding the probability of the complement is easier
than computing the event itself.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

The probability of drawing a spade, diamond or clubs can be estimated by
just calculating the probability of drawing a heart. Since the
probability of drawing a heart is \(P(Heart)=\frac{13}{52}\), then using
the complement rule,
\(P(Not \text{Heart})=1-\frac{13}{52}=\frac{39}{52}\).

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{De Morgan's Laws}\label{de-morgans-laws}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{De Morgan's Laws}

\textbf{De Morgan's Laws} relate the complement of a union to the
complement of an intersection. They are handy for rewriting a
complicated event as an easier-to-compute one.

\begin{bsformula}[DE MORGAN’S LAWS]

\[(A \cup B)^c = A^c \cap B^c \qquad\qquad (A \cap B)^c = A^c \cup B^c\]
The complement of a union is the intersection of the complements, and
the complement of an intersection is the union of the complements.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Roll a die with \(A=\{1,2,3\}\) and \(B=\{2,4,6\}\). Their union is
\(A \cup B=\{1,2,3,4,6\}\), so the complement is \((A \cup B)^c=\{5\}\).
De Morgan's Law gives the same answer from the complements:
\(A^c \cap B^c=\{4,5,6\} \cap \{1,3,5\}=\{5\}\).

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Addition Rule}\label{addition-rule}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Addition Rule}

The \textbf{Addition Rule} states:

\begin{bsformula}[THE ADDITION RULE]

\[P(A \cup B)=P(A)+P(B)-P(A \cap B)\] where \(\cap\) is intersection and
\(\cup\) is union.

\end{bsformula}

This formula prevents double-counting when events overlap by including
the intersection of events.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's calculate the probability of drawing either a heart or a face
card. If we let event \(A\) be drawing a heart, and event \(B\) be
drawing a face card, the probability is given by:
\[P(A \cup B)=\frac{13}{52}+\frac{12}{52}-\frac{3}{52}=\frac{22}{52}\]

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Multiplication Rule}\label{multiplication-rule}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Multiplication Rule}

The \textbf{Multiplication Rule} states:

\begin{bsformula}[THE MULTIPLICATION RULE]

\[P(A \cap B)= P(A|B)P(B)\] where \(P(A|B)\) is the conditional
probability.

\end{bsformula}

\textbf{Conditional probability} measures the likelihood of an event
occurring given that another event has already happened. If we
reorganize the formula above we can get:

\begin{bsformula}[CONDITIONAL PROBABILITY]

\[P(A|B)=\frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)}\]

\end{bsformula}

Note that this formula focuses on cases where Event B has already
occurred. We can then find the fraction of the cases where Event A also
happens.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Suppose we randomly draw two cards from a standard deck of 52 cards,
without replacement. The probability that both cards are aces is given
by:
\[P(Ace \cap Ace)=P(Ace|Ace) \times P(Ace)= \frac {4}{52} \times \frac{3}{51}\]

\end{bsexample}

Lastly, if events \(A\) and \(B\) are independent, we can simplify the
multiplication rule to:

\begin{bsformula}[INDEPENDENCE]

\[P(A \cap B)= P(A)P(B)\]

\end{bsformula}

Hence, we can define independence as: events \(A\) and \(B\) are
independent if the occurrence (or non occurrence) of one does not affect
the probability of the other occurring.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Suppose we randomly draw a card from a standard deck, replace it, and
then draw another card. The probability of drawing a heart in the first
draw is \(13/52\), and the probability of drawing a spade in the second
draw is also \(13/52\).

\end{bsexample}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Suppose that the weather is sunny; this has no effect on the outcome of
a lottery. Hence, the weather and lottery outcomes are independent.

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Law of Total Probability}\label{law-of-total-probability}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Law of Total Probability}

The \textbf{Law of Total Probability} states:

\begin{bsformula}[LAW OF TOTAL PROBABILITY]

\[P(A)=P(A \cap B)+P(A\cap B^c)\] which, using the multiplication rule,
can also be written as \[P(A)=P(A|B)P(B)+P(A|B^c)P(B^c)\]

\end{bsformula}

The formula mainly states that if an event \(A\) can occur in multiple
mutually exclusive scenarios (or conditions), then the total probability
of \(A\) is the sum of its probabilities under each scenario, weighted
by how often those scenarios occur. The Venn diagram helps us visualize
the law of total probability.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/venn3.png}
\end{center}

The image shows how event \(A\) can occur. Essentially, when \(B\)
occurs \(A\) is possible in the left semicircle. When \(B^c\), then
\(A\) is possible in the right semicircle. The addition of both
semicircles, complete set \(A\).

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider the sets face cards and hearts. The entire set of hearts can be
completed by taking face cards that are hearts, and non face cards (the
complement of face cards) that are hearts.

\end{bsexample}

The same idea extends to \textbf{any} number of scenarios. If
\(B_1, B_2, \dots, B_k\) are mutually exclusive and together cover the
whole sample space, the total probability of \(A\) is found by summing
across all of them:

\begin{bsformula}[LAW OF TOTAL PROBABILITY (Several Events)]

\[P(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{k} P(A \cap B_i)=\sum_{i=1}^{k} P(A|B_i)\,P(B_i)\]
where the events \(B_1, B_2, \dots, B_k\) are mutually exclusive and
exhaustive (they partition the sample space).

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

A factory makes a part on three machines. Machine 1 produces \(50\%\) of
the parts with a \(2\%\) defect rate, Machine 2 produces \(30\%\) with a
\(3\%\) defect rate, and Machine 3 produces \(20\%\) with a \(5\%\)
defect rate. The overall probability that a randomly chosen part is
defective is \[P(D)=(0.50)(0.02)+(0.30)(0.03)+(0.20)(0.05)=0.029\] so
about \(2.9\%\) of all parts are defective.

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Bayes' Theorem}\label{bayes-theorem}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Bayes' Theorem}

\textbf{Bayes' Theorem} states:

\begin{bsformula}[BAYES’ THEOREM]

\[P(A|B)= \frac{P(B|A)P(A)}{P(B)}\]

\end{bsformula}

In essence, Bayes' theorem is a way of updating probabilities in light
of new evidence. The theorem tells us that the probability of an event A
given that we have observed some evidence is equal to the prior
probability (\(P(A)\)) times a factor that measures the informativeness
of the observed evidence \(P(B|A)/P(B)\).

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Assume that a rare form of cancer affects 1\% of the population. That
is, 1 out of 100 people will likely be affected by it. There is a test
for the cancer, but it is imperfect. In particular, if you don't have
cancer, the test says that you do 10\% of the time (false positive
rate). Also, if you do have cancer, it will tell you that you don't have
it 5\% of the time. Let's say you get tested, and your doctor calls you
and says you have tested positive. How likely is it that you have this
type of cancer?

We can use the Law of Total Probability and Bayes' theorem to answer
this question. Let's first identify what we want to estimate. Since we
need to figure out the likelihood of having cancer, given that we tested
positive, we are looking for \(P(C|+)\). Now, we can set up Bayes' rule
to see what additional information we need to find our desired value.
Bayes' rule states:

\[P(C|+)= \frac{P(+|C)P(C)}{P(+)}\] Since the problem does not provide
the \(P(+)\) we must estimate it with the Law of Total Probability,
mainly:

\[P(+)=P(+|C)P(C)+P(+|NC)P(NC)=(0.95 \times 0.01+(0.1 \times 0.99)=0.1085\]
We can now use this probability to find our new belief that we have
cancer:

\[P(C|+)= \frac{(0.95 \times 0.01) }{0.1085}=0.0875\] In conclusion, if
we before thought we had a \(1\%\) chance of having the rare form of
cancer, once we were told that we tested positive, we should update our
belief to about \(8.75\%\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Contingency Tables}\label{contingency-tables}

A \textbf{contingency table} (or cross-tabulation) summarizes the
relationship between two or events (or variables). It is particularly
helpful when analyzing frequencies of events and there relationship to
other events.

The contingency table below shows the frequency of event A and B
occurring/not occurring.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
\caption{Contingency table: frequencies of A and B (n =
100)}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
& \(B\) & \(B^c\) & Total \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
& \(B\) & \(B^c\) & Total \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(A\) & 26 & 34 & 60 \\
\(A^c\) & 14 & 26 & 40 \\
Total & 40 & 60 & 100 \\
\end{longtable}

We can see that event A occurs 60 times and that event B occurs 40
times. Most importantly, we can also see how the two events are not
mutually exclusive, since A and B occur 26 times. The table can be
easily translated to probabilities, by divinding each entry by the total
amount of outcomes (i.e., 100). We call the table that includes
probabilities the \textbf{joint probability table}.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}cccc@{}}
\caption{Joint probability table: joint probabilities inside, marginal
probabilities in the Total row and column}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
& \(B\) & \(B^c\) & Total \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
& \(B\) & \(B^c\) & Total \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(A\) & 0.26 & 0.34 & 0.60 \\
\(A^c\) & 0.14 & 0.26 & 0.40 \\
Total & 0.40 & 0.60 & 1 \\
\end{longtable}

In the middle of the table we have the joint probabilities or
intersections of events (i.e., \(A \cap B\), \(A \cap B^c\), etc.). The
values in the totals columns or rows are the probabilities of each event
happening (i.e., \(P(A)\), \(P(B^c)\), etc.). We call these
probabilities \textbf{marginal probabilities} since they reside at the
margin of the table.

\section{Counting Rules}\label{counting-rules}

Counting rules are useful in probability as they allow us to determine
the number of ways events can occur without listing out every
possibility. Essentially, they facilitate the enumeration of possible
outcomes for large sets.

\subsection*{The Fundamental Counting
Principle}\label{the-fundamental-counting-principle}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{The Fundamental Counting Principle}

The most basic counting rule --- and the one all the others build on ---
is the \textbf{Fundamental Counting Principle} (also called the
Multiplication Principle). It states that if a task is completed in
separate stages, the total number of ways to complete it is the product
of the number of choices available at each stage.

\begin{bsformula}[THE FUNDAMENTAL COUNTING PRINCIPLE]

\[m_1 \times m_2 \times \dots \times m_k\] where \(m_i\) is the number
of choices at stage \(i\), for a task made up of \(k\) stages.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

A restaurant offers \(3\) appetizers, \(4\) entrées, and \(2\) desserts.
The number of different three-course meals is
\(3 \times 4 \times 2 = 24\).

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Factorial}\label{factorial}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Factorial}

To illustrate the factorial, consider the number of ways that we can
organize a group of three people (Ann, Beth, Charlie). The enumeration
of all the possible orderings in this case is not that tedious and it
can be shown in a table:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cc}
\toprule
Ordering & Order \\
\midrule
1 & (Ann, Beth, Charlie) \\
2 & (Ann, Charlie, Beth) \\
3 & (Beth, Ann, Charlie) \\
4 & (Beth, Charlie, Ann) \\
5 & (Charlie, Ann, Beth) \\
6 & (Charlie, Beth, Ann) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The table shows that there are six possible ways to organize a group of
three people. This is really just the Fundamental Counting Principle at
work: there are \(3\) choices for the first spot, then \(2\) remaining
choices for the second, and only \(1\) left for the third, giving
\(3 \times 2 \times 1 = 6\). The \textbf{Factorial} captures exactly
this pattern --- it counts how many ways a group of \(N\) objects can be
organized. Formally, we write:

\begin{bsformula}[THE FACTORIAL]

\[Factorial=N!\] where the operator \(!\) means to take the product of
all positive integers from \(N\) down to \(1\). Note that \(0!\) is
equal to one.

\end{bsformula}

If we then try to find in how many ways we can organize a group of three
people, we can avoid enumeration by calculating
\(3!=3 \times 2 \times 1=6\).

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's assume that a manager has locked down the first five batters in a
given game, but is undecided on the batting order. The number of ways
that he can organize the five batters is given by
\(5!=5 \times 4 \times 3 \times 2 \times 1=120\).

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Combinations}\label{combinations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Combinations}

The \textbf{Combination} function counts the number of ways to choose
\(x\) objects from a total of \(n\) objects. The order in which the
\(x\) objects are listed does not matter. If repetition is not allowed
use:

\begin{bsformula}[COMBINATIONS (No Repetition)]

\[C(n, x)= \frac{n!}{(n-x)!x!}\]

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's imagine visiting the Mexican restaurant Chipotle. We are choosing
a bowl for lunch and we can choose from the following items: beans,
chicken, rice, pico, guac, and beef. If we are only allowed to select
four items out of the six, in how many ways can we construct a bowl?
Below we use the combination formula to show that there are \(15\)
possible bowls. \[C(6, 4)= \frac{6!}{(6-4)!4!}=\frac{6 \times 5}{2}=15\]

\end{bsexample}

There are situations when we wish to count all possible combinations
inclusive of repetition of elements. If repetition is allowed we use the
following formula:

\begin{bsformula}[COMBINATIONS (With Repetition)]

\[C(n+x-1,x)=\frac{(x+n-1)!}{(n-1)!x!}\]

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Imagine you visit the ice cream store. You want a three scoop dish of
ice cream. In how many ways can you choose your dish if the flavors are
vanilla, chocolate, smores, brownie, and chips? Below we use the
combination formula with repetition to show that the answer is \(35\).
\[C(7,3)=\frac{(5+3-1)!}{(5-1)!3!}= 7 \times 5=35\]

\end{bsexample}

\subsection*{Permutations}\label{permutations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Permutations}

The \textbf{Permutation} function also counts the number of ways to
choose \(x\) objects from a total of \(n\) objects. However, the order
in which the \(x\) objects are listed does matter. Note that since order
matters, the permutation will always have a higher number of possible
outcomes relative to the combination. When repetition is not allowed,
permutations can be counted by:

\begin{bsformula}[PERMUTATIONS (No Repetition)]

\[P(n,x)= \frac{n!}{(n-x)!}\]

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

The Olympic medal finals for the 100 meters sprint has eight runners
competing for three medals. How many medal podiums are possible? There
are a total of \(336\). Below you can see the calculation.
\[P(8,3)= \frac{8!}{5!}= 8 \times 7 \times 6=336\]

\end{bsexample}

As with combinations, there might be situations where we wish to count
when repetition is allowed. If this is the case, use:

\begin{bsformula}[PERMUTATIONS (With Repetition)]

\[P(n,x)=n^x\]

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

You are trying to guess the combination of a lock that has three
possible numbers. Since there are 10 numbers to choose from, there are
\(10^3=1000\) possible permutations.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Probability in Excel}\label{probability-in-excel}

Excel has a collection of functions that make counting and probability
calculations straightforward.

\textbf{Contingency tables:} Use a \textbf{PivotTable}
(\texttt{Insert\ →\ PivotTable}) to build a contingency table from raw
data --- place one variable in the Rows area, the other in the Columns
area, and any field in the Values area as a count. To turn the counts
into a joint probability table, use
\texttt{Value\ Field\ Settings\ →\ Show\ Values\ As\ →\ \%\ of\ Grand\ Total}.
You can also count a specific combination of conditions directly with
\texttt{=COUNTIFS(range1,\ criteria1,\ range2,\ criteria2)}.

\textbf{Factorial:} The \texttt{=FACT(number)} function returns the
factorial of a number. For example, \texttt{=FACT(5)} returns \(120\).

\textbf{Combinations:} The \texttt{=COMBIN(number,\ number\_chosen)}
function counts the number of combinations without repetition. Use
\texttt{=COMBINA(number,\ number\_chosen)} when repetition is allowed.

\textbf{Permutations:} The \texttt{=PERMUT(number,\ number\_chosen)}
function counts the number of permutations without repetition. Use
\texttt{=PERMUTATIONA(number,\ number\_chosen)} when repetition is
allowed.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-5}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=COUNTIFS(range1,\ criteria1,\ ...)} counts the observations
  that satisfy several conditions at once, useful for building
  contingency tables.
\item
  \texttt{=FACT(number)} returns the factorial of a number.
\item
  \texttt{=COMBIN(number,\ number\_chosen)} returns the number of
  combinations without repetition;
  \texttt{=COMBINA(number,\ number\_chosen)} allows repetition.
\item
  \texttt{=PERMUT(number,\ number\_chosen)} returns the number of
  permutations without repetition;
  \texttt{=PERMUTATIONA(number,\ number\_chosen)} allows repetition.
\end{itemize}

The following exercises will help you practice some probability concepts
and formulas. In particular, the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\item
  Applying probability rules.
\item
  Using counting rules.
\end{itemize}

Answers are in the \hyperref[answers]{Answers to Exercises} at the end
of the book. Try not to peak until you have a formulated your own answer
and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

For the following exercises, make your calculations by hand and verify
results with a calculator or Excel.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A sample space \(S\) yields five equally likely events, \(A\), \(B\),
  \(C\), \(D\), and \(E\). Find \(P(D)\), \(P(B^c)\), and
  \(P(A \cup C \cup E)\).
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans70}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Consider the roll of a die. Define \(A\) as \{1,2,3\}, \(B\) as
  \{1,2,3,5,6\}, \(C\) as \{4,6\}, and \(D\) as \{4,5,6\}. Are the
  events \(A\) and \(B\) mutually exclusive, exhaustive, both or none?
  What about events \(A\) and \(D\)?
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans71}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  A recent study suggests that \(33.1\)\% of the adult U.S. population
  is overweight and \(35.7\)\% obese. What is the probability that a
  randomly selected adult in the U.S. is either obese or overweight? Are
  the events mutually exclusive and exhaustive? What is the probability
  that their weight is normal?
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans72}

\subsection*{Exercise 2}\label{exercise-2-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 2}

For the following exercises, make your calculations by hand and verify

\begin{enumerate}

\chapter{Probability II}\label{probability-ii}

Studying discrete probability distributions is essential for
understanding and predicting outcomes in uncertain scenarios,
particularly in business, finance, and data science. These distributions
model events with distinct, countable outcomes, such as customer
purchases, defect counts, or market trends, enabling decision-makers to
quantify risks and optimize strategies. Below, we introduce some popular
distributions and their business application. Each distribution is a
model of how an uncertain outcome behaves --- the kind of model that, in
the inference chapters, will let us judge how far a sample statistic
might stray from the truth.

\section{Random Variables}\label{random-variables}

The study of probability distributions begins with a random variable. A
\textbf{random variable} assigns a numerical value to each possible
experimental outcome. The table below presents a collection of
experiments along with their corresponding random variables.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\caption{Experiments and their random variables}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Experiment
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Random variable (\(x\))
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Possible values
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Experiment
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Random variable (\(x\))
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Possible values
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Contact five customers & Number of customers who place an order & 0, 1,
2, 3, 4, 5 \\
Operate a restaurant for one day & Number of customers & 0, 1, 2, 3,
\ldots{} \\
Sell a car & Result of sale & 0 = no sale; 1 = sale \\
\end{longtable}

For restaurant owners, the number of customers they attract is a crucial
variable to monitor. Tracking this variable and its possible values
helps in making informed business decisions. Below we will study how
this type of experiment and several others behave, so that we can make
better decisions. Note that since the outcomes are integer values, the
resulting distribution is discrete. The \textbf{probability mass
function} (PMF) summarizes the possible values generated by a discrete
probability distribution.

\section{Expected Value and Variance}\label{expected-value-and-variance}

When summarizing a random variable, we are mostly interested in the
variable's central tendency (Expected Value) and dispersion (Variance).

The \textbf{expected value} (mean) is a measure of central location. For
a discrete random variable it is given by:

\begin{bsformula}[THE EXPECTED VALUE]

\[E(x)=\mu=\sum xf(x)\] where \(f(x)\) is the probability mass function.
For a continuous random variable it is
\(E(x)= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} x f(x)\, dx\), where \(f(x)\) is the
probability density function.

\end{bsformula}

The \textbf{variance} summarizes the deviation of the values of the
random variable from the mean. It is calculated by:

\begin{bsformula}[THE VARIANCE]

\[var(x)=E[(x-E(x))^2]=E(x^2)-[E(x)]^2\]

\end{bsformula}

Note that this formula can be used for both discrete and continuous
random variables.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let's consider an incentive pay scheme for a company. The table below
summarizes the scheme:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
Bonus, \(x\) (in \$1,000) & Performance type & Probability, \(f(x)\) \\
\midrule
10 & Superior & 0.15 \\
6 & Good & 0.25 \\
3 & Fair & 0.40 \\
0 & Poor & 0.20 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

If a worker receives a ``Superior'' rating, they earn a \$10,000 bonus.
Based on years of employee data, the company has determined that a
``Superior'' rating occurs 15\% of the time. It also lists all of the
possible ratings of its employees and their likelihood on the table.

To summarize this distribution we can calculate the mean bonus paid.
Using the expected value formula we find that the company pays 4,200
dollars on average in compensation.

\[E(x)=(10 \times 0.15) + (6 \times 0.25) + (3 \times 0.4) + (0 \times 0.2)=4.2\]
The variability of the compensation is given by:
\[var(x)=(100 \times 0.15) + (36 \times 0.25) + (9 \times 0.4) + (0 \times 0.2)-(4.2)^2=9.96\]
The expected value and the variance has helped us summarize the results
of the company's incentive scheme. Mainly, the company pays on average
an incentive of 4,200 dollars give or take 3,156 dollars.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Discrete Uniform
Distribution}\label{discrete-uniform-distribution}

The \textbf{discrete uniform distribution} is a probability distribution
that assigns equal probability to each outcome in a finite set of
possible outcomes. In other words, each outcome in the set is equally
likely to occur.

\begin{bsformula}[THE DISCRETE UNIFORM DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)=1/n\] \[E(x)=\frac {\sum x_i}{n}\]
\[var(x)=\frac {\sum (x_i-E(x))^2}{n}\] where \(n\) is the number of
possible outcomes and \(x_i\) are the possible values.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider tossing a fair die. Since all outcomes are equally likely, the
probability of the die landing on 6 is \(f(x)=1/6\). The expected value
on a roll of a die is \(E(x)=\frac {1+2+3+4+5+6}{6}=3.5\) and the
variance is
\(var(x)=\frac {(-2.5)^2+(-1.5)^2+(-0.5)^2+0.5^2+1.5^2+2.5^2}{6}\approx 2.92\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Binomial Distribution}\label{binomial-distribution}

The binomial distribution is a probability distribution that describes
the outcome of a sequence of \(n\) independent Bernoulli trials. In a
Bernoulli trial, there are only two possible outcomes: ``success'' and
``failure''. The probability of success is denoted by \(p\), and the
probability of failure is denoted by \(q = 1 - p\). In a sequence of
\(n\) independent Bernoulli trials, the number of successes (\(x\)) is a
random variable that follows a binomial distribution.

\begin{bsformula}[THE BINOMIAL DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)=C_x^n (p^x)(1-p)^{n-x}\] \[E(x)=np \qquad var(x)=np(1-p)\] where
\(n\) is the number of trials, \(x\) is the number of successes, \(p\)
is the probability of success, and \(C_x^n\) is the number of ways there
can be \(x\) successes in \(n\) trials.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider the following scenario. Imagine your favorite team in the NBA
Finals, trailing by 2 points with seconds left. Your star player, a 90\%
free-throw shooter, gets three shots. What's the chance your team takes
the lead?

Since shooting free-throws can be thought as a binomial experiment, we
can find the probability using the binomial probability mass function.
To take the lead, the star player needs to make all three shots. We can
substitute three into the function:

\[f(3)=C_3^3 (0.9^3)(0.1)^{0}=0.9^3=0.729\]

The function indicates that there is a 72.9\% chance of taking the lead.

\end{bsexample}

\section{The Hypergeometric
Distribution}\label{the-hypergeometric-distribution}

The \textbf{hypergeometric distribution} is a probability distribution
that describes the outcome of drawing a sample from a population without
replacement. It is used to calculate the probability of drawing a
certain number of successes (\(x\)) in a sample of a given size (\(n\)),
where the success or failure of each individual draw is dependent on the
success or failure of other draws.

The \textbf{hypergeometric} experiment differs from the binomial since:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  trials are not independent.
\item
  the probability of success changes from trial to trial.
\end{itemize}

\begin{bsformula}[THE HYPERGEOMETRIC DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)=\frac {C_x^r C_{n-x}^{N-r}}{C_n^N}\]
\[E(x)=n \frac {r}{N} \qquad var(x)= n \frac {r}{N} \left(1- \frac {r}{N}\right) \left(\frac {N-n}{N-1}\right)\]
where \(n\) is the sample size, \(x\) is the number of successes in the
sample, \(r\) is the number of successes in the population, and \(N\) is
the number of elements in the population.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Picture a box of 12 electric fuses from the manufacturer Ontario
Electric. Five fuses are known to be defective. An inspector randomly
picks 3 fuses to test. What is the probability that exactly one of the
three chosen is defective?

We can use the hypergeometric probability mass function to solve this
problem. From the problem we know that \(N=12\), \(n=3\), \(r=5\), and
\(x=1\). Substituting these values into the PMF yields:

\[f(1)=\frac {C_1^5 C_{4}^{7}}{C_3^{12}}=0.4772\]

There is a 47.72\% chance that the inspector finds a defective fuse in
his sample of three.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Poisson Distribution}\label{poisson-distribution}

The \textbf{Poisson distribution} estimates the number of successes
(\(x\)) over a specified interval of time or space.

\begin{bsformula}[THE POISSON DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)= \frac {\mu^{x} e^{-\mu}}{x!}\] where \(\mu\) is the expected
number of successes in any given interval (and also the variance), and
\(e\) is Euler's number (\(2.71828...\)).

\end{bsformula}

An experiment satisfies a Poisson process if:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The number of successes with a specified time or space interval equals
  any integer between zero and infinity.
\item
  The number of successes counted in non-overlapping intervals are
  independent.
\item
  The probability of success in any interval is the same for all
  intervals of equal size and is proportional to the size of the
  interval.
\end{itemize}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Imagine a fast-food restaurant's drive-thru where, on average, 10 cars
arrive in a 15-minute span. What's the likelihood of exactly 5 cars
showing up in 15 minutes?

We can use the Poisson PMF function for this problem. The arrival rate
is 10 cars every 15 minutes. So there are 10 successes in the 15 minute
interval. We can substitute values into the PMF:

\[f(5)= \frac {10^{5} e^{-10}}{5!}=0.0378\] There is a 3.78\% chance of
five cars arriving within a 15 minute interval.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Discrete Distributions in
Excel}\label{discrete-distributions-in-excel}

Excel has built-in functions for the binomial, Poisson, and
hypergeometric distributions. The discrete uniform distribution does not
have a dedicated function, but its probabilities are easy to compute by
hand.

\textbf{Probability mass function (PMF).} Set the last (cumulative)
argument to \texttt{FALSE} to get the probability of an exact value.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Binomial: \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(x,\ n,\ p,\ FALSE)}. For the basketball
  example, \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(3,\ 3,\ 0.9,\ FALSE)} → \(0.729\).
\item
  Poisson: \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(x,\ mean,\ FALSE)}. For the drive-thru
  example, \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(5,\ 10,\ FALSE)} → \(0.0378\).
\item
  Hypergeometric: \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(x,\ n,\ r,\ N,\ FALSE)}, where
  \texttt{x} is the number of successes in the sample, \texttt{n} is the
  sample size, \texttt{r} is the number of successes in the population,
  and \texttt{N} is the population size. For the fuse example,
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(1,\ 3,\ 5,\ 12,\ FALSE)} → \(0.4772\).
\item
  Discrete uniform: the probability of any single value is
  \texttt{=1/n}. For the die, \texttt{=1/6} → \(0.167\).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Cumulative probabilities.} Set the last argument to
\texttt{TRUE} to get \(P(X \le x)\).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Die \(P(X \le 3)\): for the discrete uniform the cumulative
  probability is \((x-a+1)/n\), so \texttt{=(3-1+1)/6} → \(0.5\).
\item
  Basketball \(P(\text{at least 2 shots}) = 1 - P(X \le 1)\):
  \texttt{=1\ -\ BINOM.DIST(1,\ 3,\ 0.9,\ TRUE)} → \(0.972\).
\item
  For any upper-tail probability, subtract the cumulative from \(1\).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Quantiles.} For the binomial, \texttt{=BINOM.INV(n,\ p,\ alpha)}
returns the smallest value \(x\) such that \(P(X \le x) \ge alpha\).
Excel has no inverse function for the Poisson, hypergeometric, or
discrete uniform distributions; for those, build a column of cumulative
probabilities and read off the smallest value whose cumulative
probability reaches the desired percentile.

\textbf{Generating random numbers.} Use the Analysis ToolPak:
\texttt{Data\ →\ Data\ Analysis\ →\ Random\ Number\ Generation}. Choose
the distribution (for example, Binomial), enter the parameters (number
of trials and probability of success), specify how many values to
generate, and Excel fills a column with random draws. You can then
summarize them with a PivotTable or a histogram.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-6}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(x,\ n,\ p,\ cumulative)} returns the binomial
  probability. Use \texttt{FALSE} for the PMF and \texttt{TRUE} for the
  cumulative distribution.
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.INV(n,\ p,\ alpha)} returns the smallest value of \(x\)
  for which the cumulative binomial probability is at least
  \texttt{alpha}.
\item
  \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(x,\ mean,\ cumulative)} returns the Poisson
  probability (\texttt{FALSE} for the PMF, \texttt{TRUE} for the
  cumulative distribution).
\item
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(sample\_s,\ number\_sample,\ population\_s,\ number\_pop,\ cumulative)}
  returns the hypergeometric probability.
\item
  \textbf{Data Analysis ToolPak → Random Number Generation} draws random
  values from a chosen distribution.
\end{itemize}
