\chapter{Probability III}\label{probability-iii}

Studying continuous probability distributions is crucial for
understanding and predicting outcomes in uncertain scenarios where
variables can take on any value within a range, rather than being
limited to distinct, countable outcomes. These distributions are widely
used in fields such as engineering, economics, natural sciences, and
machine learning to model phenomena like time to failure, stock prices,
rainfall amounts, or human heights. By mastering continuous probability
distributions, analysts and decision-makers can assess uncertainties,
estimate probabilities of events, and develop data-driven strategies to
address real-world challenges. Below, we introduce some popular
continuous distributions and their practical applications. The normal
distribution in particular is the workhorse that connects probability to
inference: it describes how sample means behave, and so becomes the
foundation for the estimates and tests developed in the chapters that
follow.

\section{Continuous Random Variables}\label{continuous-random-variables}

Continuous random variables are characterized by their probability
density function \(f(x)\). The probability density function does not
directly provide probabilities!

The probability of a continuous random variable assuming a single value
is zero. Instead, probabilities are defined for intervals. These are
calculated by areas under the PDF curve (integral).

\section{Uniform Distribution}\label{uniform-distribution}

The \textbf{uniform} distribution assigns equal density across an
interval \([a,b]\):

\begin{bsformula}[THE UNIFORM DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)= \frac {1}{b-a} \quad (a \leq x \leq b)\]
\[E(x)= \frac {a+b}{2} \qquad var(x)= \frac {(b-a)^2}{12}\] where \(a\)
is the lower limit and \(b\) is the upper limit; \(f(x)=0\) outside
\([a,b]\).

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider the travel time between NY and CHI. Typically, this trips can
take anywhere from 120-140 minutes. The specific time of a particular
flight is unpredictable, but assumed to be anywhere between this
interval. We assume a uniform distribution with upper limit of 140 and
lower limit of 120. The probability that the flight take 122 minutes is
zero since the interval has an infinite amount of possible values. The
probability of the flight taking 130 minutes or less is 0.5.
\[f(x<=130)=\frac{130-120}{20}=0.5\] The uniform distribution has no
dedicated Excel function, but its cumulative probability follows
directly from the formula. In Excel, \texttt{=(130-120)/(140-120)}
returns \(0.5\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Normal Distribution}\label{normal-distribution}

The \textbf{normal} PDF is given by

\begin{bsformula}[THE NORMAL DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)= \frac {1}{\sigma \sqrt{2\pi}} e^{\frac {-1}{2} (\frac {x-\mu}{\sigma})^2}\]
where \(\mu\) is the mean, \(\sigma\) is the standard deviation,
\(\pi \approx 3.1416\), and \(e \approx 2.7183\).

\end{bsformula}

The normal distribution has the following properties:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  It is symmetrical about the mean \(\mu\).
\item
  The mean is at the middle and divides the area of the distribution
  into halves.
\item
  The total area under the curve is equal to 1.
\item
  The distribution is completely determined by its mean and standard
  deviation.
\end{itemize}

The \textbf{standard normal} has a mean of \(0\) and a standard
deviation of \(1\). Otherwise, it has the exact same properties as the
normal distribution.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Dr Tires is planning on offering a mileage guarantee on each set of
tires they sell. They are considering a 40,000 mile guarantee. Given
that the mean tire mileage is 36,500 miles with a standard deviation of
5,000 miles, we can estimate that the probability that a tire lasts more
than 40,000 miles is 24.2\%. In Excel, use
\texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.DIST(40000,\ 36500,\ 5000,\ TRUE)}, which returns
\(0.242\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Exponential Distribution}\label{exponential-distribution}

The \textbf{exponential distribution} is useful in computing
probabilities for the time it takes to complete a task. It describes the
time between events in a Poisson process.

The probability density function is given by:

\begin{bsformula}[THE EXPONENTIAL DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)=\frac{1}{\mu} e^{-x/\mu}=\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\] where \(\mu\)
is the mean and \(\lambda = 1/\mu\) is the rate.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Let \(x\) represent the loading time for a truck at the Dock Dash
loading dock. If the average loading time is 15 minutes, the probability
that loading a truck will take 6 minutes or less is 32.97\%. In Excel,
the exponential functions use the rate \(\lambda = 1/\mu = 1/15\):
\texttt{=EXPON.DIST(6,\ 1/15,\ TRUE)} returns \(0.3297\).

We can also estimate that the probability that it takes between 6 and 18
minutes is 36.91\%:
\texttt{=EXPON.DIST(18,\ 1/15,\ TRUE)\ -\ EXPON.DIST(6,\ 1/15,\ TRUE)}
returns \(0.3691\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Triangular Distribution}\label{triangular-distribution}

The \textbf{triangular distribution} is characterized by a single mode
(the peak of the distribution) and two boundaries. It is often used in
situations where the lower and upper bounds of a potential outcome are
known, but the exact likelihood of the outcome is uncertain.

The probability density function, expected value, and variance are:

\begin{bsformula}[THE TRIANGULAR DISTRIBUTION]

\[f(x)=\begin{cases} \dfrac{2(x-a)}{(b-a)(c-a)} & a \leq x < c \\[6pt] \dfrac{2}{b-a} & x = c \\[6pt] \dfrac{2(b-x)}{(b-a)(b-c)} & c < x \leq b \\[6pt] 0 & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}\]
\[E(x)= \frac{a+b+c}{3} \qquad var(x) = \frac{a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-ac-bc}{18}\]
where \(a\) is the minimum, \(b\) is the maximum, and \(c\) is the mode.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Bite Bliss is planning a new store in Williamsburg. It is estimated that
the minimum weekly sales are 1000 and the maximum is 6000. They also
estimate that the most likely outcome is around 3000. The probability
that future sales will be between 2000 and 2500 is 12.5\%. Excel has no
triangular distribution function, but the cumulative probability follows
from its formula. For a value \(x\) in the lower part of the range
(\(a \le x < c\)), \(F(x)=\frac{(x-a)^2}{(b-a)(c-a)}\). In Excel:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=(2500{-}1000)\^{}2/((6000{-}1000)*(3000{-}1000)) {-} (2000{-}1000)\^{}2/((6000{-}1000)*(3000{-}1000))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This returns \(0.225 - 0.10 = 0.125\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Continuous Distributions in
Excel}\label{continuous-distributions-in-excel}

Excel provides functions for the normal and exponential distributions.
The uniform and triangular distributions do not have dedicated
functions, but their probabilities follow directly from their formulas.

\textbf{Uniform.} The density is \(f(x)=1/(b-a)\) and the cumulative
probability is \(P(X \le x)=(x-a)/(b-a)\). Both are computed with
ordinary arithmetic.

\textbf{Normal.} Use
\texttt{=NORM.DIST(x,\ mean,\ standard\_dev,\ cumulative)} --- set
\texttt{cumulative} to \texttt{TRUE} for \(P(X \le x)\) and
\texttt{FALSE} for the density. For the standard normal (\(\mu=0\),
\(\sigma=1\)) use \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(z,\ cumulative)}. To find a value
given a probability (a quantile), use
\texttt{=NORM.INV(probability,\ mean,\ standard\_dev)} or
\texttt{=NORM.S.INV(probability)}.

\textbf{Exponential.} Use \texttt{=EXPON.DIST(x,\ lambda,\ cumulative)},
where \texttt{lambda} is the rate \(\lambda = 1/\mu\). Set
\texttt{cumulative} to \texttt{TRUE} for \(P(X \le x)\) and
\texttt{FALSE} for the density.

\textbf{Triangular.} Compute the cumulative probability from its
formula: \(F(x)=\frac{(x-a)^2}{(b-a)(c-a)}\) for \(a \le x < c\), and
\(F(x)=1-\frac{(b-x)^2}{(b-a)(b-c)}\) for \(c \le x \le b\).

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-7}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(x,\ mean,\ standard\_dev,\ cumulative)} returns the
  normal probability (\texttt{TRUE} for the cumulative distribution,
  \texttt{FALSE} for the density).
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(z,\ cumulative)} returns the standard normal
  probability.
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.INV(probability,\ mean,\ standard\_dev)} and
  \texttt{=NORM.S.INV(probability)} return the value (quantile)
  corresponding to a given cumulative probability.
\item
  \texttt{=EXPON.DIST(x,\ lambda,\ cumulative)} returns the exponential
  probability, where \texttt{lambda} is the rate \(1/\mu\).
\item
  The \textbf{uniform} and \textbf{triangular} distributions have no
  built-in functions; compute their probabilities directly from the
  formulas above.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

For the following exercises, make your calculations by hand and verify
results with a calculator or Excel.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A random variable \(X\) follows a continuous uniform distribution with
  minimum of \(-2\) and maximum of \(4\). Determine the height of the
  density function \(f(x)\), the mean, the standard deviation, and
  calculate \(P(X \leq -1)\).
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans97}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Your internet provider will arrive sometime between 10:00 am and 12:00
  pm. Suppose you have to run a quick errand at 10:00 am. If it takes
  \(15\) minutes to run the errand, what is the probability that you
  will be back before the internet provider arrives? What if you take
  \(30\) minutes?
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans98}

\subsection*{Exercise 2}\label{exercise-2-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 2}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A random variable \(Z\) follows a standard normal distribution. Find
  \(P(-0.67 \leq Z \leq -0.23)\), \(P(0 \leq Z \leq 1.96)\),
  \(P(-1.28 \leq Z \leq 0)\) and \(P(Z > 4.2)\).
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans99}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Let \(Y\) be normally distributed with \(\mu=2.5\) and \(\sigma=2\).
  Find \(P(Y>7.6)\), \(P(7.4 \leq Y \leq 10.6)\), a \(y\) such that
  \(P(Y>y)=0.025\), and a \(y\) such that
  \(P(y \leq Y \leq 2.5)=0.4943\).
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans100}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Assume that football game times are normally distributed with a mean
  of \(3\) hours and a standard deviation of \(0.4\) hour. What is the
  probability that the game lasts at most \(2.5\) hours? Find the
  maximum value for a game to be in the bottom \(1\)\% of the
  distribution.
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans101}

\subsection*{Exercise 3}\label{exercise-3-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 3}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Random variable \(S\) is exponentially distributed with mean of
  \(0.1\). What is the standard deviation of \(S\)? What is
  \(P(0.10 \leq S \leq 0.2)\)?
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans102}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  A tollbooth operator has observed that cars arrive randomly at a rate
  of \(360\) cars per hour. What is the mean time between car arrivals?
  What is the probability that the next car will arrive within ten
  seconds?
\end{enumerate}

\bsanswerref{bsans103}

\part{Infer}

\chapter{Inference I}\label{inference-i}

Statistical inference is a cornerstone of modern business
decision-making. It enables companies to extract meaningful insights
from data amid uncertainty and complexity. Businesses can predict
customer behavior, optimize marketing strategies, and assess risks by
drawing reliable conclusions from sample data. In this chapter, we
explore how we can draw insights about a population using sample data.
This is the stage the whole book has been building toward: moving from
describing a sample to reasoning about the population behind it, using
probability to keep our uncertainty honest.

\section{Statistical Inference}\label{statistical-inference}

The power of statistical inference lies in allowing us to learn about a
population by studying a sample. We estimate a \textbf{sample
statistic}, such as the sample mean \(\bar{x}\), and use it as a guess
for the corresponding \textbf{population parameter}, such as the
population mean \(\mu\) --- as long as the sample is drawn randomly.
Certain properties of the sample mean make this a sound guess. Below we
study three of them: unbiasedness, consistency, and precision, and then
the shape of the sampling distribution through the Central Limit
Theorem.

\section{Unbiasedness}\label{unbiasedness}

\textbf{Unbiasedness} states that the expected value of the sample mean
equals the population mean.

\begin{bsformula}[UNBIASEDNESS]

\[E[\bar{X}] = \mu\] where \(\bar{X}\) is the sample mean and \(\mu\) is
the population mean.

\end{bsformula}

The difference between a sample mean and the population mean is called
the \textbf{sampling error}.

\begin{bsformula}[SAMPLING ERROR]

\[\text{Sampling Error} = \bar{x} - \mu\]

\end{bsformula}

Sampling error is not a mistake --- it is the natural consequence of
sampling variability. It would be zero if the sample included the entire
population, and it tends to shrink as the sample size grows.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Consider the four numbers below as the entire population:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
\(x_1\) & \(x_2\) & \(x_3\) & \(x_4\) \\
\midrule
3 & 12 & 18 & 30 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The population mean is \(\mu = \frac{3 + 12 + 18 + 30}{4} = 15.75\).

In practice we rarely see the whole population. Suppose instead we take
a random sample of three elements. The table lists all possible samples
of size three and their sample means:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
Sample & Outcome & \(\bar{x}\) \\
\midrule
1 & \{3,12,18\} & \(11\) \\
2 & \{3,12,30\} & \(15\) \\
3 & \{3,18,30\} & \(17\) \\
4 & \{12,18,30\} & \(20\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

None of the individual sample means equals \(\mu = 15.75\) --- each
carries some sampling error. Yet the \textbf{mean of the sample means}
is exactly the population mean:

\[\frac{11 + 15 + 17 + 20}{4} = \frac{63}{4} = 15.75 = \mu\]

This confirms \(E[\bar{X}] = \mu\): although any single sample mean
deviates from \(\mu\), on average the sample mean lands on the true
population mean.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Consistency}\label{consistency}

\textbf{Consistency} states that as the sample size increases, the
sample mean converges to the population mean. Larger samples produce
more accurate estimates, so the probability that the sample mean
deviates from \(\mu\) by more than any fixed amount approaches zero as
\(n\) grows.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

Using the same population \{3, 12, 18, 30\} with \(\mu = 15.75\), we
compare the average absolute sampling error for samples of size two,
three, and four.

For all samples of size two:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
Sample & Outcome & \(\bar{x}\) & \(\bar{x} - \mu\) \\
\midrule
1 & \{3,12\} & \(7.5\) & \(-8.25\) \\
2 & \{3,18\} & \(10.5\) & \(-5.25\) \\
3 & \{3,30\} & \(16.5\) & \(0.75\) \\
4 & \{12,18\} & \(15\) & \(-0.75\) \\
5 & \{12,30\} & \(21\) & \(5.25\) \\
6 & \{18,30\} & \(24\) & \(8.25\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The average absolute error for size two is
\(\frac{8.25 + 5.25 + 0.75 + 0.75 + 5.25 + 8.25}{6} = 4.75\).

For size three (means \(11, 15, 17, 20\)) the average absolute error
falls to \(\frac{4.75 + 0.75 + 1.25 + 4.25}{4} = 2.75\).

For size four (the entire population) the error is
\(\bar{x} - \mu = 15.75 - 15.75 = 0\).

As the sample size increases from two to four, the average error shrinks
from \(4.75\) to \(0\).

\end{bsexample}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, breakable, left=2mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, coltitle=black, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, opacityback=0, titlerule=0mm, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, bottomrule=.15mm]

To explore how the sampling error changes with the sample size, visit
\url{https://jagelves.shinyapps.io/Sampling/}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Precision}\label{precision}

\textbf{Precision} means that as the sample size grows, the sample mean
becomes a more tightly clustered estimate of \(\mu\). This is because
the standard deviation of the sample mean --- the \textbf{standard
error} --- is smaller than the population standard deviation.

\begin{bsformula}[THE STANDARD ERROR OF THE MEAN]

\[\sigma_{\bar{x}} = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\] where \(\sigma\) is the
population standard deviation and \(n\) is the sample size. The sample
size \(n\) inversely affects the spread: larger samples give smaller
standard errors.

\end{bsformula}

When the population is small relative to the sample, a \textbf{finite
population correction} is applied:
\(\sigma_{\bar{x}} = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\frac{N-n}{N-1}}\),
where \(N\) is the population size. Most populations are large enough
that this correction can be ignored.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

For the population \{3, 12, 18, 30\}, the population variance is
\[\sigma^2 = \frac{(3-15.75)^2 + (12-15.75)^2 + (18-15.75)^2 + (30-15.75)^2}{4} = 96.1875\]

For a sample of size \(n=3\) from this finite population of size
\(N=4\), the variance of the sample mean is
\[\frac{\sigma^2}{n}\cdot\frac{N-n}{N-1} = \frac{96.1875}{3}\cdot\frac{4-3}{4-1} = 10.6875\]

We can verify this directly from the four sample means
(\(11, 15, 17, 20\)):
\[Var(\bar{x}) = \frac{(11-15.75)^2 + (15-15.75)^2 + (17-15.75)^2 + (20-15.75)^2}{4} = 10.6875\]

The variance of the sample mean (\(10.6875\)) is far smaller than the
population variance (\(96.1875\)) --- and it shrinks further as \(n\)
grows.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Asymptotic Normality and the Central Limit
Theorem}\label{asymptotic-normality-and-the-central-limit-theorem}

To build confidence intervals or test hypotheses, we need the
\emph{shape} of the sampling distribution, not just its center and
spread. This is where the \textbf{Central Limit Theorem (CLT)} becomes
one of the most useful results in statistics. It states that, regardless
of the population's distribution (provided it has finite variance), the
standardized sample mean becomes approximately normal as \(n\) grows
large.

\begin{bsformula}[CENTRAL LIMIT THEOREM (MEAN)]

\[\frac{\bar{X} - \mu}{\sigma / \sqrt{n}} \approx N(0, 1)\]

\end{bsformula}

This lets us use the normal (\(z\)) distribution for probability
statements even when the data are skewed or discrete. In summary, for
the sample mean \(\bar{X}\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If the population is normal, the sampling distribution of \(\bar{X}\)
  is exactly normal for any \(n\).
\item
  If the population is non-normal, the sampling distribution of
  \(\bar{X}\) is approximately normal for large \(n\) (a common rule of
  thumb is \(n > 30\)).
\end{itemize}

\section{Inference for Proportions}\label{inference-for-proportions}

Inference also applies to \textbf{proportions} --- for example, the
proportion of left-handed people or of customers who churn. A proportion
is the outcome of a binomial process: the number of successes \(x\) in
\(n\) trials, where \(x/n\) is the proportion of successes. To estimate
the population proportion \(p\), we use the \textbf{sample proportion}
\(\hat{p} = x/n\), which is unbiased (\(E[\hat{p}] = p\)). Its standard
error is:

\begin{bsformula}[THE STANDARD ERROR OF THE PROPORTION]

\[se(\hat{p}) = \sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}}\] where \(p\) is the proportion
and \(n\) is the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

By the Central Limit Theorem, the sampling distribution of \(\hat{p}\)
is also approximately normal for a large enough sample.

\begin{bsformula}[CENTRAL LIMIT THEOREM (PROPORTION)]

\[\frac{\hat{p} - p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}} \approx N(0, 1)\]

\end{bsformula}

Because the underlying population is binary, the normal approximation is
only good when there are enough expected successes and failures --- the
usual rule is \(np \geq 5\) and \(n(1-p) \geq 5\) (some texts use a more
conservative threshold of \(10\)). In practice we check these conditions
using the estimated \(\hat{p}\).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, breakable, left=2mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, coltitle=black, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, opacityback=0, titlerule=0mm, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, bottomrule=.15mm]

To explore the Central Limit Theorem and how the sample size affects the
sampling distribution press
\href{https://jagelves.shinyapps.io/Central/}{here}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Sampling in Excel}\label{sampling-in-excel}

Excel can both generate random samples and compute the normal
probabilities used in inference. There is no need for a programming loop
--- you fill a block of cells with formulas, and each column can serve
as a separate sample.

\textbf{Generating random numbers.} \texttt{=RAND()} returns a random
decimal between 0 and 1, and \texttt{=RANDBETWEEN(min,\ max)} returns a
random integer. To draw from a continuous uniform distribution on
\([a, b]\), use \texttt{=a\ +\ (b\ -\ a)*RAND()}. To draw from a normal
distribution, invert a random probability with
\texttt{=NORM.INV(RAND(),\ mean,\ standard\_dev)}. In modern Excel,
\texttt{=RANDARRAY(rows,\ cols,\ min,\ max,\ FALSE)} fills an entire
block at once --- for example, a block with one column per sample.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=RANDARRAY(10, 1000, 100, 200, FALSE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This single formula creates 1000 samples of size 10, each drawn from a
uniform distribution on \([100, 200]\).

\textbf{Freezing a sample.} \texttt{RAND}, \texttt{RANDBETWEEN}, and
\texttt{RANDARRAY} recalculate every time the sheet changes (or when you
press \texttt{F9}). To lock a sample in place, copy the block and use
\texttt{Paste\ Special\ →\ Values}.

\textbf{Computing probabilities.} Use
\texttt{=NORM.DIST(x,\ mean,\ standard\_dev,\ TRUE)} for \(P(X \le x)\),
\texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.DIST(...)} for the upper tail, and the difference of
two calls for an interval. The standard error is entered directly as the
\texttt{standard\_dev} argument, for example
\texttt{=NORM.DIST(85,\ 80,\ 14/SQRT(100),\ TRUE)}.

\textbf{Summarizing samples.} Use \texttt{=AVERAGE(range)} for the mean
of a sample and \texttt{=STDEV.S(range)} for its standard deviation.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-8}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=RAND()} returns a random decimal between 0 and 1.
\item
  \texttt{=RANDBETWEEN(bottom,\ top)} returns a random integer in a
  range.
\item
  \texttt{=RANDARRAY(rows,\ cols,\ min,\ max,\ integer)} fills a block
  with random numbers --- ideal for generating many samples at once.
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.INV(probability,\ mean,\ standard\_dev)} returns a value
  from a probability; paired with \texttt{RAND()} it generates normal
  random numbers.
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(x,\ mean,\ standard\_dev,\ cumulative)} returns a
  normal probability (\texttt{TRUE} for the cumulative distribution).
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(range)} and \texttt{=STDEV.S(range)} return the mean
  and standard deviation of a sample.
\end{itemize}

The following exercises will help you test your knowledge of inference.
In particular, the exercises work on:

  The sampling distribution of the mean.

  The sampling distribution of the proportion.

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

In this exercise we will simulate the Central Limit Theorem in Excel.
The idea is to generate many samples of random numbers, where
\textbf{each column is one sample}, and then study the distribution of
the sample means.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}

  Using the uniform distribution with a minimum of \(100\) and a maximum

  \(10\) rows and \(1000\) columns with random numbers so that each
  column is one sample. What are the mean and standard deviation of this
  population?

\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}

\chapter{Inference II}\label{inference-ii}

A single number is rarely the whole story. When a business estimates
average customer spending or the proportion of defective units from a
sample, the estimate is almost never exactly right --- it carries
sampling error. Reporting a lone \textbf{point estimate} hides this
uncertainty. Instead, we surround the estimate with a range of plausible
values and attach a level of confidence to it. This chapter shows how to
build these \textbf{confidence intervals} for means and proportions, how
the sample size and confidence level affect their width, and how to
construct them in Excel. A confidence interval is the natural next step
in learning from data: having accepted that a single sample gives only
an imperfect view of the world, we report a range of plausible values
for the population instead of pretending our estimate is exact.

\section{Confidence Intervals}\label{confidence-intervals}

A \textbf{confidence interval (CI)} is a range of plausible values for a
population parameter, together with a \textbf{confidence level} that
states how often the procedure captures the true parameter. A 95\%
confidence interval means that if we repeated the sampling process many
times, about 95\% of the intervals produced would contain the true
population parameter.

The confidence level and the \textbf{significance level} \(\alpha\) are
two sides of the same coin: a 95\% confidence level corresponds to
\(\alpha = 0.05\), the risk that the interval misses the parameter.
Every confidence interval has the same shape --- a point estimate plus
or minus a \textbf{margin of error}:

\begin{bsformula}[THE CONFIDENCE INTERVAL]

\[\text{point estimate} \pm \text{margin of error}\] The margin of error
is a \textbf{critical value} (from the normal or \(t\) distribution)
multiplied by the \textbf{standard error} of the estimate.

\end{bsformula}

Two forces control the width of the interval. A \textbf{higher
confidence level} widens it (more certainty requires a bigger net), and
a \textbf{larger sample} narrows it (more data gives a more precise
estimate).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, breakable, left=2mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, coltitle=black, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, opacityback=0, titlerule=0mm, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, bottomrule=.15mm]

A confidence interval describes the \emph{procedure}, not a single
interval. Once computed, a specific interval either contains the
parameter or it does not --- the 95\% refers to how often the method
succeeds over many samples.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Confidence Intervals for the
Mean}\label{confidence-intervals-for-the-mean}

When the population standard deviation \(\sigma\) is known and the
sampling distribution is normal (by the Central Limit Theorem), the
confidence interval for the mean uses a \(z\) critical value:

\begin{bsformula}[CONFIDENCE INTERVAL FOR THE MEAN (]

\(\sigma\) known)
\[\bar{x} \pm z_{\alpha/2} \cdot \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\] where
\(\bar{x}\) is the sample mean, \(z_{\alpha/2}\) is the normal critical
value (e.g., \(1.96\) for 95\%), \(\sigma\) is the population standard
deviation, and \(n\) is the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

A sample of \(n = 50\) gives an average life expectancy of
\(\bar{x} = 78.1\) years, and the population standard deviation is
\(\sigma = 4.5\). For a 90\% confidence interval,
\(z_{0.05} \approx 1.645\). The standard error is
\[\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{4.5}{\sqrt{50}} \approx 0.637\] so the
interval is
\[78.1 \pm 1.645 \times 0.637 \approx 78.1 \pm 1.05 = [77.05,\ 79.15]\]
We are 90\% confident that the true mean life expectancy is between
\(77.05\) and \(79.15\) years.

\end{bsexample}

\section{The t-Distribution}\label{the-t-distribution}

In practice we almost never know the population standard deviation
\(\sigma\). When we estimate it with the sample standard deviation
\(s\), we add a second source of uncertainty: not only is \(\bar{x}\) a
noisy estimate of the mean, but \(s\) is a noisy estimate of the spread.
Pretending \(s\) equals the true \(\sigma\) would make our intervals too
narrow and overstate our confidence. The \(t\)-distribution corrects for
this by accounting for the extra variability that comes from estimating
\(\sigma\) from the same small sample.

\textbf{Properties of the} \(t\)\textbf{-distribution.} The
\(t\)-distribution is bell-shaped and symmetric around zero, just like
the standard normal, but it differs in three important ways. It is
\emph{flatter in the middle and heavier in the tails}, which means
extreme values are more likely and, in turn, the critical values are
larger --- this is what makes a \(t\)-based interval wider than a
\(z\)-based one. It is a \emph{family} of curves rather than a single
fixed shape: each is indexed by its degrees of freedom. And it is
\emph{asymptotically normal} --- as the degrees of freedom increase, the
tails thin out and the curve becomes almost indistinguishable from the
standard normal. In practice, by about \(n = 30\) the two are close
enough that the choice rarely matters.

\textbf{Why degrees of freedom?} The degrees of freedom count the number
of independent pieces of information available to estimate variability.
To compute the sample standard deviation \(s\), we first need the sample
mean \(\bar{x}\). Once \(\bar{x}\) is fixed, the deviations from it must
sum to zero, so the last deviation is completely determined by the other
\(n - 1\): only \(n - 1\) of them are free to vary. We therefore
``spend'' one degree of freedom estimating the mean, leaving \(n - 1\)
to estimate the spread. With fewer degrees of freedom (a small sample),
\(s\) is a shakier estimate of \(\sigma\), so the \(t\)-distribution has
fatter tails to compensate; with more degrees of freedom, \(s\) becomes
reliable and the distribution tightens toward the normal.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, breakable, left=2mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, coltitle=black, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, opacityback=0, titlerule=0mm, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, leftrule=.75mm, bottomrule=.15mm]

Think of the degrees of freedom as a measure of how much the sample can
be trusted to pin down the spread. A sample of \(n = 5\) (\(4\) degrees
of freedom) leaves a lot of room for \(s\) to be off, so the interval
must be wide; a sample of \(n = 100\) (\(99\) degrees of freedom)
estimates \(\sigma\) well, and the \(t\) critical value is barely larger
than \(z\).

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{bsformula}[CONFIDENCE INTERVAL FOR THE MEAN (]

\(\sigma\) unknown)
\[\bar{x} \pm t_{\alpha/2} \cdot \frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\] where \(s\) is the
sample standard deviation and \(t_{\alpha/2}\) is the \(t\) critical
value with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

A sample of \(n = 20\) customer wait times has a mean of
\(\bar{x} = 8.4\) minutes and a sample standard deviation of
\(s = 2.1\). For a 95\% confidence interval with \(n - 1 = 19\) degrees
of freedom, \(t_{0.025} \approx 2.093\). The margin of error is
\[2.093 \times \frac{2.1}{\sqrt{20}} \approx 0.98\] so the interval is
\(8.4 \pm 0.98 = [7.42,\ 9.38]\) minutes. Because we estimated
\(\sigma\) from the sample, the \(t\) critical value (\(2.093\)) is
slightly larger than the corresponding \(z\) value (\(1.96\)), making
the interval a little wider.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Confidence Intervals for a
Proportion}\label{confidence-intervals-for-a-proportion}

For a population proportion \(p\), the sample proportion
\(\hat{p} = x/n\) is approximately normal by the Central Limit Theorem,
so the interval uses a \(z\) critical value:

\begin{bsformula}[CONFIDENCE INTERVAL FOR A PROPORTION]

\[\hat{p} \pm z_{\alpha/2} \cdot \sqrt{\frac{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}{n}}\]
where \(\hat{p}\) is the sample proportion and \(n\) is the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

A random sample of \(100\) yields \(40\) successes, so
\(\hat{p} = 0.4\). For a 90\% confidence interval,
\(z_{0.05} \approx 1.645\). The standard error is
\[\sqrt{\frac{0.4 \times 0.6}{100}} \approx 0.049\] so the interval is
\[0.4 \pm 1.645 \times 0.049 \approx 0.4 \pm 0.081 = [0.319,\ 0.481]\]
We are 90\% confident that the true population proportion is between
\(31.9\%\) and \(48.1\%\).

\end{bsexample}

\section{Determining the Sample Size}\label{determining-the-sample-size}

Because the margin of error shrinks as \(n\) grows, we can turn the
question around: how large a sample do we need to achieve a desired
margin of error \(E\)? Solving the margin-of-error expression for \(n\)
gives:

\begin{bsformula}[REQUIRED SAMPLE SIZE]

\[\text{Mean:} \quad n = \left(\frac{z_{\alpha/2}\,\sigma}{E}\right)^2 \qquad\qquad \text{Proportion:} \quad n = \hat{p}(1-\hat{p})\left(\frac{z_{\alpha/2}}{E}\right)^2\]
Always round \(n\) \textbf{up} to the next whole number. If \(\hat{p}\)
is unknown, use \(\hat{p} = 0.5\) for the most conservative (largest)
sample size.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

To estimate a mean within \(E = 2\) units at 95\% confidence when
\(\sigma = 10\):
\[n = \left(\frac{1.96 \times 10}{2}\right)^2 = (9.8)^2 = 96.04 \rightarrow 97\]
A sample of \(97\) observations is needed.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Confidence Intervals in
Excel}\label{confidence-intervals-in-excel}

Excel can produce the margin of error directly, so a confidence interval
is just the sample mean plus or minus that margin.

\textbf{Critical values.} For a \(z\) critical value use
\texttt{=NORM.S.INV(1\ -\ alpha/2)} --- for example,
\texttt{=NORM.S.INV(0.975)} returns \(1.96\). For a \(t\) critical value
use \texttt{=T.INV.2T(alpha,\ deg\_freedom)}, which returns the positive
two-tailed value directly --- for example, \texttt{=T.INV.2T(0.05,\ 19)}
returns \(2.093\).

\textbf{Margin of error for a mean.} Excel has two dedicated functions
that return the margin of error in one step. Use
\texttt{=CONFIDENCE.NORM(alpha,\ standard\_dev,\ size)} when \(\sigma\)
is known, and \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.T(alpha,\ standard\_dev,\ size)} when
it is estimated by \(s\). The \texttt{alpha} argument is the
significance level (\texttt{0.05} for 95\%).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=CONFIDENCE.T(0.05, 2.1, 20)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This returns \(0.98\), the margin of error for the wait-time example.
The interval is then \texttt{=8.4\ -\ 0.98} and \texttt{=8.4\ +\ 0.98}.

\textbf{Margin of error for a proportion.} There is no dedicated
function, so build it from the standard error:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=NORM.S.INV(1 {-} 0.05/2) * SQRT(0.4*(1{-}0.4)/100)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This returns \(0.096\), the 95\% margin of error for the proportion
example.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-9}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.NORM(alpha,\ standard\_dev,\ size)} returns the
  margin of error for a mean using the normal distribution (when
  \(\sigma\) is known).
\item
  \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.T(alpha,\ standard\_dev,\ size)} returns the
  margin of error for a mean using the \(t\)-distribution (when
  \(\sigma\) is unknown).
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.INV(probability)} returns a \(z\) critical value;
  \texttt{=T.INV.2T(probability,\ deg\_freedom)} returns a two-tailed
  \(t\) critical value.
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(range)} and \texttt{=STDEV.S(range)} return the
  sample mean and sample standard deviation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-11}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

In this exercise you will simulate confidence intervals in Excel. The
idea is to draw many samples, build a confidence interval around each
one, and see how often the interval captures the population mean.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Using the exponential distribution with a rate of \(0.02\), generate
  \(10{,}000\) samples of size \(50\). Arrange the data so that
  \textbf{each row is one sample}. What are the population mean and
  standard deviation?

  \bsanswerref{bsans113}
\item
  Compute the mean of each sample (each row). What are the mean and
  standard deviation of the \(10{,}000\) sample means?

  \bsanswerref{bsans114}
\item
  Using the standard error from part 2, construct a \(90\%\) confidence
  interval around the first sample mean. Does it include the population
  mean of \(50\)?

  \bsanswerref{bsans115}
\item
  Build a \(90\%\) confidence interval around every sample mean and
  count how many include the population mean. About how many of the
  \(10{,}000\) intervals do you expect to succeed?

  \bsanswerref{bsans116}
\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Inference III}\label{inference-iii}

Business decisions are often framed as claims to be tested: a new
supplier promises fewer defects, a marketing team believes a campaign
lifted sales, an analyst suspects average spending has changed.
\textbf{Hypothesis testing} is the formal procedure for deciding whether
the evidence in a sample is strong enough to support such a claim, or
whether the difference we see could just be sampling error. In this
chapter you will learn to state null and alternative hypotheses, compute
a test statistic, weigh it against a chosen level of risk, and reach a
defensible conclusion --- all in Excel. This is where the cycle closes:
we began by wanting to learn something about the world, and hypothesis
testing is the formal step of asking whether the data we collected
actually support the idea we started with.

\section{Hypothesis Testing
Intuition}\label{hypothesis-testing-intuition}

The \textbf{null hypothesis} (\(H_0\)) is a statement about a population
parameter --- usually the status quo, or ``no effect.'' It always
contains some form of the equality sign (\(=\), \(\geq\), or \(\leq\)).
The \textbf{alternative hypothesis} (\(H_a\)) directly contradicts the
null and states the effect we are looking for, using a strict inequality
(\(\ne\), \(>\), or \(<\)).

Suppose we hypothesize that the average annual income in the U.S. is
\$50,000. When we take a large sample, the sample mean behaves like a
normal random variable (by the Central Limit Theorem) centered at the
hypothesized value. Our job is to decide whether the observed sample
mean is ``close enough'' to \$50,000 to have plausibly come from that
distribution, or ``far enough'' that we should doubt the null and favor
the alternative.

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

We test whether the average income is \$50,000. A sample of \(100\)
people has a mean of \$48,000. Is \$48,000 close to \$50,000, or far? It
depends entirely on the \textbf{standard error} of the sampling
distribution:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If the standard error were small (say \$1,000), then \$48,000 is two
  standard errors below \$50,000 --- strong evidence \emph{against} the
  null.
\item
  If the standard error were large (say \$15,000), then \$48,000 is only
  a fraction of a standard error away --- perfectly \emph{consistent}
  with the null.
\end{itemize}

Distance alone tells us nothing; distance measured in standard errors is
what matters.

\end{bsexample}

We capture that idea with the \textbf{test statistic}, which measures
how far the sample mean sits from the hypothesized value in
standard-error units.

\begin{bsformula}[TEST STATISTIC FOR A MEAN]

\[t = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s / \sqrt{n}}\] with degrees of freedom
\(df = n - 1\), where \(\bar{x}\) is the sample mean, \(\mu_0\) is the
hypothesized mean, \(s\) is the sample standard deviation, and \(n\) is
the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

The larger the test statistic in absolute value, the stronger the
evidence against the null hypothesis.

\section{The Null and Alternative
Hypotheses}\label{the-null-and-alternative-hypotheses}

Every test starts by writing the two competing hypotheses. The direction
of the alternative determines whether the test is one-tailed or
two-tailed.

\textbf{For a mean:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(H_0: \mu \leq \mu_0\) vs.~\(H_a: \mu > \mu_0\) --- right-tailed test
\item
  \(H_0: \mu \geq \mu_0\) vs.~\(H_a: \mu < \mu_0\) --- left-tailed test
\item
  \(H_0: \mu = \mu_0\) vs.~\(H_a: \mu \ne \mu_0\) --- two-tailed test
\end{itemize}

\textbf{For a proportion:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(H_0: p \leq p_0\) vs.~\(H_a: p > p_0\) --- right-tailed test
\item
  \(H_0: p \geq p_0\) vs.~\(H_a: p < p_0\) --- left-tailed test
\item
  \(H_0: p = p_0\) vs.~\(H_a: p \ne p_0\) --- two-tailed test
\end{itemize}

\section{Two Approaches to a
Decision}\label{two-approaches-to-a-decision}

There are two equivalent ways to decide whether the observed difference
is too large to be explained by sampling error. Both always reach the
same conclusion at the same significance level \(\alpha\).

\textbf{The critical value (rejection region) approach.} Choose a
significance level \(\alpha\), look up the \textbf{critical value} from
the \(t\) or \(z\) distribution for that \(\alpha\) and the direction of
the test, and reject \(H_0\) if the test statistic falls beyond it. For
a two-tailed test at \(\alpha = 0.05\) with \(df = 99\), the critical
values are approximately \(\pm 1.984\); a test statistic more extreme
than these lands in the outer \(5\%\) of the distribution.

\textbf{The p-value approach.} Compute the \textbf{p-value} --- the
probability, assuming the null is true, of observing a test statistic at
least as extreme as the one obtained. Then apply the decision rule:
reject \(H_0\) if \(\text{p-value} \leq \alpha\), and fail to reject it
otherwise. A small p-value (say \(0.003\)) means the data would be very
unlikely if the null were true, so it is evidence \emph{against}
\(H_0\); a large p-value (say \(0.42\)) means the data are consistent
with \(H_0\).

\section{Type I and Type II Errors}\label{type-i-and-type-ii-errors}

Because a decision is made from a sample, it can be wrong in two ways.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
 & \(H_0\) is true & \(H_0\) is false \\
\midrule
\textbf{Reject} \(H_0\) & Type I error (\(\alpha\)) & Correct decision (power) \\
\textbf{Fail to reject} \(H_0\) & Correct decision & Type II error (\(\beta\)) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

A \textbf{Type I error} is rejecting a true null hypothesis (a false
positive); its probability is the significance level \(\alpha\). A
\textbf{Type II error} is failing to reject a false null hypothesis (a
false negative); its probability is \(\beta\). The \textbf{power} of a
test, \(1 - \beta\), is the chance of correctly detecting a real effect.
Lowering \(\alpha\) guards against false positives but makes false
negatives more likely, so the choice of \(\alpha\) balances the two
risks.

\section{Steps to Perform a Hypothesis
Test}\label{steps-to-perform-a-hypothesis-test}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{State the hypotheses.} Write \(H_0\) and \(H_a\) using the
  forms above.
\item
  \textbf{Choose the significance level} \(\alpha\). Common choices are
  \(0.10\), \(0.05\), and \(0.01\), corresponding to confidence levels
  of \(90\%\), \(95\%\), and \(99\%\).
\item
  \textbf{Compute the test statistic.} For a mean, use the \(t\)
  statistic given above. For a proportion, use:

  \begin{bsformula}[TEST STATISTIC FOR A PROPORTION]

  \[z = \frac{\hat{p} - p_0}{\sqrt{p_0(1 - p_0)/n}}\] where \(\hat{p}\)
  is the sample proportion, \(p_0\) is the hypothesized proportion, and
  \(n\) is the sample size. Note that the hypothesized \(p_0\) is used
  in the standard error.

  \end{bsformula}
\item
  \textbf{Find the p-value} (replacing \(t\) with \(z\) for
  proportions):

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Right-tailed test: \(\text{p-value} = P(T \geq t)\)
  \item
    Left-tailed test: \(\text{p-value} = P(T \leq t)\)
  \item
    Two-tailed test: \(\text{p-value} = 2 \times P(T \geq |t|)\)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Decide.} Reject \(H_0\) if \(\text{p-value} < \alpha\);
  otherwise fail to reject it. Remember that failing to reject \(H_0\)
  is \emph{not} proof that \(H_0\) is true --- it only means the sample
  did not provide enough evidence against it.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Hypothesis Testing in Excel}\label{hypothesis-testing-in-excel}

Excel does not have a one-click one-sample test, but every piece is a
single formula. First build the test statistic from the sample, then
convert it to a p-value.

\textbf{The test statistic.} With the data in a range, the \(t\)
statistic for a mean is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=(AVERAGE(A2:A21) {-} 100) / (STDEV.S(A2:A21) / SQRT(COUNT(A2:A21)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{p-values from the} \(t\)-distribution. Use
\texttt{=T.DIST(t,\ df,\ TRUE)} for a left-tailed test,
\texttt{=T.DIST.RT(t,\ df)} for a right-tailed test, and
\texttt{=T.DIST.2T(ABS(t),\ df)} for a two-tailed test. For the critical
value use \texttt{=T.INV.2T(alpha,\ df)} (two-tailed) or
\texttt{=T.INV(alpha,\ df)} (one-tailed).

\textbf{p-values for a proportion.} The proportion test statistic is a
\(z\) value, so use the standard normal: \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(z,\ TRUE)}
for a left-tailed test, \texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(z,\ TRUE)} for a
right-tailed test, and \texttt{=2*(1\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(ABS(z),\ TRUE))}
for a two-tailed test.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-10}

Below is a list of the Excel functions used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST(x,\ deg\_freedom,\ TRUE)} returns the left-tail
  probability of the \(t\)-distribution.
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST.RT(x,\ deg\_freedom)} returns the right-tail
  probability, and \texttt{=T.DIST.2T(x,\ deg\_freedom)} returns the
  two-tailed probability (with a positive \texttt{x}).
\item
  \texttt{=T.INV.2T(probability,\ deg\_freedom)} returns the two-tailed
  \(t\) critical value.
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(z,\ TRUE)} returns the standard normal cumulative
  probability, used for proportion tests.
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE}, \texttt{=STDEV.S}, \texttt{=COUNT}, and
  \texttt{=COUNTIF} build the sample mean, standard deviation, size, and
  counts that go into the test statistic.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-12}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Consider the following hypothesis: \(H_{0}: \mu = 50\),
  \(H_{a}: \mu \neq 50\). A sample of \(16\) observations yields a mean
  of \(46\) and a standard deviation of \(10\). Calculate the test
  statistic. At a \(5\%\) significance level, does the population mean
  differ from \(50\)?

  \bsanswerref{bsans122}
\item
  Consider the following hypothesis: \(H_{0}: \mu \geq 100\),
  \(H_{a}: \mu < 100\). A sample from a normally distributed population
  yields the values in the table below. Test the hypothesis at a \(1\%\)
  significance level.

  \begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{cccccc}
  \toprule
  96 & 102 & 93 & 87 & 92 & 82 \\
  \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}

  \bsanswerref{bsans123}
\item
  Consider the following hypothesis: \(H_{0}: \mu \leq 210\),
  \(H_{a}: \mu > 210\). A sample from a normally distributed population
  yields the values in the table below. Test the hypothesis at a
  \(10\%\) significance level.

  \begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{cccccccccc}
  \toprule
  210 & 220 & 299 & 220 & 290 & 280 & 233 & 221 & 292 & 299 \\
  \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}

  \bsanswerref{bsans124}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Exercise 3}\label{exercise-3-11}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 3}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  To test whether the population proportion differs from \(0.4\), a
  scientist draws a random sample of \(100\) observations and obtains a
  sample proportion of \(0.48\). State the competing hypotheses. At a
  \(5\%\) significance level, does the population proportion differ from
  \(0.4\)?

  \bsanswerref{bsans126}
\item
  A sample of \(320\) observations yields \(128\) successes. Test
  \(H_{0}: p \geq 0.45\) against \(H_{a}: p < 0.45\) at a \(5\%\)
  significance level.

  \bsanswerref{bsans127}
\item
  Determine whether more than \(50\%\) of the observations in a
  population are below \(10\), using the sample data below. Conduct the
  test at a \(1\%\) significance level.

  \begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{cccccccccc}
  \toprule
  8 & 12 & 5 & 9 & 14 & 11 & 9 & 3 & 7 & 12 \\
  \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}

  \bsanswerref{bsans128}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Exercise 4}\label{exercise-4-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 4}

According to \href{https://www.worldatlas.com}{worldatlas.com}, \(5\%\)
of the population has hazel eyes. Use the \textbf{HairEyeColor} data set
and a two-tailed test to determine whether this claim holds for the
\(592\) surveyed students.

\bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/HairEyeColor.xlsx}{Download Data}

\bsanswerref{bsans129}

\chapter{Regression and Inference}\label{regression-and-inference}

This final chapter brings hypothesis testing and regression together.
You will learn to judge whether a correlation is real or just noise,
whether two groups truly differ, and whether a regression coefficient
carries any explanatory weight. These are the tools that turn a
descriptive relationship into a defensible conclusion --- letting you
evaluate market trends, compare performance across groups, and decide
which predictors actually matter in a model. It is a fitting close to
the book: it unites the descriptive tool of regression with the
inferential habit of asking whether a pattern is real, turning a fitted
line into a tested claim about the world.

\section{Correlation Significance}\label{correlation-significance}

A sample correlation coefficient is almost never exactly zero, even when
the two variables are unrelated. To decide whether an observed
correlation reflects a real relationship in the population, we test the
population correlation \(\rho\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(H_0: \rho \geq 0\) vs.~\(H_a: \rho < 0\) --- left-tailed
\item
  \(H_0: \rho \leq 0\) vs.~\(H_a: \rho > 0\) --- right-tailed
\item
  \(H_0: \rho = 0\) vs.~\(H_a: \rho \ne 0\) --- two-tailed
\end{itemize}

\begin{bsformula}[CORRELATION TEST STATISTIC]

\[t = \frac{r_{xy}\sqrt{n-2}}{\sqrt{1-r_{xy}^2}}\] with degrees of
freedom \(df = n - 2\), where \(r_{xy}\) is the sample correlation
coefficient and \(n\) is the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

\begin{bsexample}[Example]

A sample of \(n = 30\) shows a correlation of \(r = 0.5\) between
advertising and sales. Testing \(H_0: \rho = 0\) against
\(H_a: \rho \ne 0\):
\[t = \frac{0.5\sqrt{30-2}}{\sqrt{1-0.5^2}} = \frac{0.5 \times 5.29}{0.866} = 3.06\]
With \(df = 28\), the two-tailed p-value is about \(0.005\). Because
\(0.005 < 0.05\), we reject the null and conclude the correlation is
statistically significant.

\end{bsexample}

\section{Difference of Means Tests}\label{difference-of-means-tests}

We often want to know whether two populations have different means ---
for example, whether two divisions post different sales, or whether a
treatment changes an outcome. The test statistic depends on how the two
samples relate.

\begin{bsformula}[DIFFERENCE OF MEANS TEST STATISTICS]

\[\text{Unpaired, unequal variances:} \quad t = \frac{(\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2) - d_0}{\sqrt{\frac{s_1^2}{n_1} + \frac{s_2^2}{n_2}}}\]
\[\text{Unpaired, equal variances:} \quad t = \frac{(\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2) - d_0}{\sqrt{s_p^2\left(\frac{1}{n_1} + \frac{1}{n_2}\right)}}\]
\[\text{Paired:} \quad t = \frac{\bar{d} - d_0}{s / \sqrt{n}}\] where
\(d_0\) is the hypothesized difference, \(s_p^2\) is the pooled
variance, and \(\bar{d}\) and \(s\) are the mean and standard deviation
of the paired differences.

The degrees of freedom differ across the three tests:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cc}
\toprule
Test & Degrees of freedom \\
\midrule
Unpaired, unequal variances & \(df = \dfrac{\left(\frac{s_1^2}{n_1} + \frac{s_2^2}{n_2}\right)^2}{\frac{(s_1^2/n_1)^2}{n_1-1} + \frac{(s_2^2/n_2)^2}{n_2-1}}\)
(rounded down) \\
Unpaired, equal variances & \(df = n_1 + n_2 - 2\) \\
Paired & \(df = n - 1\), where \(n\) is the number of \textbf{pairs}, not the
number of observations \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\end{bsformula}

A \textbf{paired} test is used when the two samples are naturally linked
--- the same subjects measured twice (before and after), for instance.
An \textbf{unpaired} (independent) test is used when the two samples
come from separate groups.

\begin{bsexample}[Example (unpaired).]

A company evaluates a training program. Six employees who took the
program score a mean of \(\bar{x}_1 = 33.17\) on a productivity
assessment (with \(s_1 = 7.55\)), while six \emph{different} employees
who did not take it score a mean of \(\bar{x}_2 = 30.00\) (with
\(s_2 = 7.18\)). Testing \(H_0: \mu_1 - \mu_2 = 0\) against
\(H_a: \mu_1 - \mu_2 \ne 0\) with unequal variances:
\[t = \frac{(33.17 - 30.00) - 0}{\sqrt{\frac{7.55^2}{6} + \frac{7.18^2}{6}}} = \frac{3.17}{4.25} = 0.74\]
The Welch formula gives about \(10\) degrees of freedom, and the
two-tailed p-value is roughly \(0.47\). Because \(0.47 > 0.05\), we fail
to reject the null: the \(3.17\)-point gap is swamped by the large
variation \emph{between} employees, so we cannot conclude the program
helped.

\end{bsexample}

\begin{bsexample}[Example (paired).]

Now suppose those same six employees were each measured \emph{before and
after} the program, so every ``after'' score is matched to that person's
own ``before'' score:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
Employee & Before & After & Difference \\
\midrule
1 & 20 & 23 & 3 \\
2 & 32 & 36 & 4 \\
3 & 25 & 27 & 2 \\
4 & 40 & 43 & 3 \\
5 & 28 & 31 & 3 \\
6 & 35 & 39 & 4 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The group means are identical to the unpaired case (\(30.00\) before,
\(33.17\) after), but now we work with each person's change. The
individual gains are remarkably consistent: \(\bar{d} = 3.17\) with
\(s = 0.75\). Testing \(H_0: \mu_d = 0\) against \(H_a: \mu_d \ne 0\):
\[t = \frac{\bar{d} - 0}{s / \sqrt{n}} = \frac{3.17}{0.75 / \sqrt{6}} = 10.30\]
With \(df = n - 1 = 6 - 1 = 5\) (six \emph{pairs}, not twelve
observations) the two-tailed p-value is about \(0.0001\). Because
\(0.0001 < 0.05\), we strongly reject the null: the program clearly
raised productivity.

\end{bsexample}

The two examples use the \textbf{same numbers} yet reach opposite
conclusions, and the reason is the design. The unpaired test must carry
the large variation \emph{between} employees --- scores range from
\(20\) to \(40\) --- and that noise hides the modest improvement. The
paired test cancels that person-to-person variation by looking only at
each individual's change, leaving a small, consistent difference that
stands out sharply (\(s\) drops from about \(7.5\) to \(0.75\)).
Whenever the data are naturally matched, pairing is the more powerful
choice.

\section{Regression Inference}\label{regression-inference}

After fitting a regression, two kinds of tests tell us whether the model
has explanatory power.

\textbf{Testing a single coefficient.} Each coefficient is tested
against zero --- is this predictor related to \(y\), holding the others
fixed?

\begin{bsformula}[TEST FOR A SINGLE COEFFICIENT]

\[H_0: \beta_j = 0 \quad\text{vs.}\quad H_a: \beta_j \ne 0, \qquad t = \frac{b_j}{se(b_j)}\]
where \(b_j\) is the estimated coefficient and \(se(b_j)\) is its
standard error, with \(df = n - k - 1\).

\end{bsformula}

\textbf{Testing joint significance.} The \(F\)-test asks whether
\emph{all} the slope coefficients are zero at once --- is the model, as
a whole, better than no predictors?

\begin{bsformula}[JOINT SIGNIFICANCE (F-TEST)]

\[H_0: \beta_1 = \beta_2 = \dots = \beta_k = 0, \qquad F = \frac{SSR/k}{SSE/(n-k-1)} = \frac{MSR}{MSE}\]
where \(k\) is the number of predictors and \(n\) is the sample size.

\end{bsformula}

The ANOVA table organizes these pieces:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
\toprule
Source & df & SS & MS & F \\
\midrule
Regression & \(k\) & \(SSR\) & \(MSR = \frac{SSR}{k}\) & \(F = \frac{MSR}{MSE}\) \\
Residual & \(n-k-1\) & \(SSE\) & \(MSE = \frac{SSE}{n-k-1}\) &  \\
Total & \(n-1\) & \(SST\) &  &  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\section{Regression and Inference in
Excel}\label{regression-and-inference-in-excel}

\textbf{Correlation significance.} Compute the correlation with
\texttt{=CORREL(x\_range,\ y\_range)}, then turn it into a test
statistic and p-value:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=(r*SQRT(n{-}2)) / SQRT(1 {-} r\^{}2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

followed by \texttt{=T.DIST.2T(ABS(t),\ n-2)} for a two-tailed test (or
\texttt{=T.DIST} / \texttt{=T.DIST.RT} for one tail).

\textbf{Difference of means.} The
\texttt{=T.TEST(array1,\ array2,\ tails,\ type)} function returns the
p-value directly. The \texttt{tails} argument is \texttt{1} (one-tailed)
or \texttt{2} (two-tailed); the \texttt{type} argument is \texttt{1}
(paired), \texttt{2} (two-sample, equal variances), or \texttt{3}
(two-sample, unequal variances). For full output --- the test statistic,
critical values, and both one- and two-tailed p-values --- use the
\textbf{Data Analysis ToolPak}, which offers
\texttt{t-Test:\ Paired\ Two\ Sample\ for\ Means},
\texttt{t-Test:\ Two-Sample\ Assuming\ Equal\ Variances}, and
\texttt{t-Test:\ Two-Sample\ Assuming\ Unequal\ Variances}.

\textbf{Regression inference.} Run
\texttt{Data\ →\ Data\ Analysis\ →\ Regression}. The output reports each
coefficient with its standard error, \(t\)-statistic, and p-value (the
individual coefficient tests), the \(F\)-statistic with its
\textbf{Significance F} (the joint test), and the ANOVA table ---
everything in this chapter in a single step.

\section{Excel Function Summary}\label{excel-function-summary-11}

Below is a list of the Excel functions and tools used in this section:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(x\_range,\ y\_range)} returns the correlation
  coefficient, the starting point for a correlation test.
\item
  \texttt{=T.TEST(array1,\ array2,\ tails,\ type)} returns the p-value
  of a difference-of-means test. \texttt{type} is \texttt{1} (paired),
  \texttt{2} (equal variances), or \texttt{3} (unequal variances).
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST.2T(x,\ deg\_freedom)},
  \texttt{=T.DIST(x,\ df,\ TRUE)}, and \texttt{=T.DIST.RT(x,\ df)}
  convert a test statistic to a p-value.
\item
  \textbf{Data Analysis ToolPak → t-Test} performs the three
  difference-of-means tests with full output.
\item
  \textbf{Data Analysis ToolPak → Regression} returns coefficients,
  standard errors, \(t\)-statistics, p-values, the \(F\)-statistic,
  \(R^2\), and the ANOVA table.
\end{itemize}
