\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-13}

The following exercises will help you test your knowledge of regression
and inference. In particular, the exercises work on:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Determining the significance of a correlation.
\item
  Conducting paired and unpaired tests of means.
\item
  Determining the significance of regression coefficients, individually
  and jointly.
\item
  Making predictions from a regression model.
\end{itemize}

Try not to peek at the answers until you have formulated your own answer
and double checked your work for any mistakes.

\subsection*{Exercise 1}\label{exercise-1-13}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 1}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Consider the competing hypotheses \(H_{0}: \rho = 0\),
  \(H_{a}: \rho \neq 0\). A sample of \(25\) observations reveals a
  correlation coefficient of \(0.15\) between two variables. At a
  \(5\%\) significance level, can we reject the null hypothesis?

  \bsanswerref{bsans130}
\item
  Use the \textbf{Hitters} data set to examine the relationship between
  \emph{Hits} and \emph{Salary}. Calculate the correlation coefficient
  and test \(H_{0}: \rho = 0\) against \(H_{a}: \rho \neq 0\) at the
  \(1\%\) significance level.

  \bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/Hitters.xlsx}{Download Data}

  \bsanswerref{bsans131}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Exercise 2}\label{exercise-2-13}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 2}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Use the \textbf{Hitters} data set to investigate whether the average
  number of \emph{Hits} differs between the two leagues (American and
  National), using the \emph{NewLeague} and \emph{Hits} variables. Test
  at the \(5\%\) significance level. Is there reason to believe the
  population variances differ?

  \bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/Hitters.xlsx}{Download Data}

  \bsanswerref{bsans132}
\item
  Use the \textbf{BrainCancer} data set to test whether males have a
  higher average survival \emph{time} than females, using the \emph{sex}
  and \emph{time} variables. Test at the \(5\%\) significance level. Is
  there reason to believe the population variances differ?

  \bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/BC.xlsx}{Download Data}

  \bsanswerref{bsans133}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Exercise 3}\label{exercise-3-12}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 3}

Use the \textbf{sleep} data set. At the \(1\%\) significance level, is
there an effect of the drug on the \(10\) patients? Treat \emph{group}
\(1\) as the measurement before the drug and \emph{group} \(2\) as
after, so the two groups are the \textbf{same} patients measured twice.

\bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/sleep.xlsx}{Download Data}

\bsanswerref{bsans134}

\subsection*{Exercise 4}\label{exercise-4-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Exercise 4}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Use the \textbf{Hitters} data set to investigate the effect of
  \emph{HmRun}, \emph{RBI}, and \emph{Years} on a player's
  \emph{Salary}. Which variables are statistically different from zero?
  Are the variables jointly significant? Does the \(R^2\) suggest a good
  fit?

  \bsdownload{https://jagelves.github.io/BusinessStats/Hitters.xlsx}{Download Data}

  \bsanswerref{bsans135}
\item
  José Altuve had \(28\) home runs, \(57\) RBIs, and \(12\) years in the
  league. What salary does the model predict for him, and what is the
  \(95\%\) prediction interval? (The model predicts a 1987 salary.)

  \bsanswerref{bsans136}
\end{enumerate}

\part{Toolkit}

\chapter{Excel Basics}\label{excel-basics}

This chapter reviews the essential Excel skills used throughout the
book. The first sections walk through the Excel interface, how to
navigate a worksheet, how references and formulas work, and the built-in
functions for working with numbers, dates, text, and lookups. The
chapter then closes with a dictionary of the statistical functions used
in each chapter, so you can quickly find the right tool when you need
it. No prior experience is assumed --- if you have used Excel before,
treat the early sections as a refresher.

\section{The Excel Interface}\label{the-excel-interface}

Excel is an \textbf{electronic spreadsheet}. A spreadsheet is a grid
made up of \textbf{cells}, and each cell holds a single piece of data
--- either text or a number. A cell is identified by its \textbf{column}
(a letter) and its \textbf{row} (a number), so the cell in the first
column and first row is \texttt{A1}. The \textbf{Name Box}, at the left
end of the formula bar, always shows the reference of the currently
selected cell; you can also type a reference (or a range name) into it
and press \texttt{Enter} to jump straight to that location.

The \textbf{Home tab} collects the formatting tools you will use most
often: fonts, number formats, borders, fill colors, and alignment.
Formatting changes how a value is displayed without changing the value
itself.

What makes a spreadsheet powerful is that cells can be \emph{related to
one another} through formulas. Instead of typing a result, you write a
formula that references other cells, and Excel keeps the result up to
date as those cells change.

A handful of keyboard shortcuts will save you a great deal of time. On
Windows use \texttt{Ctrl}; on a Mac use \texttt{Cmd}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lc}
\toprule
Action & Shortcut \\
\midrule
Copy & \texttt{Ctrl/Cmd\ +\ C} \\
Paste & \texttt{Ctrl/Cmd\ +\ V} \\
Cut & \texttt{Ctrl/Cmd\ +\ X} \\
Undo & \texttt{Ctrl/Cmd\ +\ Z} \\
Redo & \texttt{Ctrl/Cmd\ +\ Y} \\
Find & \texttt{Ctrl/Cmd\ +\ F} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

To keep your work organized, you can rename a worksheet by
double-clicking its tab and typing a new name, and you can color-code a
tab by right-clicking it and choosing \texttt{Tab\ Color}. Selecting a
block of data and choosing \texttt{Home\ →\ Format\ as\ Table} (or
pressing \texttt{Ctrl\ +\ T}) turns the range into a structured
\textbf{Excel Table} with built-in filtering, banded rows, and named
columns.

\subsection*{Cells and References}\label{cells-and-references}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Cells and References}

When a formula refers to another cell, it uses a \textbf{cell
reference}. References come in three forms, and the difference matters
as soon as you copy a formula:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Relative} (\texttt{A1}) adjusts automatically when the formula
  is copied to another cell.
\item
  \textbf{Absolute} (\texttt{\$A\$1}) stays fixed when copied. The
  dollar signs lock both the column and the row.
\item
  \textbf{Mixed} (\texttt{\$A1} or \texttt{A\$1}) locks only the column
  or only the row.
\end{itemize}

For example, \texttt{=A1*2} multiplies the value in \texttt{A1} by two.
Pressing \texttt{F4} (or \texttt{Cmd\ +\ T} on a Mac) while editing a
reference cycles through the relative, absolute, and mixed forms.

\subsection*{Ranges}\label{ranges}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Ranges}

A \textbf{range} is a group of adjacent cells, written as the top-left
cell, a colon, and the bottom-right cell. For example, \texttt{A1:A10}
is a single column of ten cells, and \texttt{A1:C10} is a rectangular
block. A range is the Excel equivalent of a list of values, and it is
what most functions take as an input. You can reference an entire column
with \texttt{A:A} or an entire row with \texttt{1:1}.

\section{Entering Formulas and
Functions}\label{entering-formulas-and-functions}

Every calculation in Excel begins with an equals sign (\texttt{=}). A
\textbf{function} relates an input (its arguments) to an output. The
workflow is always the same: type \texttt{=}, name the function, read
the tooltip that Excel shows to remind you of the arguments, fill in the
arguments separated by commas, and press \texttt{Enter}. If you are
unsure how a function works, click the \textbf{\texttt{fx}} button next
to the formula bar for guided help.

The two functions you will reach for first are \texttt{SUM} and
\texttt{AVERAGE}. To total the values in cells \texttt{A2} through
\texttt{A5}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=SUM(A2:A5)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and to average them:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=AVERAGE(A2:A5)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Once a formula is written, you rarely retype it. Select the cell and
drag the small square at its bottom-right corner (the \textbf{fill
handle}) to copy the formula. Because references are relative by
default, copying \textbf{down} makes Excel adjust the row numbers, and
copying \textbf{across} makes it adjust the column letters. This is what
lets a single formula compute a whole column of results. Many functions
also accept a \textbf{criteria} argument that filters the data, such as
\texttt{=AVERAGEIF(A2:A100,\ "\textgreater{}=1",\ B2:B100)}. If a
formula cannot be evaluated, Excel returns an error such as
\texttt{\#DIV/0!}, \texttt{\#VALUE!}, \texttt{\#N/A}, or
\texttt{\#REF!}.

\subsection*{Order of Operations}\label{order-of-operations}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Order of Operations}

Excel evaluates formulas following the standard order of operations:
parentheses first, then exponents, then multiplication and division, and
finally addition and subtraction. For example, \texttt{=2\^{}3*2+2} is
evaluated as \((2^3) \times 2 + 2 = 18\), and \texttt{=2+4*5} is
evaluated as \(2 + (4 \times 5) = 22\). When in doubt, add parentheses
to make the intended order explicit --- \texttt{=(2+4)*5} returns
\(30\).

\subsection*{Array Formulas}\label{array-formulas}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Array Formulas}

A few calculations --- like a conditional variance --- must be entered
as \textbf{array formulas}, which operate on an entire range at once. In
modern Excel these ``spill'' automatically, but in older versions you
must complete them by pressing \texttt{Ctrl\ +\ Shift\ +\ Enter} instead
of just \texttt{Enter}.

\section{Navigating and Selecting}\label{navigating-and-selecting}

Moving quickly around a large worksheet is a skill worth practicing.
Turning off the grid with \texttt{View\ →\ Gridlines} gives a cleaner
canvas, especially when building a report or a chart.

The navigation shortcuts below let you move and select without the
mouse:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{Ctrl\ +\ Arrow} jumps to the edge of the current block of data
  in that direction.
\item
  \texttt{Ctrl\ +\ Shift\ +\ Arrow} \textbf{selects} everything from the
  active cell to the edge of the data --- the fastest way to grab a
  whole column of values.
\item
  \texttt{F2} (\texttt{fn\ +\ F2} on some Macs) toggles the active cell
  into edit mode so you can see and adjust the formula in place.
\item
  \texttt{Ctrl\ +\ Enter} fills every cell in a selection with the same
  formula at once.
\end{itemize}

When you want to keep headers visible while scrolling, use
\texttt{View\ →\ Freeze\ Panes}. \texttt{View\ →\ Split} divides the
window into independently scrolling panes, and you can \texttt{Hide}
rows, columns, or entire sheets you do not want to see.

\section{Range Names}\label{range-names}

Rather than referring to \texttt{\$B\$2:\$B\$100}, you can give a range
a memorable \textbf{name} and use it in formulas and charts. There are
three ways to create one: select the range and go to
\texttt{Formulas\ →\ Define\ Name}; type a name directly into the
\textbf{Name Box} and press \texttt{Enter}; or select a block that
includes labels and use \texttt{Formulas\ →\ Create\ from\ Selection} to
name each column or row from its header.

Names follow a few rules: they cannot contain spaces, they cannot look
like a cell reference (so \texttt{Q1} is not allowed as a name), and
they must begin with a letter or underscore. Use the \textbf{Name
Manager} (\texttt{Formulas\ →\ Name\ Manager}) to review, edit, or
delete existing names. A well-named range such as \texttt{Returns} makes
\texttt{=AVERAGE(Returns)} far easier to read and audit than a raw
address.

\section{Data Types}\label{data-types}

The main data types in Excel are number, text, logical, date, and error.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Number} includes integers and decimals. Numbers are
  right-aligned in a cell by default.
\item
  \textbf{Text} represents labels or strings and is left-aligned by
  default.
\item
  \textbf{Logical} values are \texttt{TRUE} or \texttt{FALSE}, usually
  produced by a comparison.
\item
  \textbf{Date and time} values are stored as serial numbers (see
  below), which is why dates can be added and subtracted.
\item
  \textbf{Error} values are the result of an invalid operation, such as
  dividing by zero.
\end{itemize}

\section{Working with Dates and Time}\label{working-with-dates-and-time}

Excel stores every date as a \textbf{serial number}: day \texttt{1} is
January 1, 1900, day \texttt{2} is January 2, 1900, and so on. The
\textbf{integer part} of the number counts the days since that starting
point, while the \textbf{decimal part} represents the time as a fraction
of a 24-hour day. So \texttt{0} corresponds to \texttt{12:00\ am},
\texttt{0.5} corresponds to \texttt{12:00\ pm}, and a value like
\texttt{44000.75} is a specific date at \texttt{6:00\ pm}.

Because dates are really numbers, the way a cell is \emph{formatted}
determines whether you see \texttt{1/1/2024} or \texttt{45292}. If a
calculation returns a date when you wanted a plain number (or vice
versa), change the cell's number format under \texttt{Home\ →\ Number}.
To pull just the \textbf{time} (the decimal part) out of a date-time
value, use \texttt{MOD} with a divisor of \(1\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=MOD(A1, 1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Useful date and time functions include \texttt{=TODAY()} and
\texttt{=NOW()} for the current date and date-time,
\texttt{=DATE(year,\ month,\ day)} to build a date, and
\texttt{=YEAR()}, \texttt{=MONTH()}, \texttt{=DAY()}, and
\texttt{=WEEKDAY()} to extract the parts of a date.

\section{Working with Text}\label{working-with-text}

Text functions let you clean and reshape labels --- for example,
splitting a ``Last, First'' name into two columns. The most useful ones
are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=TRIM(text)} removes extra spaces, leaving only single spaces
  between words.
\item
  \texttt{=LEN(text)} returns the number of characters in the text.
\item
  \texttt{=LEFT(text,\ n)} and \texttt{=RIGHT(text,\ n)} return the
  first or last \texttt{n} characters.
\item
  \texttt{=MID(text,\ start,\ n)} returns \texttt{n} characters
  beginning at position \texttt{start}.
\item
  \texttt{=SEARCH(find,\ text)} (or \texttt{=FIND}) returns the position
  of a character or substring; \texttt{SEARCH} is not case-sensitive,
  \texttt{FIND} is.
\item
  \texttt{=UPPER(text)}, \texttt{=LOWER(text)}, and
  \texttt{=PROPER(text)} change capitalization.
\item
  \texttt{=SUBSTITUTE(text,\ old,\ new)} replaces every occurrence of
  \texttt{old} with \texttt{new}.
\item
  \texttt{=REPLACE(text,\ start,\ n,\ new)} replaces \texttt{n}
  characters starting at a position with new text.
\end{itemize}

As an example, suppose cell \texttt{D6} contains \texttt{Doe,\ John}.
The position of the comma is \texttt{=SEARCH(",",\ D6)}, which returns
\texttt{4}. The last name is then
\texttt{=LEFT(D6,\ SEARCH(",",\ D6)\ -\ 1)} → \texttt{Doe}, and the
first name is \texttt{=RIGHT(D6,\ LEN(D6)\ -\ SEARCH(",",\ D6)\ -\ 1)} →
\texttt{John}. To join text back together, use the ampersand operator:
\texttt{=B6\ \&\ "\ "\ \&\ A6}, or the \texttt{=CONCAT()} function.

\section{Lookup Functions}\label{lookup-functions}

Lookup functions find a value in a table based on a key.
\texttt{=VLOOKUP(lookup\_value,\ table,\ column\_number,\ {[}match{]})}
searches down the first column of a table and returns a value from a
column to its right; \texttt{=HLOOKUP()} does the same across the first
row. The modern replacement,
\texttt{=XLOOKUP(lookup\_value,\ lookup\_range,\ return\_range)}, is
more flexible because the lookup and return ranges can be anywhere and
it can look left as well as right.

The optional match argument controls \textbf{exact} versus
\textbf{approximate} matching. An approximate (binary) search assumes
the lookup column is \textbf{sorted}: Excel repeatedly compares the
target with the middle value and discards half the list each time, which
is very fast on large data sets --- but it returns wrong answers if the
data is not sorted. For most business lookups you want an exact match.

Lookups also support \textbf{wildcards} in the search text: \texttt{?}
matches any single character (so \texttt{B?ll} matches \texttt{Ball} and
\texttt{Bell}), and \texttt{*} matches any number of characters (so
\texttt{B*ll} matches \texttt{Ball}, \texttt{Bell}, and
\texttt{Barbell}). Use \texttt{\textasciitilde{}} before a \texttt{?} or
\texttt{*} when you need to match the literal character.

\section{Logical and Conditional
Functions}\label{logical-and-conditional-functions}

The \texttt{=IF(test,\ value\_if\_true,\ value\_if\_false)} function
returns one of two values depending on whether a condition is met, and
\texttt{IF} statements can be nested inside one another. When you have
several conditions to check in order,
\texttt{=IFS(test1,\ value1,\ test2,\ value2,\ ...)} is cleaner. The
logical helpers \texttt{=AND(...)}, \texttt{=OR(...)}, and
\texttt{=NOT(...)} combine or reverse conditions.

A closely related family aggregates values that meet a condition:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COUNTIF(range,\ criteria)} counts the cells that satisfy a
  condition, and \texttt{=COUNTIFS(...)} handles several conditions at
  once.
\item
  \texttt{=SUMIF(range,\ criteria,\ sum\_range)} totals the values that
  meet a condition, and \texttt{=SUMIFS(...)} allows several conditions.
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGEIF(range,\ criteria,\ average\_range)} averages the
  values that meet a condition.
\end{itemize}

When a criterion is a comparison, write it as text in quotes, for
example \texttt{"\textgreater{}2"} or \texttt{"\textgreater{}=1"}. To
rank values, \texttt{=RANK.AVG(number,\ range)} returns the position of
a value within a list (averaging ties). A common use is to highlight the
top items in a chart: rank each value, then use a formula such as
\texttt{=IF(rank\ \textless{}=\ 5,\ value,\ NA())} so that only the top
five carry a value and the rest return \texttt{NA()}.

\section{OFFSET and MATCH}\label{offset-and-match}

\texttt{=MATCH(lookup\_value,\ array,\ 0)} returns the \textbf{position}
of a value within a row or column (the \texttt{0} requests an exact
match). It does not return the value itself --- it tells you
\emph{where} the value sits, which you can then feed to another
function.

\texttt{=OFFSET(reference,\ rows,\ cols,\ {[}height{]},\ {[}width{]})}
returns a range that is a given number of rows and columns away from a
starting \texttt{reference}. The optional \texttt{height} and
\texttt{width} arguments let it return a whole block rather than a
single cell. Combining \texttt{MATCH} (to locate a position) with
\texttt{OFFSET} (to retrieve the value at that position) creates
flexible lookups; the same idea is often written with \texttt{INDEX} and
\texttt{MATCH}.

Because \texttt{OFFSET} can size a range on the fly, it is the key to
\textbf{dynamic ranges}. Pairing it with \texttt{=COUNTA(range)} ---
which counts the non-empty cells --- lets you define a named range that
automatically grows as you add data. A chart built on that name then
updates itself whenever new rows arrive, with no manual re-selection.

\section{Sorting, Filtering, and Dynamic
Arrays}\label{sorting-filtering-and-dynamic-arrays}

The \texttt{Data\ →\ Sort} and \texttt{Data\ →\ Filter} tools reorder
and subset a table interactively. Excel also provides \textbf{dynamic
array} functions that return a whole range of results that ``spill''
into neighboring cells:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SORT(range)} returns the range sorted.
\item
  \texttt{=FILTER(range,\ condition)} returns only the rows that satisfy
  a condition.
\item
  \texttt{=RANK(number,\ range)} returns the rank of a value.
\end{itemize}

These combine naturally with the conditional functions. For example, to
keep only the years that appear in a second, larger list, you can flag
matches with \texttt{COUNTIF} and pass the result to \texttt{FILTER}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=FILTER(Years, COUNTIF(OtherYears, Years))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{COUNTIF} returns a \texttt{1} for each year found in
\texttt{OtherYears} and a \texttt{0} otherwise, and \texttt{FILTER}
keeps only the rows flagged with a \texttt{1}.

\section{Importing and Web Data}\label{importing-and-web-data}

To bring outside data into Excel you can open a CSV directly with
\texttt{File\ →\ Open}, or use
\texttt{Data\ →\ Get\ Data\ →\ From\ Text/CSV} for control over how
columns are separated. \texttt{Data\ →\ Get\ Data\ →\ From\ Web} pulls
tables from a web page --- for example, stock history from a finance
site. Imported data becomes a \textbf{query}; under
\texttt{Data\ →\ Queries\ \&\ Connections} (and
\texttt{Data\ Source\ Settings}) you can refresh it, change the source,
or adjust how it loads. Excel's \textbf{Stocks} and \textbf{Geography}
data types can also enrich a plain list of tickers or country names with
live fields. When previewing a large import, freeze the header row with
\texttt{View\ →\ Freeze\ Panes} and jump to the end with
\texttt{Ctrl\ +\ Down} or \texttt{Ctrl\ +\ End}.

\section{PivotTables}\label{pivottables}

A \textbf{PivotTable} (\texttt{Insert\ →\ PivotTable}) automatically
summarizes and counts data, making it the primary tool for building
frequency and relative frequency distributions. You drag fields into the
\textbf{Rows}, \textbf{Columns}, and \textbf{Values} areas, and Excel
aggregates the underlying data for you --- counting, summing, or
averaging --- without writing a single formula. PivotTables are used
throughout the descriptive statistics chapters; see \emph{Descriptive
Statistics II} for a worked example.

\section{Charts and Visualization}\label{charts-and-visualization}

Charts are created from \texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts}. Choosing the right
type matters: use a \textbf{bar chart} to compare categories and a
\textbf{line chart} to show change over time. A good chart maximizes the
\emph{data-to-ink ratio} --- that is, it removes anything that does not
convey information. In practice that means removing gridlines, adding
subtle borders to bars, cleaning up the axes (sensible tick marks and
units), placing the title inside the plot, and adding data labels by
right-clicking a series and choosing \texttt{Add\ Data\ Labels}.

To compare two series, add a second set of values to the chart. When the
two series are on very different scales --- a count and a percentage,
say --- plot one of them on a \textbf{secondary axis} using a
\textbf{combo chart}
(\texttt{Chart\ Design\ →\ Change\ Chart\ Type\ →\ Combo}). You can
\textbf{highlight} a particular bar, such as the maximum or the top few,
by adding a helper column that returns the value only for the rows to
highlight and \texttt{NA()} for the rest; because Excel does not plot
\texttt{NA()}, only the highlighted bars appear, and setting the series
overlap to \texttt{100\%} layers them on top of the originals. A
\textbf{dummy variable} that switches between \texttt{0} and \texttt{1}
can be plotted as a shaded band to mark periods such as recessions. Once
you have styled a chart the way you like, right-click it and choose
\texttt{Save\ as\ Template} to reuse the formatting later.

\section{The Data Analysis ToolPak}\label{the-data-analysis-toolpak}

Some analyses --- such as regression --- require the \textbf{Analysis
ToolPak}, an add-in that ships with Excel but is turned off by default.
To enable it on Windows, go to
\texttt{File\ →\ Options\ →\ Add-ins\ →\ Manage:\ Excel\ Add-ins\ →\ Go},
check \texttt{Analysis\ ToolPak}, and click \texttt{OK}. On a Mac, use
\texttt{Tools\ →\ Excel\ Add-ins}. Once enabled, the ToolPak appears
under \texttt{Data\ →\ Data\ Analysis} and provides one-click tools for
descriptive statistics, regression, random number generation, and more.

\section{Statistical Function
Dictionary}\label{statistical-function-dictionary}

The remainder of the chapter lists the statistical Excel functions used
in the book, grouped by the chapter in which they first appear. Each is
explained in detail, with worked examples, in its home chapter.

\subsection*{General Purpose}\label{general-purpose}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{General Purpose}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=SUM(range)} adds all of the numbers in a range.
\item
  \texttt{=COUNT(range)} counts the cells that contain numbers, while
  \texttt{=COUNTA(range)} counts all non-empty cells.
\item
  \texttt{=MIN(range)} and \texttt{=MAX(range)} return the smallest and
  largest values in a range.
\item
  \texttt{=ABS(number)} returns the absolute value of a number.
\item
  \texttt{=SQRT(number)} returns the square root of a number.
\item
  \texttt{=IF(test,\ value\_if\_true,\ value\_if\_false)} returns one of
  two values depending on whether a condition is met.
\item
  \texttt{=AND(...)}, \texttt{=OR(...)}, and \texttt{=NOT(...)} combine
  or reverse logical conditions.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Tables and Charts (Descriptive Stats
II)}\label{tables-and-charts-descriptive-stats-ii}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Tables and Charts (Descriptive Stats
II)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{PivotTable} (\texttt{Insert\ →\ PivotTable}) automatically
  summarizes and counts data, making it the primary tool for building
  frequency and relative frequency distributions.
\item
  \texttt{=IFS(test1,\ value1,\ test2,\ value2,\ ...)} checks multiple
  conditions in order and returns the value for the first one that is
  true. It is used to assign each numerical observation to its class
  (bin) when building a frequency distribution.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Measures of Central Location (Descriptive Stats
III)}\label{measures-of-central-location-descriptive-stats-iii}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Measures of Central Location
(Descriptive Stats III)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(range)} calculates the mean of a range of numbers.
\item
  \texttt{=MEDIAN(range)} calculates the median.
\item
  \texttt{=MODE(range)} returns the single most frequent value, while
  \texttt{=MODE.MULT(range)} returns all modes when the data is
  multimodal.
\item
  \texttt{=SUMPRODUCT(array1,\ array2)} multiplies corresponding values
  in two ranges and sums the results. Dividing by \texttt{=SUM(weights)}
  gives the weighted mean.
\item
  \texttt{=GEOMEAN(range)} calculates the geometric mean. For rates of
  change, apply the function to the growth factors (\texttt{1\ +\ r})
  and subtract one at the end.
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGEIF(range,\ criteria,\ average\_range)} calculates the
  mean of the values that meet a given condition.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Measures of Dispersion (Descriptive Stats
IV)}\label{measures-of-dispersion-descriptive-stats-iv}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Measures of Dispersion (Descriptive
Stats IV)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=COUNTIF(range,\ criteria)} counts the number of cells that
  meet a condition.
\item
  \texttt{=MINIFS(min\_range,\ criteria\_range,\ criteria)} and
  \texttt{=MAXIFS(max\_range,\ criteria\_range,\ criteria)} return the
  minimum and maximum values filtered by one or more conditions.
\item
  \texttt{=VAR.S(range)} calculates the sample variance;
  \texttt{=VAR.P(range)} calculates the population variance.
\item
  \texttt{=STDEV.S(range)} calculates the sample standard deviation (the
  square root of the sample variance); \texttt{=STDEV.P(range)}
  calculates the population standard deviation.
\item
  \texttt{=VAR(IF(criteria\_range="condition",\ value\_range))} and
  \texttt{=STDEV(IF(criteria\_range="condition",\ value\_range))}
  calculate a conditional variance and standard deviation. Excel has no
  native \texttt{VARIF} or \texttt{STDEVIF}, so these are entered as
  array formulas.
\item
  \textbf{Coefficient of Variation} has no dedicated function; compute
  it as \texttt{=STDEV.S(range)\ /\ AVERAGE(range)}.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Measures of Shape and Position (Descriptive Stats
V)}\label{measures-of-shape-and-position-descriptive-stats-v}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Measures of Shape and Position
(Descriptive Stats V)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=PERCENTILE.INC(array,\ k)} returns the \(k\)th percentile of
  a range using the formula \(L_p = 1 + k(n-1)\), where \(k\) is
  expressed as a decimal between 0 and 1.
  \texttt{=PERCENTILE.EXC(array,\ k)} uses the alternative formula
  \(L_p = k(n+1)\).
\item
  \texttt{=QUARTILE.INC(array,\ quart)} returns the five point summary.
  The argument \texttt{quart} takes values 0 (minimum), 1 (\(Q_1\)), 2
  (median), 3 (\(Q_3\)), and 4 (maximum).
\item
  \texttt{=STANDARDIZE(x,\ mean,\ std\_dev)} calculates the z-score for
  a value. It can also be computed manually as
  \texttt{=(x\ -\ AVERAGE(range))\ /\ STDEV.S(range)}.
\item
  \texttt{=SKEW(range)} calculates the skewness of a range.
\item
  \texttt{=KURT(range)} calculates the excess kurtosis of a range.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Measures of Association (Regression
I)}\label{measures-of-association-regression-i}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Measures of Association (Regression
I)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S(array1,\ array2)} calculates the sample
  covariance between two variables; \texttt{=COVARIANCE.P()} calculates
  the population covariance.
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(array1,\ array2)} calculates the correlation
  coefficient, which is always between \(-1\) and \(1\).
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(array1,\ array2)\^{}2} calculates the coefficient of
  determination \(R^2\) by squaring the correlation coefficient.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Regression (Regression II)}\label{regression-regression-ii}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Regression (Regression II)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Data Analysis ToolPak → Regression} returns the coefficients,
  \(R^2\), adjusted \(R^2\), standard error, and the ANOVA table in a
  single output.
\item
  \texttt{=SLOPE(y\_range,\ x\_range)} and
  \texttt{=INTERCEPT(y\_range,\ x\_range)} return the slope and
  intercept of a simple regression line.
\item
  \texttt{=FORECAST.LINEAR(x,\ y\_range,\ x\_range)} returns the
  predicted value of \(y\) for a given \(x\).
\item
  \texttt{=RSQ(y\_range,\ x\_range)} returns the \(R^2\), and
  \texttt{=STEYX(y\_range,\ x\_range)} returns the residual standard
  error.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Probability Distributions (Probability
I--III)}\label{probability-distributions-probability-iiii}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Probability Distributions (Probability
I--III)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{=FACT(number)}, \texttt{=COMBIN(n,\ x)}, and
  \texttt{=PERMUT(n,\ x)} return the factorial, number of combinations,
  and number of permutations. Use \texttt{=COMBINA()} and
  \texttt{=PERMUTATIONA()} when repetition is allowed.
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(x,\ n,\ p,\ cumulative)},
  \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(x,\ mean,\ cumulative)}, and
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(sample\_s,\ number\_sample,\ population\_s,\ number\_pop,\ cumulative)}
  return discrete probabilities (\texttt{FALSE} for the PMF,
  \texttt{TRUE} for the cumulative distribution).
  \texttt{=BINOM.INV(n,\ p,\ alpha)} returns a binomial quantile.
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(x,\ mean,\ std\_dev,\ cumulative)} and
  \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(z,\ cumulative)} return normal probabilities,
  while \texttt{=NORM.INV(probability,\ mean,\ std\_dev)} and
  \texttt{=NORM.S.INV(probability)} return normal quantiles.
  \texttt{=EXPON.DIST(x,\ lambda,\ cumulative)} returns exponential
  probabilities.
\end{itemize}

\bsanswerkeystart

\bsanswerchapter{1}{Descriptive Stats I}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans1}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \emph{The ``Title'' variable represents the names of books. Therefore,
  this is a categorical variable.}
\item
  \emph{``Price'' represents the cost of each book in a numeric format,
  making it a numerical variable.}
\item
  \emph{``Year Published'' indicates the publication year of each book.
  It is numerical.}
\item
  \emph{If ``Rating'' represents a numerical score based on a continuous
  scale (e.g., average user ratings on a platform like Goodreads), it is
  numerical because arithmetic operations like averaging or comparing
  differences are meaningful.} \emph{If ``Rating'' represents predefined
  categories (e.g., ``Excellent,'' ``Good,'' ``Fair,'' ``Poor'') or is
  interpreted as ranks without meaningful differences between values, it
  would be categorical.}
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans2}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \emph{The measurement scale is nominal for ``Title'' since these are
  labels used to identify each book and do not have a numerical meaning
  or order.}
\item
  \emph{If ``Rating'' represents a score (e.g., 4.2, 4.0) given to each
  book, it is numerical and could be considered interval data because
  the scale represents a meaningful difference, but it may not have an
  absolute zero or meaningful ratios (e.g., a book rated 4.0 is not
  ``twice as good'' as one rated 2.0).}
\item
  \emph{``Price'' is a measurable quantity with a meaningful zero (e.g.,
  a book priced at \$0 means it is free), making it ratio data.}
\item
  \emph{``Year'' is interval data because the zero point is arbitrary
  (year 0 does not represent the absence of time) and differences
  between years are meaningful (e.g., 1897 - 1818 = 79 years).}
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans3}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{The variable Year is measured on the interval scale because the
observations can be ranked, categorized and measured when using this
kind of scale. However, there is no true zero point so we cannot
calculate meaningful ratios between years.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans4}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{The variable Quarter is measured on the ordinal scale, even though
it contains numbers.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans5}{Exercise 2, question 3}

\emph{The variable Deliveries is measured on the ratio scale. It is the
strongest level of measurement because it allows us to categorize and
rank the data as well as find meaningful differences between
observations. Also, with a true zero point, we can interpret the ratios
between observations.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans6}{Exercise 3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \emph{Customer ID: Categorical, Nominal. These are arbitrary labels
  used only for identification. There is no order or numerical meaning;
  you can only determine if two IDs are the same or different.}
\item
  \emph{Satisfaction Level: Categorical, Ordinal. The ratings have a
  clear order (higher numbers indicate greater satisfaction), but the
  intervals between levels are not necessarily equal (the difference
  between 4 and 5 may not be perceived the same as between 1 and 2).
  Ratios are not meaningful.}
\item
  \emph{Visit Duration: Numerical, Ratio. Time in minutes has a true
  zero (0 minutes means no time spent), equal intervals, and meaningful
  ratios (e.g., 60 minutes is twice as long as 30 minutes). All
  arithmetic operations are valid.}
\item
  \emph{Beverage Type: Categorical, Nominal. These are unordered
  categories/labels for different drinks. You can group or count them,
  but there is no natural order or numerical relationship between
  categories like ``Latte'' and ``Tea''.}
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{2}{Descriptive Stats II}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans7}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The most common diagnosis is Meningioma, a slow-growing tumor that
forms from the membranous layers surrounding the brain and spinal cord.
The diagnosis represents about} \(48.28\)\% of the sample.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
Diagnosis & Frequency & Relative \\
\midrule
Meningioma & 42 & 48.28\% \\
LG glioma & 9 & 10.34\% \\
HG glioma & 22 & 25.29\% \\
Other & 14 & 16.09\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{In Excel, build a PivotTable: drag the \texttt{diagnosis} field to
the Rows area and again to the Values area (Excel counts each category).
To add the relative frequency, drag \texttt{diagnosis} to the Values
area a second time and set
\texttt{Value\ Field\ Settings\ →\ Show\ Values\ As\ →\ \%\ of\ Column\ Total}.
Alternatively, use \texttt{=COUNTIF(range,\ "Meningioma")} for each
category and divide by the total \texttt{=COUNTA(range)}.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans8}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The majority of diagnoses are Meningioma. Low grade glioma is the
least common. High grade glioma and Other have about the same
frequency.}

\emph{With the PivotTable selected, go to
\texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Clustered\ Column} to create the bar
chart.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans9}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\(33\) people did not survive Meningioma --- the highest number of
non-survivals. The survival rate of Meningioma is only \(21.43\)\%.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
\toprule
Status & Meningioma & LG glioma & HG glioma & Other \\
\midrule
0 & 33 & 5 & 5 & 9 \\
1 & 9 & 4 & 17 & 5 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{In Excel, build a PivotTable with \texttt{diagnosis} in the
Columns area, \texttt{status} in the Rows area, and \texttt{status} in
the Values area as a count. To obtain the survival rates, set
\texttt{Show\ Values\ As\ →\ \%\ of\ Column\ Total}; the survival rate
for Meningioma is} \(9/42 = 21.43\)\%.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans10}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{The highest frequency is in the} \(80 < x \le 90\) bin. \(8\)
temperatures were between \(50 < x \le 60\) degrees.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cc}
\toprule
Interval & Frequency \\
\midrule
\((50,60]\) & 8 \\
\((60,70]\) & 25 \\
\((70,80]\) & 52 \\
\((80,90]\) & 54 \\
\((90,100]\) & 14 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{In Excel, add a helper column with an \texttt{=IFS()} formula to
assign each temperature to its class, then build a PivotTable to count
the observations in each bin, exactly as shown in the chapter.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans11}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{The temperature was} \(5.23\)\% of the time between \(50\) and
\(60\); the temperature was \(70\) or less \(33\) times; the temperature
was above \(70\), \(78.43\)\% of the time.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
\toprule
Interval & Frequency & Relative & Cumulative & Rel. Cumulative \\
\midrule
\((50,60]\) & 8 & 5.23\% & 8 & 5.23\% \\
\((60,70]\) & 25 & 16.34\% & 33 & 21.57\% \\
\((70,80]\) & 52 & 33.99\% & 85 & 55.56\% \\
\((80,90]\) & 54 & 35.29\% & 139 & 90.85\% \\
\((90,100]\) & 14 & 9.15\% & 153 & 100.00\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{In the PivotTable, drag the bin field to the Values area a second
time and use \texttt{Show\ Values\ As\ →\ \%\ of\ Column\ Total} for the
relative frequency, and \texttt{Show\ Values\ As\ →\ Running\ Total\ In}
for the cumulative and relative cumulative distributions.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans12}{Exercise 2, question 3}

\emph{The distribution is not perfectly symmetric. It is skewed slightly
to the left.}

\emph{With the PivotTable selected, insert a Clustered Column chart and
set the \texttt{Gap\ Width} to} \(0\)\% to turn it into a histogram.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{3}{Descriptive Statistics III}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans18}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{To find the mean we will use the following formula}
\(( \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=i}^{n} x_{i})\). The summation of the values is
\(51\) and the number of observations is \(5\). The mean is
\(51/5=10.2\).

\emph{The median is found by locating the middle value when data is
sorted in ascending order.} \(x_{sorted}=[8,9,10,12,12]\). The median in
this example is \(10\).

\emph{The mode is the value with the highest frequency. In this example
the mode is} \(12\) since it is repeated twice and all other numbers
appear only once.

\emph{To verify in Excel, enter the data in cells \texttt{A1:A5} and
use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(8,9,10,12,12)} → \(10.2\)
\item
  \texttt{=MEDIAN(8,9,10,12,12)} → \(10\)
\item
  \texttt{=MODE(8,9,10,12,12)} → \(12\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans19}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The mean is} \(-2.67\), the median is \(-3.5\), the mode is
\(-4\). \emph{To verify in Excel, enter the data in cells \texttt{A1:A6}
and use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(-4,0,-6,1,-3,-4)} → \(-2.67\)
\item
  \texttt{=MEDIAN(-4,0,-6,1,-3,-4)} → \(-3.5\)
\item
  \texttt{=MODE(-4,0,-6,1,-3,-4)} → \(-4\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans20}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{The mean is} \(18.33\), the median is \(20\), the data is bimodal
with both \(15\) and \(20\) being modes.

\emph{To verify in Excel, enter the data in cells \texttt{A1:A9} and
use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(20,15,25,20,10,15,25,20,15)} → \(18.33\)
\item
  \texttt{=MEDIAN(20,15,25,20,10,15,25,20,15)} → \(20\)
\item
  \texttt{=MODE.MULT(20,15,25,20,10,15,25,20,15)} → \(15\) and \(20\)
  (note: use \texttt{MODE.MULT} instead of \texttt{MODE} to capture all
  modes)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans21}{Exercise 2, question 1}

Minute Maid is \(2.09\).

D and} \texttt{PriceMM} \emph{is in column E, use:}

\begin{itemize}

\item

Assuming \texttt{StoreID} is in column C and \texttt{PriceCH} is in

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans24}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{The average price of sale is found by using the weighted average
formula.} \(\frac{\sum w_{i}x_{i}}{\sum w_{i}}\) The weights (\(w_{i}\))
are given by the number of shares bought and the values (\(x_{i}\)) are
the prices. The weighted average is \(246.802\).

\emph{To verify in Excel use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SUMPRODUCT(\{250.34,234.59,270.45\},\{80,120,50\})\ /\ SUM(80,120,50)}
  → \(246.802\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

*The average growth rate is \(20.38%
\). Use the geometric mean formula with compounding.

\[\bar x_{g}=0.2038\]

You can use the command \texttt{GEOMEAN(1.3,1.2308,1.125,1.1667)-1} in

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans28}{Exercise 5}

\emph{At the end of 4 years it is predicted that you would have}
\(1621.17\) dollars. Each year you would have gained \(12.84\)\% on
average.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(0.173,\ 0.196,\ 0.068,\ 0.082)} → \(0.12975\)
  arithmetic mean
\item
  \texttt{=GEOMEAN(1.173,\ 1.196,\ 1.068,\ 1.082)-1} → \(0.1173\)

\bsanswerchapter{4}{Descriptive Stats IV}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans31}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The mean is} \(60\), the Range is \(94\), the MAD is \(28\), the
variance is \(1186\) and the variance is \(34.44\).

\emph{Here is the table of deviations:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) & \(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule
70 & 10 & 100 & 10 \\
68 & 8 & 64 & 8 \\
4 & -56 & 3136 & 56 \\
98 & 38 & 1444 & 38 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{The variance averages out the squared deviations}
\((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\), the MAD averages out the absolute deviations
\(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\), and the standard deviation is the square root of
the variance.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans32}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The mean is \(-2\), Range is \(7\), the MAD is \(2.33\), the
variance is \(7.6\) and the standard deviation is \(2.76\).}

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) & \(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule
-4 & -2 & 4 & 2 \\
0 & 2 & 4 & 2 \\
-6 & -4 & 16 & 4 \\
1 & 3 & 9 & 3 \\
-3 & -1 & 1 & 1 \\
0 & 2 & 4 & 2 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans33}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{The mean is \(6\), Range is \(8\), the MAD is \(2\), the variance
is \(8\) and the standard deviation is \(2.83\).}

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) & \(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule
2 & -4 & 16 & 4 \\
8 & 2 & 4 & 2 \\
4 & -2 & 4 & 2 \\
10 & 4 & 16 & 4 \\
6 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
6 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{The range is \(Range = 10 - 2 = 8\).}

\emph{The MAD is \(mad = (4+2+2+4+0+0)/6 = 12/6 = 2\).}

\emph{The sample variance is
\(s^2 = (16+4+4+16+0+0)/(6-1) = 40/5 = 8\).}

\emph{The standard deviation is \(s = \sqrt{8} = 2.83\).}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans34}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{For the sample taken, GBTC has the less variation. The standard
deviation of GBTC is \(9.43\), which is less than \(16.57\) for VTI or
\(50.38\) for TSLA.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans35}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{The answer is the same, since the MAD for GBTC is \(8.46\) which
is lower than \(14.27\) for VTI or \(41.67\) for TSLA.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans36}{Exercise 2, question 3}

\emph{By considering the magnitudes of the stock prices, it seems like
VTI is the less volatile stock. VTI has a CV of \(0.08\) which is lower
than \(0.44\) for GBTC or \(0.18\) for TSLA. In fact, by CV Bitcoin
seems to be the most risky asset.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans37}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{The best performing stock on average is stock X. It has an average
return of \(-0.078\)\% vs.~\(0.097\)\% for stock Y. The safest stock is
stock X as well, since the standard deviation is \(1.062\) percentage
points vs.~\(1.14\) percentage points for stock Y.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans38}{Exercise 3, question 2}

*The Sharpe Ratio measures the excess return per unit of risk taken.
Stock X has the better Sharpe Ratio \(-0.106\) vs.~\(-0.115\) for stock
Y. Stock X is recommended since it provides a higher excess return per
unit of risk taken.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans39}{Exercise 4}

\emph{Start by computing the sample mean:}

\[\bar{x} = \frac{2.1 + (-0.5) + 3.0 + 1.2 + (-1.8) + 0.7 + 2.5 + (-0.3)}{8} = \frac{6.9}{8} = 0.8625\]

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) & \(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule
2.1 & 1.2375 & 1.5314 & 1.2375 \\
-0.5 & -1.3625 & 1.8564 & 1.3625 \\
3.0 & 2.1375 & 4.5689 & 2.1375 \\
1.2 & 0.3375 & 0.1139 & 0.3375 \\
-1.8 & -2.6625 & 7.0889 & 2.6625 \\
0.7 & -0.1625 & 0.0264 & 0.1625 \\
2.5 & 1.6375 & 2.6814 & 1.6375 \\
-0.3 & -1.1625 & 1.3514 & 1.1625 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{The sample variance is:}

\[s^2 = \frac{\sum(x_i - \bar{x})^2}{n-1} = \frac{19.2188}{7} = 2.7455\]

\emph{The sample standard deviation is:}

\[s = \sqrt{2.7455} = 1.657\]

\emph{The Mean Absolute Deviation is:}

\[mad = \frac{\sum|x_i - \bar{x}|}{n} = \frac{10.7}{8} = 1.3375\]

\emph{The Coefficient of Variation is:}

\[CV = \frac{s}{\bar{x}} \times 100 = \frac{1.657}{0.8625} \times 100 = 192.1\%\]

\emph{The CV is about \(192\%\), which is large: the standard deviation
is almost twice the mean. This indicates the returns are highly variable
relative to their average --- the mean is small compared with typical
month-to-month fluctuations. In practice, a CV this large signals that
the project's returns are unreliable; an investor cannot count on
earning close to the average return in any given month.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans40}{Exercise 5, question 1}

\emph{The sample mean is:}

\[\bar{x} = \frac{120 + (-85) + 200 + (-150) + 95 + (-60) + 310 + (-220) + 175 + (-45)}{10} = \frac{340}{10} = 34\]

\emph{The bettor is profitable on average, earning \(\$34\) per week.}

\emph{Here is the table of deviations from the mean:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
\(x_{i}\) & \(x_{i}-\bar{x}\) & \((x_{i}-\bar{x})^2\) & \(|x_{i}-\bar{x}|\) \\
\midrule
120 & 86 & 7,396 & 86 \\
-85 & -119 & 14,161 & 119 \\
200 & 166 & 27,556 & 166 \\
-150 & -184 & 33,856 & 184 \\
95 & 61 & 3,721 & 61 \\
-60 & -94 & 8,836 & 94 \\
310 & 276 & 76,176 & 276 \\
-220 & -254 & 64,516 & 254 \\
175 & 141 & 19,881 & 141 \\
-45 & -79 & 6,241 & 79 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{The sample variance is:}

\[s^2 = \frac{262{,}340}{9} = 29{,}149\]

\emph{The sample standard deviation is:}

\[s = \sqrt{29{,}149} = \$170.73\]

\emph{The MAD is:}

\[mad = \frac{86+119+166+184+61+94+276+254+141+79}{10} = \frac{1{,}460}{10} = \$146\]

\emph{The bettor is profitable on average but faces large week-to-week
swings --- a typical week deviates from the mean by about \(\$146\) to
\(\$171\) depending on the measure used.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans41}{Exercise 5, question 2}

\[CV = \frac{170.73}{34} \times 100 = 502\%\]

\emph{A CV of \(502\%\) is extremely large (i.e., the standard deviation
is five) times the mean. This indicates that the average weekly profit
of \(\$34\) is tiny relative to the typical week-to-week swings of
\(\$171\). The bettor cannot reliably count on earning near the mean in
any given week. A high CV signals that the strategy is highly risky
relative to its average reward.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans42}{Exercise 5, question 3}

\emph{With a risk-free rate of \(\$0\):}

\[SR = \frac{\bar{x} - R_f}{s} = \frac{34 - 0}{170.73} = 0.199\]

\emph{A Sharpe ratio of \(0.199\) is low. As a point of reference, a
Sharpe ratio above \(1\) is generally considered acceptable in finance.
The bettor earns only \(\$0.20\) of profit per dollar of risk taken ---
suggesting that while the strategy is marginally profitable, the risk
taken to achieve that profit is not well compensated. A more disciplined
betting strategy or better odds selection would be needed to improve the
risk-adjusted return.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{5}{Descriptive Stats V}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans43}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The minimum is} \(0\), the first quartile is \(2\), second
quartile is \(4\), third quartile is \(8\), and maximum is \(30\).
\(30\) is an outlier since it is beyond \(Q_{3}+1.5 \times IQR\).

\emph{Quartiles are calculated with the location formula}
\(L_p = 1 + k(n-1)\). The sorted data is \(\{0,1,3,4,6,10,30\}\) with
\(n = 7\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(L_{25} = 1 + 0.25(6) = 2.5 \Rightarrow Q_1 = 1 + 0.5(3-1) = 2\)
\item
  \(L_{50} = 1 + 0.50(6) = 4 \Rightarrow Median = 4\)
\item
  \(L_{75} = 1 + 0.75(6) = 5.5 \Rightarrow Q_3 = 6 + 0.5(10-6) = 8\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{In Excel we get the five point summary with}
\texttt{=QUARTILE.INC()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 0)   /* Minimum = 0  */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 1)   /* Q1      = 2  */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 2)   /* Median  = 4  */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 3)   /* Q3      = 8  */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 4)   /* Maximum = 30 */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\emph{The interquartile range is needed to determine if there are any
outliers.} The \(IQR = Q_3 - Q_1 = 6\). Since
\(Q_3 + 1.5 \times IQR = 8 + 1.5(6) = 17\), everything beyond \(17\) is
an outlier --- so \(30\) is an outlier.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 3) {-} QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 1)   /* IQR = 6  */}
\NormalTok{=QUARTILE.INC(A:A, 3) + 1.5 * 6                 /* UL  = 17 */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans44}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{If we use the} \(z\)-score instead we find that \(30\) is
\textbf{not} an outlier since the \(z\)-score is \(Z_{30}=2.15\). This
observation is only \(2.15\) standard deviations away from the mean.

\emph{In Excel we make a quick calculation of the} \(z\)-score to
confirm our result. The \(z\)-score is
\(Z_{i}=\frac{x_{i}-\bar{x}}{s}\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=STANDARDIZE(30, AVERAGE(A:A), STDEV.S(A:A))   /* = 2.15 */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\emph{Since} \(2.15 < 3\), the value \(30\) is not flagged as an outlier
by the \(z\)-score method, even though the IQR method flagged it. The
two methods can disagree.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans45}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{Chebyshev's theorem states that} \(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\) of the data
lies between \(z\) standard deviations from the mean.

\emph{Substituting the} \(z\)-score found in 2. we get that at least
\(78.34\%\) of the data lies within that many standard deviations of the
mean. In Excel:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=1 {-} 1/(2.15\^{}2)    /* ≈ 0.7834, i.e. 78.34\% */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans46}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\[\bar{x} = \frac{2+2+2+6+13}{5} = \frac{25}{5} = 5\]

\[s = \sqrt{\frac{9+9+9+1+64}{4}} = \sqrt{23} = 4.796\]

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=AVERAGE(A2:A6)    /* mean = 5      */}
\NormalTok{=STDEV.S(A2:A6)    /* s    = 4.796  */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans47}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
\toprule
\(x_i\) & \(x_i - \bar{x}\) & \(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\) & \(\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^3\) & \(\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^4\) \\
\midrule
2 & \(-3\) & \(-0.625\) & \(-0.244\) & \(0.153\) \\
2 & \(-3\) & \(-0.625\) & \(-0.244\) & \(0.153\) \\
2 & \(-3\) & \(-0.625\) & \(-0.244\) & \(0.153\) \\
6 & \(1\) & \(0.208\) & \(0.009\) & \(0.002\) \\
13 & \(8\) & \(1.667\) & \(4.630\) & \(7.716\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{In Excel, put the standardized value in a helper column and copy
it down, then square and cube it:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=(A2 {-} AVERAGE($A$2:$A$6)) / STDEV.S($A$2:$A$6)   /* z    */}
\NormalTok{=B2\^{}3                                             /* z\^{}3  */}
\NormalTok{=B2\^{}4                                             /* z\^{}4  */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans48}{Exercise 2, question 3}

\[Sk = \frac{5}{(4)(3)}\sum\left(\frac{x_i-\bar{x}}{s}\right)^3 = \frac{5}{12}(-0.244-0.244-0.244+0.009+4.630)\]
\[= \frac{5}{12}(3.907) = 1.628\]

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{6}{Regression I}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans49}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The covariance is} \(14.9\) and the correlation is \(0.96\). The
results indicate that there is a strong direct relationship between the
two variables.

\emph{Start by finding the mean of each variable:} \(\bar{x} = 19.8\)
and \(\bar{y} = 16.6\). Then build the deviations table:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccc}
\toprule
\(x_i\) & \(y_i\) & \(x_i-\bar{x}\) & \(y_i-\bar{y}\) & Product \\
\midrule
20 & 17 & \(0.2\) & \(0.4\) & \(0.08\) \\
21 & 19 & \(1.2\) & \(2.4\) & \(2.88\) \\
15 & 12 & \(-4.8\) & \(-4.6\) & \(22.08\) \\
18 & 13 & \(-1.8\) & \(-3.6\) & \(6.48\) \\
25 & 22 & \(5.2\) & \(5.4\) & \(28.08\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\[s_{xy} = \frac{0.08+2.88+22.08+6.48+28.08}{4} = \frac{59.6}{4} = 14.9\]

\[r = \frac{14.9}{sd(x) \times sd(y)} = \frac{14.9}{3.49 \times 4.34} = 0.96\]

\emph{To verify in Excel, enter x in column A and y in column B and
use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(14.9\)
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(0.96\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans50}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The covariance is} \(0.85\) and the correlation is \(0.148\). The
results indicate that there is a very weak direct relationship between
the two variables. They might be unrelated.

\emph{To verify in Excel, enter w in column A and z in column B and
use:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(0.85\)
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A6,\ B2:B6)} → \(0.148\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans51}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{The correlation coefficient is} \(-0.78\). This is indicative of a
moderately strong inverse relationship between mpg and hp. As horsepower
increases, fuel efficiency tends to decrease.

\emph{In Excel, assuming \texttt{mpg} is in column A and \texttt{hp} is
in column B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A33,\ B2:B33)} → \(-0.78\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans52}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{The scatter diagram is downward sloping and most points are close
to the trend line. It is what was expected from a correlation
coefficient of} \(-0.78\).

\emph{In Excel, select the \texttt{hp} and \texttt{mpg} columns and go
to \texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Scatter}. To add the trend line,
right-click any point and choose \texttt{Add\ Trendline\ →\ Linear}.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans53}{Exercise 2, question 3}

\emph{The coefficient of determination is} \(0.60\). This value is not
very close to one. This is expected since miles per gallon can also vary
because of the car's weight and fuel efficiency. It makes sense that
\texttt{hp} only explains \(60\%\) of the total variation.

\emph{In Excel, square the correlation coefficient:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A33,\ B2:B33)\^{}2} → \(0.60\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans54}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{It seems like there is a direct relationship between both
variables. The more debt a graduate takes on, the higher the earnings
tend to be.}

\emph{In Excel, select the \texttt{MD\_EARN\_WNE\_P10} and
\texttt{GRAD\_DEBT\_MDN} columns and go to
\texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Scatter}. Add a linear trend line by
right-clicking a point and choosing \texttt{Add\ Trendline\ →\ Linear}.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans55}{Exercise 3, question 2}

\emph{The correlation coefficient shows a moderate direct relationship
between earnings and debt,} \(r = 0.46\). The coefficient of
determination indicates that only \(21\%\) of the variation in earnings
can be explained by debt.

\emph{In Excel, assuming \texttt{GRAD\_DEBT\_MDN} is in column A and
\texttt{MD\_EARN\_WNE\_P10} is in column B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A1000,\ B2:B1000)} → \(0.46\)
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A1000,\ B2:B1000)\^{}2} → \(0.21\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Note: use \texttt{=CORREL()} rather than \texttt{=COVARIANCE.S()}
when there are missing values, as CORREL handles them more gracefully in
Excel.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans56}{Exercise 4, question 1}

\emph{There is a direct relationship between bedrooms and price. As the
number of bedrooms increases, sale prices tend to rise. This is
intuitive since larger homes generally command higher market values.}

\emph{In Excel, select the \texttt{bedrooms} and \texttt{Price} columns
and go to \texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Scatter}. Add a linear trend
line by right-clicking a point and choosing
\texttt{Add\ Trendline\ →\ Linear}.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans57}{Exercise 4, question 2}

\emph{The correlation coefficient is} \(r = 0.92\), indicating a strong
direct relationship between bedrooms and sale price. Larger homes tend
to sell for higher prices, though many other factors (location,
condition, amenities) also influence price, which prevents a perfect
correlation.

\emph{In Excel, assuming \texttt{bedrooms} is in column A and
\texttt{Price} is in column B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A348,\ B2:B348)} → \(0.92\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans58}{Exercise 4, question 3}

\emph{The coefficient of determination is} \(R^2 = 0.85\), so about
\(85\%\) of the variation in home prices is explained by the number of
bedrooms alone. The remaining variation is likely driven by factors such
as location, number of bathrooms, lot size, school district quality, and
the overall condition of the property.

\emph{In Excel, square the correlation coefficient:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(A2:A348,\ B2:B348)\^{}2} → \(0.85\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{7}{Regression II}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans59}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The regression line is} \(\hat{y}=-4.93+1.09x\). For each unit
increase in \(x\), \(y\) increases on average by \(1.09\).

\emph{Start with the deviations from the mean (}\(\bar{x}=19.8\),
\(\bar{y}=16.6\)):

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccccc}
\toprule
\(x_i\) & \(y_i\) & \(x_i-\bar{x}\) & \(y_i-\bar{y}\) & Product & \((x_i-\bar{x})^2\) \\
\midrule
20 & 17 & \(0.2\) & \(0.4\) & \(0.08\) & \(0.04\) \\
21 & 19 & \(1.2\) & \(2.4\) & \(2.88\) & \(1.44\) \\
15 & 12 & \(-4.8\) & \(-4.6\) & \(22.08\) & \(23.04\) \\
18 & 13 & \(-1.8\) & \(-3.6\) & \(6.48\) & \(3.24\) \\
25 & 22 & \(5.2\) & \(5.4\) & \(28.08\) & \(27.04\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\[\hat{\beta}=\frac{\sum(x_i-\bar{x})(y_i-\bar{y})}{\sum(x_i-\bar{x})^2}=\frac{59.6}{54.8}=1.09\]

\[\hat{\alpha}=\bar{y}-\hat{\beta}\bar{x}=16.6-1.09(19.8)=-4.93\]

\emph{To verify in Excel, enter x in column A and y in column B. A
single} \texttt{LINEST} \emph{returns both coefficients, with the
intercept last:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(B2:B6, A2:A6, TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B6,A2:A6,TRUE,TRUE),1,1)} →
  \(\hat{\beta}=1.09\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B6,A2:A6,TRUE,TRUE),1,2)} →
  \(\hat{\alpha}=-4.93\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans60}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{SST is} \(69.2\), SSR is \(64.82\) and SSE is \(4.38\) (note that
\(SSR+SSE=SST\)). The \(R^2\) is \(\frac{SSR}{SST}=0.94\) and the
Standard Error estimate is \(1.21\). Both indicate a great fit of the
regression line to the data.

\emph{All four numbers come out of the same} \texttt{LINEST}
\emph{block, so spill it once with}
\texttt{=LINEST(B2:B6,\ A2:A6,\ TRUE,\ TRUE)} \emph{and read off:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B6,A2:A6,TRUE,TRUE),5,1)} → \(SSR = 64.82\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B6,A2:A6,TRUE,TRUE),5,2)} → \(SSE = 4.38\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B6,A2:A6,TRUE,TRUE),3,1)} → \(R^2 = 0.94\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B6,A2:A6,TRUE,TRUE),3,2)} → \(s_e = 1.21\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Then} \(SST = SSR + SSE = 69.2\), which \texttt{=DEVSQ(B2:B6)}
confirms.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans61}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{If} \(x=32\) then \(\hat{y}=-4.93+1.09(32)=29.87\). The regression
is a good fit, so we can feel good about our prediction. However, we
would be concerned about the small sample size of the data.

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=TREND(B2:B6,\ A2:A6,\ 32)} → \(29.87\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans62}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{An extra year of education increases the annual salary by about}
\(\$5,300\) (the slope). A person with no education would be expected to
earn about \(\$17,258\) (the intercept).

\emph{In Excel, assuming \texttt{Salary} is in column B and
\texttt{Education} is in column C:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(B2:B101, C2:C101, TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B101,C2:C101,TRUE,TRUE),1,1)} → \(5.30\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B101,C2:C101,TRUE,TRUE),1,2)} → \(17.26\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans63}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{The} \(R^2\) is \(0.67\) and the standard error is about \(21\).
The line is a moderately good fit. A person with \(16\) years of
education is predicted to earn about \(\$102,000\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B101,C2:C101,TRUE,TRUE),3,1)} →
  \(R^2 = 0.67\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B101,C2:C101,TRUE,TRUE),3,2)} → \(s_e = 21\)
\item
  \texttt{=TREND(B2:B101,\ C2:C101,\ 16)} → \(102.08\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans64}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{Approximately} \(36\%\) of the sample owns a home (mean
\(=0.3625\)).

\emph{In Excel, assuming \texttt{OwnHome} is in column A and
\texttt{Food} is in column B, first delete any rows with missing values.
Then add a helper column C with the dummy variable:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=IF(A2="Yes", 1, 0)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\emph{Copy it down for all rows and take the average:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(C2:C81)} → \(0.3625\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{You can also skip the helper column entirely --- the same
proportion is} \texttt{=AVERAGE(IF(A2:A81="Yes",1,0))}.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans65}{Exercise 3, question 2}

\emph{The intercept (}\(\approx 6473\)) is the average food expenditure
of individuals without homes. The slope (\(\approx -3418\)) is the
difference in food expenditure between individuals who own a home and
those who do not --- home owners spend about \(\$3,418\) less on food
than non-owners.

\emph{In Excel, with \texttt{Food} in column B and the dummy in column
C:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{=LINEST(B2:B81, C2:C81, TRUE, TRUE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B81,C2:C81,TRUE,TRUE),1,2)} → intercept
  \(= 6473\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(B2:B81,C2:C81,TRUE,TRUE),1,1)} → slope
  \(= -3418\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Because a single dummy just splits the sample in two, the two
predictions are the two group means:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=TREND(B2:B81,\ C2:C81,\ 0)} → \(6473\), the same as
  \texttt{=AVERAGEIF(A2:A81,"No",B2:B81)}
\item
  \texttt{=TREND(B2:B81,\ C2:C81,\ 1)} → \(3055\), the same as
  \texttt{=AVERAGEIF(A2:A81,"Yes",B2:B81)}
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans66}{Exercise 3, question 3}

\emph{The regression line is} \(\widehat{dummy}=1.432-0.000204\,Food\).
This is a linear probability model: it predicts the likelihood of owning
a home from food expenditure. The slope tells us how that likelihood
changes as food expenditure increases by one dollar --- in general, the
likelihood of owning a home decreases as food expenditure increases. The
scatter plot shows that most home owners have food expenditures below
\(\$6,000\), while non-owners are mostly above it.

\emph{In Excel, with the dummy in column C and \texttt{Food} in column
B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(C2:C81,B2:B81,TRUE,TRUE),1,2)} → intercept
  \(= 1.432\)
\item
  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(C2:C81,B2:B81,TRUE,TRUE),1,1)} → slope
  \(= -0.000204\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{For the scatter plot, select \texttt{Food} and the dummy variable,
go to \texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Scatter}, and add a linear trend
line by right-clicking a point and choosing
\texttt{Add\ Trendline\ →\ Linear}.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans67}{Exercise 4, question 1}

\emph{With an} \(R^2=0.81\), the model fits the data very well.

\emph{In Excel, first isolate Japan: select the data, go to

\begin{itemize}

  \texttt{=INDEX(LINEST(D2:D63,C2:C63,TRUE,TRUE),3,1)} → \(R^2 = 0.81\)

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans68}{Exercise 4, question 2}

\emph{The prediction for} \(2030\) is about \(140\) million people
(\(140{,}268{,}585\)).

\begin{itemize}

\item
  \texttt{=TREND(D2:D63,\ C2:C63,\ 2030)} → \(140{,}268{,}585\)
\end{itemize}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans69}{Exercise 4, question 3}

\emph{After looking at the scatter plot, it seems unlikely that the
population in Japan will hit} \(140\) million. In the most recent years

overstates the future population --- the prediction should be treated
