\bsanswerchapter{8}{Probability I}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans70}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(P(D)=1/5=0.2\) since all events are equally likely.
\item
  \(P(B^c)=4/5=0.8\)
\item
  \(P(A \cup C \cup E)=P(A + C + E)=3/5=0.6\).
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans71}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \emph{Events} \(A\) and \(B\) are not mutually exclusive since they
  share some of the same elements.
\item
  \emph{The events are not exhaustive since the union of both doesn't
  create the sample space.}
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans72}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \emph{The probability is} \(68.8\)\%.
\item
  \emph{The events are mutually exclusive. If someone is classified as
  obese, the person is not classified again as overweight.}
\item
  \emph{The events are not exhaustive since there are people in the U.S.
  that have a normal weight.}
\item
  \emph{The probability that the person drawn has normal weight is}
  \(31.2\)\%.
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans73}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{From the multiplication rule,} \[P(A|B)*P(B)=P(A \cap B)\]

\emph{substituting values yields:} \[P(A \cap B)=0.45*0.3=0.135\]
\emph{From the addition rule,} \[P(A \cup B)=P(A)+P(B)-P(A \cap B)\]
\emph{substituting yields:} \[P(A \cup B)=0.65+0.3-0.135=0.815\]
\emph{From the multiplication rule once again,}
\[P(B|A)=\frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(A)}\] \emph{substituting values yields,}
\[P(B|A)=0.135/0.65=0.2076923\]

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans74}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{From the complement rule we have that} \(P(A^c)=0.6\) and
\(P(B^c)=0.5\).\\
\emph{Using the multiplication rule,}
\[P(A^c|B^c)=\frac{P(A^c \cap B^c)}{P(B^c)}\] \emph{substituting
yields:} \[P(A^c|B^c)=0.24/0.5=0.48\] \emph{From the addition rule,}
\[P(A^c \cup B^c)=P(A^c)+P(B^c)-P(A^c \cap B^c)\] \emph{substituting
yields:} \[P(A^c \cup B^c)=0.6+0.5-0.24=0.86\] \emph{The event that has
no elements of} \(A\) or \(B\) is given by \(P(A^c \cap B^c)\).
Therefore \(P(A \cup B)=1-0.24=0.76\) has all the elements of A and B.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans75}{Exercise 2, question 3}

\emph{In short, the problem states} \(P(A)=0.4\), \(P(B)=0.6\), and
\(P(A|B)=0.5\). Where \(A\) and \(B\) are events of stocks rising in
price. The question asks for: \[P(A \cup B)=P(A)+P(B)-P(A \cap B)\]
\emph{Using the multiplication rule,} \[P(A \cap B)=0.5*0.6=0.3\]
\emph{Hence,} \[P(A \cup B)=0.4+0.6-0.3=0.7\] \emph{The events are not
mutually exclusive since} \(P(A \cap B)=0.3 \neq 0\). The events are
also not independent since \(P(A|B)=0.5 \neq 0.4=P(A)\).

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans76}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{Below is the joint probability table. The} \(P(A)=0.26+0.34=0.6\),
\(P(A \cap B)=0.26\), \(P(A|B)=0.26/0.4=0.65\), and
\(P(B|A^c)=0.14/0.4=0.35\). Events \(A\) and \(B\) are not independent
since \(P(A) \neq P(A|B)\). The events are not mutually exclusive since
\(P(A \cap B)=0.26 \neq 0\).

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
 & \(B\) & \(B^c\) & Total \\
\(A\) & 0.26 & 0.34 & 0.6 \\
\(A^c\) & 0.14 & 0.26 & 0.4 \\
Total & 0.4 & 0.6 & 1 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans77}{Exercise 4, question 1}

\emph{The contingency table below shows the counts for each combination
of Crash Type and Light Condition:}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
 & Daylight & Not Daylight & Total \\
\midrule
Head-on & 166 & 108 & 274 \\
Not Head-On & 3258 & 1326 & 4584 \\
Total & 3424 & 1434 & 4858 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\emph{In Excel, build a PivotTable: drag \texttt{Crash\ Type} to the
Rows area, \texttt{Light\ Condition} to the Columns area, and either
field to the Values area as a Count. To turn the counts into a joint
probability table, use
\texttt{Value\ Field\ Settings\ →\ Show\ Values\ As\ →\ \%\ of\ Grand\ Total}.
You can also count any single cell directly. Assuming
\texttt{Crash\ Type} is in column B and \texttt{Light\ Condition} in
column C:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COUNTIFS(B:B,\ "Head-on",\ C:C,\ "Daylight")} → \(166\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans78}{Exercise 4, question 2}

\emph{a) The probability of a Head-On crash is:}

\[P(\text{Head-on})=\frac{166+108}{4858}=\frac{274}{4858}=0.056\]

\emph{b) The probability that the crash is in daylight is:}

\[P(\text{Daylight})=\frac{166+3258}{4858}=\frac{3424}{4858}=0.70\]

\emph{c) The probability that a crash is Head-On given daylight is:}

\[P(\text{Head-on}\mid\text{Daylight})=\frac{P(\text{Head-on}\cap\text{Daylight})}{P(\text{Daylight})}=\frac{166}{3424}=0.048\]

\emph{In Excel these follow from the PivotTable counts. For example,
part a) is \texttt{=COUNTIF(B:B,"Head-on")\ /\ COUNTA(B2:B4859)}.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans79}{Exercise 4, question 3}

\emph{The two variables are dependent since}
\(P(\text{Head-on}\mid\text{Daylight}) \neq P(\text{Head-on})\), that is
\(0.048 \neq 0.056\).

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans80}{Exercise 5, question 1}

\(P(A^c)=1-P(A)=1-0.7=0.3\),
\(P(A \cap B)=𝑃(𝐵|𝐴)𝑃(𝐴) = 0.55(0.70) = 0.385\),
\(P(A^c \cap B)=𝑃(B|A^c)𝑃(A^c) = 0.10(0.30) = 0.03\),
\(P(B)= 𝑃(A \cap B) + 𝑃(𝐴^c \cap 𝐵) = 0.385 + 0.03 = 0.415\), and
\(P(A|B)= \frac{𝑃(A \cap B)}{P(B)}=0.385/0.415=0.9277\).

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans81}{Exercise 5, question 2}

\emph{Let the event of failing be} \(F\), the event of not failing be
\(NF\), the event of having a tutor be \(T\), and the event of not
having a tutor be \(NT\). The probability of failing the course is
\(0.25\).
\((𝐹) = 𝑃(𝐹 \cap 𝑇) + 𝑃(𝐹 \cap 𝑇^c) = 𝑃(𝐹|𝑇)𝑃(𝑇) + 𝑃(𝐹|𝑇^c)𝑃(𝑇^c) = 0.10(0.50) + 0.40(0.50) = 0.05 + 0.20 = 0.25\)
The probability of not having a tutor, given that she failed the course
is \(0.2\).
\(P(𝑇|𝐹) = \frac{𝑃(𝐹\cap𝑇)}{𝑃(𝐹\cap𝑇)+𝑃(𝐹 \cap𝑇^c)}= 0.05/0.25 = 0.20\).

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans82}{Exercise 6, question 1}

\(3!=3 \times 2 \times 1=6\), \(4!=4 \times 3 \times 2 \times 1=24\),
\(C_6^8=28\), and \(P_6^8=20{,}160\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=FACT(3)} → \(6\)
\item
  \texttt{=FACT(4)} → \(24\)
\item
  \texttt{=COMBIN(8,\ 6)} → \(28\)
\item
  \texttt{=PERMUT(8,\ 6)} → \(20{,}160\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans83}{Exercise 6, question 2}

\emph{If order doesn't matter, there are} \(252\) ways. If order
matters, then there are \(30{,}240\) ways.

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COMBIN(10,\ 5)} → \(252\)
\item
  \texttt{=PERMUT(10,\ 5)} → \(30{,}240\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{9}{Probability II}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans84}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The expected value is} \(10.75\) and the standard deviation is
\(5.31\). The probability of \(x<15\) is \(0.65\).

\emph{In Excel, enter the values x in column A and the probabilities
P(X=x) in column B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SUMPRODUCT(A2:A5,\ B2:B5)} → \(10.75\) (expected value)
\item
  \texttt{=SQRT(SUMPRODUCT((A2:A5-10.75)\^{}2,\ B2:B5))} → \(5.31\)
  (standard deviation)
\item
  \(P(x<15) = 0.35 + 0.3 = 0.65\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans85}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The expected value is} \(-17.4\) and the standard deviation is
\(6.86\). The probability of \(x \geq -9\) is \(0.25\).

\emph{In Excel, with y in column A and P(Y=y) in column B:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SUMPRODUCT(A2:A5,\ B2:B5)} → \(-17.4\)
\item
  \texttt{=SQRT(SUMPRODUCT((A2:A5-(-17.4))\^{}2,\ B2:B5))} → \(6.86\)
\item
  \(P(y \geq -9) = 0.15 + 0.1 = 0.25\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans86}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{Both funds have the same expected return of} \(6\). The safest
return comes from fund 1 since the standard deviation is only \(11.14\)
vs.~\(31.05\) for fund 2.

\emph{In Excel, enter the probabilities in column A, Fund 1 in column B,
and Fund 2 in column C:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SUMPRODUCT(A2:A4,\ B2:B4)} → \(6\) (Fund 1 expected return)
\item
  \texttt{=SUMPRODUCT(A2:A4,\ C2:C4)} → \(6\) (Fund 2 expected return)
\item
  \texttt{=SQRT(SUMPRODUCT((B2:B4-6)\^{}2,\ A2:A4))} → \(11.14\) (Fund 1
  std. dev.)
\item
  \texttt{=SQRT(SUMPRODUCT((C2:C4-6)\^{}2,\ A2:A4))} → \(31.05\) (Fund 2
  std. dev.)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Since both funds have the same expected return, choose Fund 1 ---
it achieves that return with much less risk.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans87}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{The expected return of the portfolio is} \(10.4\) and the standard
deviation is \(14.60\).

\emph{The portfolio weights are} \(\alpha=2/5=0.4\) for X and
\(\beta=3/5=0.6\) for Y. In Excel:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Expected return \(\alpha R_1 + \beta R_2\): \texttt{=0.4*8\ +\ 0.6*12}
  → \(10.4\)
\item
  Standard deviation
  \(\sqrt{\alpha^2 \sigma_1^2+\beta^2 \sigma_2^2+2\alpha\beta\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2}\):
  \texttt{=SQRT(0.4\^{}2*12\^{}2\ +\ 0.6\^{}2*20\^{}2\ +\ 2*0.4*0.6*0.4*12*20)}
  → \(14.60\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans88}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\(P(Z=1)=0.31\), and \(P(Z \geq 2)=0.57\). The expected value is
\(np=1.75\) and the standard deviation is \(\sqrt{np(1-p)}=1.067\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(1,\ 5,\ 0.35,\ FALSE)} → \(0.31\)
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ BINOM.DIST(1,\ 5,\ 0.35,\ TRUE)} → \(0.57\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans89}{Exercise 3, question 2}

\(P(W>160)=0.14\), and \(P(155 \leq W \leq 165 )=0.45\). The expected
value is \(np=154\) and the standard deviation is
\(\sqrt{np(1-p)}=5.95\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ BINOM.DIST(160,\ 200,\ 0.77,\ TRUE)} → \(0.14\)
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(165,\ 200,\ 0.77,\ TRUE)\ -\ BINOM.DIST(154,\ 200,\ 0.77,\ TRUE)}
  → \(0.45\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans90}{Exercise 3, question 3}

\emph{The probabilities are the same. Each event has a probability of}
\(0.3456\). If the probability changes to \(0.5\) now the event of two
women and two men is more likely.

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(3,\ 4,\ 0.6,\ FALSE)} → \(0.3456\) (three men)
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(2,\ 4,\ 0.6,\ FALSE)} → \(0.3456\) (two men)
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(3,\ 4,\ 0.5,\ FALSE)} → \(0.25\)
\item
  \texttt{=BINOM.DIST(2,\ 4,\ 0.5,\ FALSE)} → \(0.375\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{At} \(p=0.5\), having two of each is the most likely outcome.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans91}{Exercise 4, question 1}

\emph{The} \(P(S=2)=0.25\) and \(P(S \geq 2)=0.44\). The expected value
and the variance is \(1.5\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(2,\ 1.5,\ FALSE)} → \(0.25\)
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ POISSON.DIST(1,\ 1.5,\ TRUE)} → \(0.44\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans92}{Exercise 4, question 2}

\emph{The} \(P(T=14)=0.039\) and \(P(18 \leq T \leq 23)=0.49\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(14,\ 20,\ FALSE)} → \(0.039\)
\item
  \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(23,\ 20,\ TRUE)\ -\ POISSON.DIST(17,\ 20,\ TRUE)}
  → \(0.49\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans93}{Exercise 4, question 3}

\emph{The probability of administering more than} \(8\) but less than
\(12\) shots is \(0.3\).

\emph{If} \(84\) shots are administered on average weekly, then \(12\)
are administered daily. Using this daily mean in Excel:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=POISSON.DIST(11,\ 12,\ TRUE)\ -\ POISSON.DIST(8,\ 12,\ TRUE)}
  → \(0.30\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans94}{Exercise 5, question 1}

\(P(X=0)=0.58\), \(P(X=1)=0.37\), and \(P(X \leq 1)=0.94\).

\emph{In Excel (successes in population} \(r=3\), population \(N=25\),
sample \(n=4\)):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(0,\ 4,\ 3,\ 25,\ FALSE)} → \(0.58\)
\item
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(1,\ 4,\ 3,\ 25,\ FALSE)} → \(0.37\)
\item
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(1,\ 4,\ 3,\ 25,\ TRUE)} → \(0.94\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans95}{Exercise 5, question 2}

\emph{The probability is} \(0.078\).

\emph{In Excel (successes in population} \(r=25\), population \(N=100\),
sample \(n=20\)):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ HYPGEOM.DIST(7,\ 20,\ 25,\ 100,\ TRUE)} → \(0.078\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans96}{Exercise 5, question 3}

\emph{The probability of matching two white balls is} \(5\%\). Winning
the jackpot is extremely unlikely! A probability of \(0.00000000512\).
It is more likely to be struck by lightning according to the CDC.

\emph{In Excel, treat the five drawn white balls as the successes in a
population of} \(59\), with the player's five picks as the sample:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(2,\ 5,\ 5,\ 59,\ FALSE)} → \(0.05\) (match two
  white balls)
\end{itemize}

\emph{For the jackpot, multiply the probability of matching all five
white balls by the probability of matching the single Powerball (1 of
39):}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=HYPGEOM.DIST(5,\ 5,\ 5,\ 59,\ FALSE)\ *\ HYPGEOM.DIST(1,\ 1,\ 1,\ 39,\ FALSE)}
  → \(0.00000000512\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{10}{Probability III}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans97}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The height of the density function} \(f(x)=0.1667\), the mean is
\(1\), standard deviation is \(1.73\), and \(P(X \leq -1)=0.1667\).

\emph{The uniform distribution has no Excel function, so each quantity
is computed directly from its formula:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Height \(f(x)=\frac{1}{b-a}\): \texttt{=1/(4-(-2))} → \(0.1667\)
\item
  Mean \(\mu=\frac{a+b}{2}\): \texttt{=(-2+4)/2} → \(1\)
\item
  Standard deviation \(\sigma=\sqrt{\frac{(b-a)^2}{12}}\):
  \texttt{=SQRT((4-(-2))\^{}2/12)} → \(1.73\)
\item
  \(P(X \leq -1)=\frac{x-a}{b-a}\): \texttt{=(-1-(-2))/(4-(-2))} →
  \(0.1667\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans98}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The probability that you will arrive on time is} \(0.875\). If the
time of the errand is 30 minutes, then the probability goes down to
\(0.75\).

\emph{There is a} \(120\) minute interval in which the provider can
arrive, so \(P(X>t)=1-\frac{t}{120}\). In Excel:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ 15/120} → \(0.875\)
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ 30/120} → \(0.75\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans99}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\(P(-0.67 \leq Z \leq -0.23)=0.158\), \(P(0 \leq Z \leq 1.96)=0.475\),
\(P(-1.28 \leq Z \leq 0)=0.4\) and \(P(Z > 4.2) \approx 0\).

\emph{Use the \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST()} function for the standard normal
distribution:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(-0.23,\ TRUE)\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(-0.67,\ TRUE)} →
  \(0.158\)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(1.96,\ TRUE)\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(0,\ TRUE)} →
  \(0.475\)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(0,\ TRUE)\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(-1.28,\ TRUE)} →
  \(0.4\)
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(4.2,\ TRUE)} → \(\approx 0\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans100}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\(P(Y>7.6)=0.005386\), \(P(7.4 \leq Y \leq 10.6)=0.0071\), a \(y\) such
that \(P(Y>y)=0.025\) is \(6.42\), and a \(y\) such that
\(P(y \leq Y \leq 2.5)\) is \(-2.56\).

\emph{Use \texttt{=NORM.DIST()} for probabilities and
\texttt{=NORM.INV()} for quantiles:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.DIST(7.6,\ 2.5,\ 2,\ TRUE)} → \(0.005386\)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(10.6,\ 2.5,\ 2,\ TRUE)\ -\ NORM.DIST(7.4,\ 2.5,\ 2,\ TRUE)}
  → \(0.0071\)
\item
  \(P(Y>y)=0.025\) means \(y\) is the \(97.5\)th percentile:
  \texttt{=NORM.INV(0.975,\ 2.5,\ 2)} → \(6.42\)
\item
  Since \(2.5\) is the mean, \(P(y \leq Y \leq 2.5)=0.4943\) leaves
  \(0.5-0.4943=0.0057\) in the left tail:
  \texttt{=NORM.INV(0.0057,\ 2.5,\ 2)} → \(-2.56\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans101}{Exercise 2, question 3}

\emph{The probability is} \(10.56\)\%. A game lasting no more than
\(2.069\) hours would be in the bottom \(1\)\%.

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(2.5,\ 3,\ 0.4,\ TRUE)} → \(0.1056\)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.INV(0.01,\ 3,\ 0.4)} → \(2.069\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans102}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{The standard deviation is equal to the mean} \(0.1\).
\(P(0.10 \leq S \leq 0.2)=0.2325\)

\emph{The rate is} \(\lambda = 1/\mu = 1/0.1 = 10\). In Excel:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=EXPON.DIST(0.2,\ 10,\ TRUE)\ -\ EXPON.DIST(0.1,\ 10,\ TRUE)}
  → \(0.2325\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans103}{Exercise 3, question 2}

\emph{The mean time between car arrivals is} \(1/360=0.002778\) hours.
The probability that the next car will arrive within the next 10 seconds
is \(0.6321\).

\emph{Ten seconds is} \(10/3600 = 1/360\) of an hour. Using a rate of
\(\lambda = 360\) cars per hour in Excel:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=EXPON.DIST(1/360,\ 360,\ TRUE)} → \(0.6321\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{11}{Inference I}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans104}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{In a modern version of Excel, type the following in the top-left
cell of an empty block; it will spill into a} \(10 \times 1000\) grid
where each column is a sample of size \(10\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=RANDARRAY(10,\ 1000,\ 100,\ 200,\ FALSE)}
\end{itemize}

\emph{In an older version, enter \texttt{=100\ +\ 100*RAND()} in the
top-left cell and copy it across} \(1000\) columns and down \(10\) rows.

\emph{Because the values are drawn from a uniform distribution on}
\([100, 200]\), the population mean is \(\mu = \frac{100+200}{2} = 150\)
and the population standard deviation is
\(\sigma = \frac{200-100}{\sqrt{12}} \approx 28.87\). (The values
recalculate on every edit; to freeze a sample, copy the block and use
\texttt{Paste\ Special\ →\ Values}.)

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans105}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{Below the block, compute the mean of each column and copy it
across all} \(1000\) columns:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(A1:A10)} → the mean of the first sample
\end{itemize}

\emph{This produces} \(1000\) sample means. Summarize them with:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(...)} → about \(150\), matching the population mean
  (unbiasedness)
\item
  \texttt{=STDEV.S(...)} → about \(9.1\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{The standard deviation of the sample means is far smaller than the
population standard deviation (}\(\approx 28.87\)), and it matches the
standard error \(\sigma / \sqrt{n} = 28.87 / \sqrt{10} \approx 9.13\).

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans106}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{Select the} \(1000\) sample means and go to
\texttt{Insert\ →\ Charts\ →\ Histogram} (or use the bins-and-PivotTable
method from Descriptive Statistics II). Although the population is
uniform, the distribution of the sample means is bell-shaped --- this is
the Central Limit Theorem in action.

\emph{Using a normal distribution with mean} \(150\) and standard error
\(9.13\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(160,\ 150,\ 9.13,\ TRUE)\ -\ NORM.DIST(140,\ 150,\ 9.13,\ TRUE)}
  → about \(0.73\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{There is roughly a} \(73\%\) probability that the sample mean
falls between \(140\) and \(160\). Because the numbers are random, your
exact value will vary slightly each time the sheet recalculates.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans107}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{The expected value is} \(80\) (equal to the population mean). The
standard error is \(1.4\), and the probability is \(98.38\%\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=14/SQRT(100)} → \(1.4\) (standard error)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(85,\ 80,\ 1.4,\ TRUE)\ -\ NORM.DIST(77,\ 80,\ 1.4,\ TRUE)}
  → \(0.9838\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans108}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{The probabilities are} \(24.66\%\) and \(1.8\%\).

\emph{For the mean of four cars, use the standard error}
\(3.5/\sqrt{4}\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.DIST(35,\ 33.8,\ 3.5/SQRT(4),\ TRUE)} → \(0.2466\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{For all four cars, first find the probability that one car
exceeds} \(35\), then raise it to the fourth power (the draws are
independent):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=(1\ -\ NORM.DIST(35,\ 33.8,\ 3.5,\ TRUE))\^{}4} → \(0.018\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans109}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{The expected value is} \(0.75\) (the same as the population). The
standard error is \(\sqrt{p(1-p)/n} = 0.03\), and the probability is
\(0.8975\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SQRT(0.75*0.25/200)} → \(0.0306\)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.DIST(0.8,\ 0.75,\ SQRT(0.75*0.25/200),\ TRUE)\ -\ NORM.DIST(0.7,\ 0.75,\ SQRT(0.75*0.25/200),\ TRUE)}
  → \(0.8975\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans110}{Exercise 3, question 2}

\emph{The probability with a sample of} \(50\) is \(69.29\%\). With a
sample of \(200\) it rises to \(84.33\%\). As the sample size increases,
the standard error shrinks, so the sampling distribution of \(\hat{p}\)
becomes tighter around \(0.23\) and more of its area lies above
\(0.20\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.DIST(0.2,\ 0.23,\ SQRT(0.23*0.77/50),\ TRUE)} →
  \(0.6929\)
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.DIST(0.2,\ 0.23,\ SQRT(0.23*0.77/200),\ TRUE)} →
  \(0.8433\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans111}{Exercise 4, question 1}

\emph{The process appears to be out of control. In the early samples the
machine is not filling the cans with enough drink; it recovers in later
periods, but the early behavior is a warning sign.}

\emph{Build a control chart. The center line is} \(350\), and the limits
are three standard errors away, where the standard error is
\(10/\sqrt{10}\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=350\ +\ 3*(10/SQRT(10))} → \(359.49\) (upper limit)
\item
  \texttt{=350\ -\ 3*(10/SQRT(10))} → \(340.51\) (lower limit)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Plot the eight sample means together with the two limits: put the
sample numbers, the sample means, and two columns holding the constant
limits side by side, then insert a Line or Scatter chart. Several early
samples fall below the lower limit of} \(340.51\), confirming the
process is out of control.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans112}{Exercise 4, question 2}

\emph{The process looks in control. All five proportions fall within
three standard errors of the mean.}

\emph{The center line is} \(0.01\), and the limits use the standard
error \(\sqrt{0.01 \times 0.99 / 1000}\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=0.01\ +\ 3*SQRT(0.01*0.99/1000)} → \(0.0194\) (upper limit)
\item
  \texttt{=0.01\ -\ 3*SQRT(0.01*0.99/1000)} → \(0.0006\) (lower limit)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Plotting the five proportions against these limits shows every
point inside the band, so the process is under control.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{12}{Inference II}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans113}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{For an exponential distribution with rate} \(\lambda = 0.02\), the
mean and standard deviation are both \(1/\lambda = 50\). To draw an
exponential value in Excel, transform a uniform random number:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=-LN(RAND())/0.02}
\end{itemize}

\emph{Fill a block of} \(10{,}000\) rows and \(50\) columns with this
formula; each row is a sample of size \(50\). (The values recalculate on
every edit --- copy the block and use \texttt{Paste\ Special\ →\ Values}
to freeze them.)

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans114}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{In the column just after the data, average each row and copy the
formula down all} \(10{,}000\) rows:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(A2:AX2)} → the mean of the first sample
\end{itemize}

\emph{Summarizing the} \(10{,}000\) sample means:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(...)} → about \(50\), matching the population mean
\item
  \texttt{=STDEV.S(...)} → about \(7.07\), which matches the standard
  error \(\sigma/\sqrt{n} = 50/\sqrt{50} \approx 7.07\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans115}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{With the standard error stored in a cell (call it \texttt{SE}}
\(\approx 7.07\)), the limits are the sample mean plus or minus
\(z_{0.05} \times SE\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=FirstMean\ -\ NORM.S.INV(0.95)*SE} → lower limit
\item
  \texttt{=FirstMean\ +\ NORM.S.INV(0.95)*SE} → upper limit
\end{itemize}

\emph{Because a typical sample mean sits near} \(50\), the interval will
usually contain the population mean. About \(90\%\) of such intervals
do.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans116}{Exercise 1, question 4}

\emph{Rather than build} \(10{,}000\) pairs of limits, note that an
interval includes \(50\) exactly when the sample mean is within
\(z_{0.05} \times SE\) of \(50\). Add a helper column:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=IF(ABS(SampleMean\ -\ 50)\ \textless{}=\ NORM.S.INV(0.95)*SE,\ 1,\ 0)}
\end{itemize}

\emph{Copy it down and sum it with \texttt{=SUM(...)}. About}
\(9{,}000\) of the \(10{,}000\) intervals contain the population mean
--- that is exactly what a \(90\%\) confidence level means.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans117}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{The 90\% interval is} \([94.52,\ 114.68]\) and the 95\% interval
is \([92.44,\ 116.76]\). The higher the confidence level, the wider the
interval.

\emph{Since} \(\sigma\) is unknown, use the \(t\)-distribution.
\texttt{CONFIDENCE.T} returns the margin of error directly:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.T(0.10,\ 28.8,\ 24)} → \(10.08\), so the 90\%
  interval is \(104.6 \pm 10.08\)
\item
  \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.T(0.05,\ 28.8,\ 24)} → \(12.16\), so the 95\%
  interval is \(104.6 \pm 12.16\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans118}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{The interval for} \(n = 16\) is \([30.75,\ 66.61]\) and for
\(n = 25\) is \([34.79,\ 62.57]\). As the sample size grows, the
interval gets narrower and more precise.

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.T(0.05,\ 33.64,\ 16)} → \(17.93\), so the interval
  is \(48.68 \pm 17.93\)
\item
  \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.T(0.05,\ 33.64,\ 25)} → \(13.89\), so the interval
  is \(48.68 \pm 13.89\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans119}{Exercise 3}

\emph{The 95\% interval for Group 1 is} \([-0.53,\ 2.03]\) and for Group
2 is \([0.90,\ 3.76]\).

\emph{The data is sorted by group, so with \texttt{extra} in column A
the ten Group 1 values are in \texttt{A2:A11} and the ten Group 2 values
in \texttt{A12:A21}. For each group, find the mean and the margin of
error:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(A2:A11)} → \(0.75\) for Group 1, and
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(A12:A21)} → \(2.33\) for Group 2
\item
  \texttt{=CONFIDENCE.T(0.05,\ STDEV.S(A2:A11),\ 10)} → the margin of
  error, giving \texttt{AVERAGE\ ±\ margin}
\end{itemize}

\emph{(You can also get each group's mean with
\texttt{=AVERAGEIF(B:B,\ "1",\ A:A)}.) Drug 2 appears more effective.
Its interval lies entirely above zero, so it is unlikely to have no
effect, and its mean increase in sleep is \(2.33\) hours, well above
Drug 1's \(0.75\) hours.}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans120}{Exercise 4, question 1}

\emph{The 90\% interval is} \([0.319,\ 0.481]\) and the 95\% interval is
\([0.304,\ 0.496]\). Neither includes \(0.5\), so at both levels we can
conclude the population proportion differs from \(0.5\).

\emph{With} \(\hat{p} = 0.4\) and \(n = 100\), the standard error is
\texttt{=SQRT(0.4*0.6/100)} \(= 0.049\). The margins of error are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.INV(0.95)\ *\ SQRT(0.4*0.6/100)} → \(0.081\), giving
  \(0.4 \pm 0.081\)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.INV(0.975)\ *\ SQRT(0.4*0.6/100)} → \(0.096\), giving
  \(0.4 \pm 0.096\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans121}{Exercise 4, question 2}

\emph{The 95\% confidence interval is} \([0.128,\ 0.186]\).

\emph{The data is in tidy form with columns \texttt{Hair}, \texttt{Eye},
\texttt{Sex}, and \texttt{n} (the count). Total the Hazel rows and
divide by the overall total to get the sample proportion:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SUMIF(Eye\_range,\ "Hazel",\ n\_range)} → \(93\) Hazel-eyed
  students
\item
  \texttt{=SUM(n\_range)} → \(592\) students, so
  \(\hat{p} = 93/592 = 0.157\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Then the margin of error and interval are:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.INV(0.975)\ *\ SQRT(0.157*(1-0.157)/592)} → \(0.029\),
  giving \(0.157 \pm 0.029 = [0.128,\ 0.186]\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{13}{Inference III}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans122}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{The test statistic is} \(-1.6\). The two-tailed p-value is
\(13.04\%\), which is greater than \(5\%\), so we cannot reject the
null. The population mean is not statistically different from \(50\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=(46\ -\ 50)\ /\ (10\ /\ SQRT(16))} → \(-1.6\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST.2T(ABS(-1.6),\ 15)} → \(0.1304\) (two-tailed p-value)
\end{itemize}

\emph{The critical value \texttt{=T.INV.2T(0.05,\ 15)} is} \(2.131\).
Since \(|-1.6| < 2.131\), we fail to reject the null.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans123}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The null hypothesis cannot be rejected: the left-tailed p-value
of} \(1.9\%\) is greater than the \(1\%\) significance level.

\emph{Enter the data in a range (say \texttt{A2:A7}). In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(A2:A7)} → \(92\) and \texttt{=STDEV.S(A2:A7)} →
  \(6.96\)
\item
  \texttt{=(92\ -\ 100)\ /\ (6.96\ /\ SQRT(6))} → \(-2.82\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST(-2.82,\ 5,\ TRUE)} → \(0.019\) (left-tailed p-value)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans124}{Exercise 1, question 3}

\emph{The null hypothesis can be rejected: the right-tailed p-value of}
\(0.2\%\) is less than the \(10\%\) significance level.

\emph{Enter the data in a range (say \texttt{A2:A11}). In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(A2:A11)} → \(256.4\) and \texttt{=STDEV.S(A2:A11)} →
  \(38.28\)
\item
  \texttt{=(256.4\ -\ 210)\ /\ (38.28\ /\ SQRT(10))} → \(3.83\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST.RT(3.83,\ 9)} → \(0.002\) (right-tailed p-value)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans125}{Exercise 2}

\emph{The claim that the average waiting time is} \(92\) minutes can be
rejected at the \(10\%\), \(5\%\), and \(1\%\) significance levels.

\emph{With the \texttt{waiting} column in column B (272 observations),
in Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=AVERAGE(B2:B273)} → \(70.9\) and \texttt{=STDEV.S(B2:B273)} →
  \(13.6\)
\item
  \texttt{=(70.9\ -\ 92)\ /\ (13.6\ /\ SQRT(272))} → \(-25.6\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST.2T(ABS(-25.6),\ 271)} → approximately \(0\)
  (two-tailed p-value)
\end{itemize}

\emph{The sample mean is so far below} \(92\) that the p-value is
essentially zero, so we reject the null at any conventional level.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans126}{Exercise 3, question 1}

\emph{The hypotheses are} \(H_{0}: p = 0.4\) and \(H_{a}: p \neq 0.4\).
The two-tailed p-value is \(0.102\), greater than \(0.05\), so we cannot
reject the null. The population proportion is not significantly
different from \(0.4\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=(0.48\ -\ 0.4)\ /\ SQRT(0.4*(1-0.4)/100)} → \(1.633\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=2*(1\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(1.633,\ TRUE))} → \(0.102\) (two-tailed
  p-value)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans127}{Exercise 3, question 2}

\emph{The sample proportion is} \(128/320 = 0.4\). The left-tailed
p-value of \(3.6\%\) is less than \(5\%\), so we reject the null and
conclude the population proportion is less than \(0.45\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=(0.4\ -\ 0.45)\ /\ SQRT(0.45*(1-0.45)/320)} → \(-1.80\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=NORM.S.DIST(-1.80,\ TRUE)} → \(0.036\) (left-tailed p-value)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans128}{Exercise 3, question 3}

\emph{The hypotheses are} \(H_{0}: p \leq 0.5\) and \(H_{a}: p > 0.5\).
The right-tailed p-value of \(0.26\) is greater than the \(1\%\)
significance level, so we cannot reject the null. We therefore
\textbf{cannot} conclude that more than \(50\%\) of the population is
below \(10\).

\emph{Enter the data in a range (say \texttt{A2:A11}). In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=COUNTIF(A2:A11,\ "\textless{}10")\ /\ COUNT(A2:A11)} →
  \(0.6\) (sample proportion)
\item
  \texttt{=(0.6\ -\ 0.5)\ /\ SQRT(0.5*(1-0.5)/10)} → \(0.632\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=1\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(0.632,\ TRUE)} → \(0.264\) (right-tailed
  p-value)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans129}{Exercise 4}

\emph{We reject the null hypothesis: the proportion of hazel-eyed
students is significantly different from} \(5\%\).

\emph{The data is tidy, with columns \texttt{Hair}, \texttt{Eye},
\texttt{Sex}, and \texttt{n}. In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=SUMIF(Eye\_range,\ "Hazel",\ n\_range)} → \(93\) and
  \texttt{=SUM(n\_range)} → \(592\), so \(\hat{p} = 93/592 = 0.157\)
\item
  \texttt{=(0.157\ -\ 0.05)\ /\ SQRT(0.05*(1-0.05)/592)} → \(11.96\)
  (test statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=2*(1\ -\ NORM.S.DIST(11.96,\ TRUE))} → approximately \(0\)
  (two-tailed p-value)
\end{itemize}

\emph{The sample proportion of} \(15.7\%\) is nearly \(12\) standard
errors above the claimed \(5\%\), so we reject the null decisively.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\bsanswerchapter{14}{Regression and Inference}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans130}{Exercise 1, question 1}

\emph{We cannot reject the null: the p-value of} \(0.47\) is greater
than \(0.05\).

\emph{In Excel:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=(0.15*SQRT(25-2))\ /\ SQRT(1\ -\ 0.15\^{}2)} → \(0.73\) (test
  statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST.2T(0.73,\ 23)} → \(0.47\) (two-tailed p-value)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans131}{Exercise 1, question 2}

\emph{The correlation is} \(0.44\) and the test statistic is \(7.89\).
The p-value is approximately \(0\), so we reject the null hypothesis ---
the correlation between Hits and Salary is highly significant.

\emph{In Excel (there are} \(263\) players with a recorded salary):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=CORREL(Hits\_range,\ Salary\_range)} → \(0.44\)
\item
  \texttt{=(0.44*SQRT(263-2))\ /\ SQRT(1\ -\ 0.44\^{}2)} → \(7.89\)
  (test statistic)
\item
  \texttt{=T.DIST.2T(7.89,\ 261)} → approximately \(0\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans132}{Exercise 2, question 1}

\emph{There is no strong reason to believe the variances differ ---
players are recruited from a common pool. At the} \(5\%\) level the
difference in mean Hits is not significant, so we cannot reject the
null.

\emph{Copy the Hits for \texttt{NewLeague\ =\ "A"} into one column and
the Hits for \texttt{NewLeague\ =\ "N"} into another (or filter the
data). This is an unpaired, two-tailed test assuming equal variances:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=T.TEST(A\_range,\ N\_range,\ 2,\ 2)} → \(0.28\) (p-value)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Since} \(0.28 > 0.05\), we fail to reject the null.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans133}{Exercise 2, question 2}

\emph{There may be reason to believe the variances differ, so we use an
unequal-variance test. At the} \(5\%\) level we cannot reject
\(H_{0}: \mu_{male} - \mu_{female} \leq 0\) --- there is no evidence
that males survive longer.

\emph{Run
\texttt{Data\ →\ Data\ Analysis\ →\ t-Test:\ Two-Sample\ Assuming\ Unequal\ Variances}
on the male and female survival times. The male mean (}\(26.78\)) is
actually \textbf{below} the female mean (\(28.10\)), giving a test
statistic of \(-0.31\). Because the difference points opposite to the
alternative, the one-tailed p-value is about \(0.62\) --- far above
\(0.05\) --- so we fail to reject the null.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans134}{Exercise 3}

\emph{The drug has an effect: we reject} \(H_{0}: \bar{d} = 0\). The
mean difference in extra sleep is statistically different from zero.

\emph{Because the same patients are measured twice, use a paired,
two-tailed test. With the Group 1 values in \texttt{A2:A11} and the
Group 2 values in \texttt{A12:A21}:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=T.TEST(A2:A11,\ A12:A21,\ 2,\ 1)} → \(0.0028\) (p-value)
\end{itemize}

\emph{Since} \(0.0028 < 0.01\), we reject the null at the \(1\%\) level.

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans135}{Exercise 4, question 1}

\emph{RBI and Years are statistically significant (p-values near}
\(0\)): salary rises with experience and with RBIs. Home runs are
\textbf{not} significant (p-value about \(0.14\)). The \(F\)-statistic
is highly significant, so the predictors are jointly significant.
However, the \(R^2\) and adjusted \(R^2\) are only about \(0.32\), so
the model explains just \(32\%\) of the variation in salary --- more
variables would be needed for a better fit.

\emph{Copy \texttt{HmRun}, \texttt{RBI}, and \texttt{Years} into three
adjacent columns (the ToolPak needs a contiguous X range), then run
\texttt{Data\ →\ Data\ Analysis\ →\ Regression} with \texttt{Salary} as
the Input Y Range and those three columns as the Input X Range. The
output gives:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  each coefficient's \textbf{p-value} (the individual tests): RBI and
  Years significant, HmRun not
\item
  the \textbf{Significance F} (the joint test): approximately \(0\)
\item
  \textbf{R Square} and \textbf{Adjusted R Square}: about \(0.33\) and
  \(0.32\)
\end{itemize}

\end{bsanswerkey}

\begin{bsanswerkey}{bsans136}{Exercise 4, question 2}

\emph{The predicted salary is} \(619.93\) (thousand dollars), with a
\(95\%\) prediction interval of \([-129.89,\ 1369.7]\).

\emph{Using the coefficients from the regression output
(}\(\hat{\alpha}=-90.09\), \(b_{HmRun}=-7.35\), \(b_{RBI}=9.16\),
\(b_{Years}=32.82\)), the point prediction is:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{=-90.09\ +\ (-7.35)*28\ +\ 9.16*57\ +\ 32.82*12} → \(619.93\)
\end{itemize}

\emph{The prediction interval widens the estimate by} \(t \times s_e\),
where \(t =\) \texttt{T.INV.2T(0.05,\ 259)} \(\approx 1.97\) and \(s_e\)
is the regression's standard error (\(\approx 381\) for a new
observation). This gives \(619.93 \pm 749.8 = [-129.89,\ 1369.7]\). The
interval is very wide --- and even includes negative salaries ---
because the model explains only about a third of the variation in
salary.

\end{bsanswerkey}
